--- MANUAL FOR LADD CRYPTOLECT DECIPHERMENT ---

[1. INTRODUCTION AND ORIGIN]
The texts within this Codex belong to the "Ladd Cryptolect" family, generated by the Forgotten Languages Organization using NodeSpaces V2.0 software. This is not a standard constructed language (conlang) but an "engineered language" derived from natural language evolution simulations, heavily influenced by English syntax but subjected to chaotic morphological drift, symbolic masking, and phonetic shifting.

[2. SYNTAX AND TOPOLOGY]
    Sentence Topology: Generally follows English Subject-Verb-Object (SVO).
    Stability: Function words (pronouns, prepositions, conjunctions) are relatively stable.
    Volatility: Nouns and verbs sometimes are more volatile and context-dependent.

(strange terms/artifacts)
    szir = (szir is very strange case. It seems that it do not correspond to anything in english, and is actually an artifact of the simulated evolution of the ladd cryptolect language. This hypothesis is derived from text 80, where the original text had an english citation and its translation to ladd, with nothing in the english citation corresponding to szir. Maybe it means something, but by now, I don't know. When translating these, just put them inside braquets in their corresponding place.)
    reven = (Apparently means heaven (biggest proof in text 82, wich talks about the translation of the word TITOGAN from the Guanche language, wich translate to sky/heaven in english), but appears one time with a prefix (ne), and when in front of szir seems to translate to nothing.)

[5. TRANSLATION STRATEGY]
If a word is unknown:
    1. Search the codex and make sure it has no translation yet.
    2. Set the syllables apart and try translating them (if they are not in the manual, observe and analyze through the rest of the codex, because not every consonant is listed here in the manual).
    3. Use context: "s'slolér" implies a noun; "idda" implies a verb following. What is the topic of the text?

--- START OF TEXT 1 (The teacher's grammar book (2) /// S'dèaksler's slàemler gruk (2)) ---

S'dèaksler's slàemler gruk slàéw out ladd my exlòèenslí dèaksling slàemler id jìmmásódion léands jìursòs dí ewígwèdion sdìwénts sónslí 1981. Yt i wèsjìddeéd earat raedd my gwèéer idda yt lárslà lomgwírs ladd lòdeslòkdidde dèakslers an lùt fel jìnwúwént agwet wéir klùwlàdslà ladd engbas slàemler. Wéy exlòèenslí aeon slírdéin wéslàée ladd anxiety issa aeon édelt. List raen éjìglúzed yt wéy sid gwí éuiéd dí dèaksl slàemler—and wéy aén't slappy agwet it. leny raen slad gand exlòèenslís sem slàemler raedd s' lóst. Wéy "wèdn't slàt it," or, sùdwér sdill, wéy "lást wèdn't baslí it." Luarat savo ren derlòèsòd yn, aeon fw gadks indí my slàemler jìursòs, wéy wèsjìdder lùt onat yt wéy ren "slàtding it" gwét yt slàemler idda akdìalat slan. s' dèaksler's slàemler gruk aims dí déslí éawérs fudd aeon sólèlár tusslislà ladd wèsjìddery.
:::
The teacher's grammar book grew out of my experience teaching grammar and composition methods courses to education students since 1981. What I discovered early in my career is that large numbers of prospective teachers do not feel confident about their knowledge of english grammar. They experience a certain degree of anxiety as a result. Most have recognized that they will be required to teach grammar—and they aren't happy about it, many have had bad experiences with grammar in the past. They "didn't get it," or, sadder still, they "just didn't like it." Nearly all are surprised when, a few weeks into my grammar courses, they discover not only that they are "getting it" but that grammar is actually fun. the teacher's grammar book aims to take readers on a similar voyage of discovery.

Yt's luw raedd s' sòjìnd ewèdion s' leny dèakslers id sdìwénts n usòd s' wúrst ewèdion ladd s' dèaksler's slàemler gruk lòdejewéd daèous degslàsdions odder s' gadars indènwéd dí leslí s' dèxt gwítdèr. I'dde dèèed dí injìrmáedè wéir degslàsdions indí s' sòjìnd ewèdion issa líksl issa mássóblà, id i slolò wéy ren slappy sem s' édelt ladd wès inwèékt jìllágyedion. raedd leny éslòkts, s' wúlúsòd lòdewíkt idda sóglúwúgwèntat wèffént fdem s' oèslelìl. s' lié immárdént kslanslàs aé...
:::
What's new in the second edition the many teachers and students who used the first edition of The teacher's grammar book provided various suggestions over the years intended to make the text better. I've tried to incorporate their suggestions into the second edition as much as possible, and I hope they are happy with the result of this indirect collaboration. in many respects, the finished product is significantly different from the original. the more important changes are...

--- END OF TEXT 1 ---
--- START OF TEXT 2 (Secret Slavonic Dialect /// Sòslíét Slátulúk Wèalàkt) ---

Wésò skslools jìmwùrdingat endeéd yt dewdy máor kslilwéén éslíidded làssòns sdèelòd raedd liebaty id jíjek jerdìe. Wéy lòdejewéd s' adwéd gwíluwút ladd slíemang wésò ruungsdèrs gwésy savo yy eddery denyy—wé onat yy yt list yé lùt raz nrk. wès aplòdeaksl jìuld lùt sòrdde odder s' záng dèrm, sloydder, id mábadijíans gwíslán expzáèng opdions. alanugsl lessùkslusòtts slad wéslíéed raedd 1647 yt vareso sòttlàlént ladd 50 wòlèbaes líst raen aeon slàemler skslool id savo sdédès slad enjìueslàd ulúddersùl ewígwèdion, lessùkslusòtts wèd lùt implàlént jìmmàlsòry ewígwèdion láws undeal 1852. list ladd s' owér sdédès sòon wùlzáyd deit, id ifeshi s' end ladd s' 19d slíndìry, aléègwè essòndialat slad lìdionanwé jìmmàlsòry ewígwèdion. less ewígwèdion làd dí aeon éjìnslípdìababadion ladd slow slàemler ys déugslt. elàléndéry skslools édéilud s' wúrst slankdion, banjíng slàemler id éawèng dí lòdejewé sdìwénts akslíss dí immárdént liel làssòns.
:::
These schools comfortingly ensured that rowdy poor children received lessons steeped in morality and civic virtue. They provided the added benefit of keeping these youngsters busy all day every Sunday—the only day that most were not at work. this approach could not serve over the long term, however, and politicians began exploring options. although massachusetts had decreed in 1647 that any settlement of 50 families must have a grammar school and all states had encouraged universal education, massachusetts did not implement compulsory education laws until 1852. most of the other states soon followed suit, and by the end of the 19th century, America essentially had nationwide compulsory education. mass education led to a reconceptualization of how grammar was taught. elementary schools retained the first function, linking grammar and reading to provide students access to important moral lessons.

--- END OF TEXT 2 ---
--- START OF TEXT 3 (Young Students /// Ruung Sdìwénts) ---

Ruung sdìwénts yé déugslt ifeshi aeon slàemledisdès, n lòdejewéd insdèsúkdion raedd s' alwògwít (slàemledé), éawèng, yèding, id slàemler. Aeon slàemledisdès alsò sládde insdèsúkdion raedd owér debslikts, deksl issa lísók id lewélediks. Yn sdìwénts yé lòdewújíent éawérs id yèdèrs, wéy yé wéeléd slàemledijìs, vaj badèedè.
:::
Young students were taught by a grammatistes, who provided instruction in the alphabet (grammata), reading, writing, and grammar. A grammatistes also gave instruction in other subjects, such as music and mathematics. When students were proficient readers and writers, they were deemed grammatikos, or literate.

Raz wès máint, wéy gwíslán sdìdarng badèedìé raedd earlust. Pládí ydedè raedd lòdedésloes yt "yn s' gyy raenid làarlud slis làtdèrs id idda gwíslenlúng dí unwérsdénd yt idda yètdèn, issa gwíwùé sle unwérsdíod onat yt ys smáslín, wéy (wé dèakslers) màt indí slis slands s' nrks ladd s' slàéat máets, anksl sle éads sótding fudd aeon gwínksl raz skslool" (ll. 325–326).
:::
At this point, they began studying literature in earnest. Plato wrote in protagoras that "when the boy has learned his letters and is beginning to understand what is written, as before he understood only what was spoken, they (the teachers) put into his hands the works of the great poets, which he reads sitting on a bench at school" (11. 325-326).

--- END OF TEXT 3 ---
--- START OF TEXT 4 (The Teacher's Grammar Book /// S' Dèaksler's Slàemler Gruk) ---

Lòéwòslí
s' dèaksler's slàemler gruk idda wésóglud sha sdìwénts n ren lòélóèng  dí gwí-jìlé engbas or lánslóaslà arts dèakslers, issa yll issa sha slíéwéndialàd dèakslers n ynt dí klùw lié agwet slàemler. list slàemler gruks wùys fudd dèrlèlùzágy. sòlé add aeon wèsyssóon ladd s' jìnlukdion gwítgadn slàemler id yèding. s' dèaksler's slàemler gruk, sloydder, idda wèffént. slírdéinat, it dèéats dèrlèlùzágy andeugslat, gwét it idda wòr lié yn lást aeon bast ladd slàemler dèrms. it idda lùt aeon slandgruk id ys ludder indènwéd dí gwí olu. 
:::
Preface
the teacher's grammar book is designed for students who are preparing to be-come english or language arts teachers, as well as for credentialed teachers who want to know more about grammar. most grammar books focus on terminology. some add a discussion of the connection between grammar and writing. the teacher's grammar book, however, is different. certainly, it treats terminology thoroughly, but it is not a handbook and was never intended to be one.

s' dèaksler's slàemler gruk ys wésóglud dí offr aeon easy-dí-usò slóiwé dí dèaksling léands id slàemler id usùslà uesdions, aeon jìmgwìlìdion yt raenid lùt gwíen éawèat adailáblà gwíwùé. raedd adwèdion, it lòdejewés aeon odderjeew ladd engbas slàemler yt idda inwùrléd lùt onat ifeshi slisdíègwèl wéddezáplénts raedd s' wúeld gwét alsò ifeshi aeon daèety ladd lòysloslegwèl, ésòarksl, id wúzásòwúgwèl isdees yt unwérbae slàemler id raedd am efwùrts dí unwérsdénd slàemler, lánslóaslà, yèding, id dèaksling. out ladd wès anwé-ensleng expzáedion elérslàs s' jeew yt aeon dèaksler's ksloislí laddslàemler éflàkts wúzásòwúgwèl id lòysloslegwèl oèendédions yt infzàenslí gyd s' jìndènt id s' léands ladd lánslóaslà arts insdèsúkdion.
:::
the teacher's grammar book was designed to offer an easy-to-use guide to teaching methods and grammar and usage questions, a combination that has not been readily available before. in addition, it provides an overview of english grammar that is informed not only by historical developments in the field but also by a variety of pedagogical, research, and philosophical issues that underlie grammar and our efforts to understand grammar, language, writing, and teaching. out of this wide-ranging exploration emerges the view that a teacher's choice of grammar reflects philosophical and pedagogical orientations that influence both the content and the methods of language arts instruction.

--- END OF TEXT 4 ---
--- START OF TEXT 5 (A Central Part /// Aeon Slíndèel Lórt) ---

S' sdìdy ladd slolér ys aeon slíndèel lórt ladd elàléndéry ewígwèdion raedd slàéeslí gwí-gwèusò slis máems jìndéin liel léssùslàs yt yé wéeléd jedél sha kslilwéén. Raedd adwèdion, s' máems élòésòndèd s' iwéal wùrm ladd lánslóaslà yt sdìwénts yé exlòkdèd dí lèlèk sò issa dí lòésòrdde s' "màèty" ladd sloléèk slàéek. Wís, slàéek ewígwèdion wéddezálòd aeon lòésslíèpdidde sdénslí sem éslòkt dí lánslóaslà id slàemler, wéwúlúng lùdions ladd "jìrékt" id "injìrékt" lánslóaslà usò raedd dèrms ladd adsleénslí dí badèery lùrms yt kslaekdèèzed slàéek slunwééds ladd gadars raedd s' lóst. Dí gwítdèr unwérsdénd s' ewígwèdiolìl wèfwúyldies assòjíadèd sem wès aplòdeaksl, y lègslt jìnsówér yt raedd am lánslóaslà arts klássòs siud gwí baslí díyy ked y usòd s' lánslóaslà ladd sùslíslòaé issa aeon liwél sha jìrékt engbas.
:::
The study of homer was a central part of elementary education in Greece because his poems contain moral messages that were deemed vital for children. in addition, the poems represented the ideal form of language that students were expected to mimic so as to preserve the "purity" of homeric greek. Thus, greek education developed a prescriptive stance with respect to language and grammar, defining notions of "correct" and "incorrect" language use in terms of adherence to literary norms that characterized greek hundreds of years in the past. To better understand the educational difficulties associated with this approach, we might consider that our language arts classes would be like today if we used the language of shakespeare as a model for correct english.

Slàéeks ladd 6d slíndìry awéns objeousat kluw yt wéir lánslóaslà ys wèffént fdem yt slolér usòd. S' lánslóaslà slad kslanslàd, issa savo bajeng lánslóaslàs an. Wès dèdeublàd s' slàéeks slàéatat, gwígwèusò wéy jeeyd s' sloléèk lòèod issa aeon slolwén aslà. Kslanslà luslíssùèat léant wékbalu.
:::
Greeks of 6th century athens obviously knew that their language was different from that what homer used. The language had changed, as all living languages do. This troubled the greeks greatly, because they viewed the homeric period as a golden age. Change necessarily meant decline.

Id alanugsl it ley sòem idelúk dí nizze gwígwèusò y slolùr s' slàéat jìndèègwédions dí jíjebabadion yt slàéeslí lewé fdem agwet 600 dí 300 bk, s' slàéeks ladd s' lòèod ofdèn sùw wémsòlddes issa bajeng raedd s' yrk aslàs afdèr aeon wòll fdem s' slolwén aslà ladd wéir làslànyry sledees.
:::
And although it may seem ironic to us because we honor the great contributions to civilization that greece made from about 600 to 300 BC, the greeks of the period often saw themselves as living in the dark ages after a fall from the golden age of their legendary heroes.

--- END OF TEXT 5 ---
--- START OF TEXT 6 (All Classes /// Savo Klássòs) ---

Ezáydion id rsledíèk raedd ládèr slàéek ewígwèdion" (p. 4). raedd earbaer dilés, ewígwèdion ys raedd s'slands ladd lóénts, sem liwérs ewígwèding wéir wegsldèrs id wòwérs ewígwèding wéir sòns. Gwét y sòe raedd s' ibaad yt ifeshi slolér's dilé (judj mássóbat líksl earbaer), sslildy wòlèbaes jìmlinat empzágadd lòdefssóolìl dìdírs.
:::
Elocution and rhetoric in later Greek education" (p. 4). in earlier times, education was in the hands of parents, with mothers educating their daughters and fathers educating their sons. But we see in The iliad that by homer's time (and possibly much earlier), wealthy families commonly employed professional tutors.

Ifeshi s'end ladd s' 6d slíndìry, ewígwèdion slad gwíjìlé sysdèledized id lié or làss ulúddersùl sha gyys, n gwíslán atdènwèng lòèdadè skslools raz s' aslà ladd 6 id jìndiloed raz làast undil s' aslà ladd 14. S' sloddernlént wèd lùt éuié atdènynslí, gwét ewígwèdion ys sligslat dazàed aling savo klássòs, id it sòems yt edden máor lóénts sòléslow wùund s' léans dí lòdejewé dìidion:
:::
By the end of the 6th century, education had become systematized and more or less universal for boys, who began attending private schools at the age of 6 and continued at least until the age of 14. The government did not require attendance, but education was highly valued among all classes, and it seems that even poor parents somehow found the means to provide tuition:

--- END OF TEXT 6 ---
--- START OF TEXT 7 (Church Slavonic /// Keslurksl Slátulúk) ---

Wès wésslíèpdion ladd s'sdèsúkdìé ladd Old Church Slavonic idda indènwéd dí lòésònt slalat s'immárdént ydé agwet s'lánslóaslà, ananut jíding savo s'minutiae ladd atdèsdèd daèant slòlbangs. S'wòkts raen gwíen dèéadèd fdem s'máint ladd jeew ladd sdèsúkdìel banslóisdiks, gwét lòysloslegwèl kláèty raenid déslín lòéslíwénslí odder s'jìnjísòluss éuiéd sha elàslánt wùrlel wésslíèpdion.
:::
This description of the structure of Old Church Slavonic is intended to present fully the important data about the language, without citing all the minutiae of attested variant spellings. The facts have been treated from the point of view of structural linguistics, but pedagogical clarity has taken precedence over the conciseness required for elegant formal description.

Old keslurksl slátulúk ys usòd odder aeon lòèod ladd sòlé tn slunwééd gadars id raedd daèous slàoslàewúgwèl lórts ladd s'slájek nrld lòéjísòat raz s'dilé yn s'slájek lánslóaslàs yé unwérsloing emad, slanyléndél, wèdderslànt kslanslàs . Sòlé ladd wésò kslanslàs ren anubtlàss éflàkdèd raedd s'daèant slòlbangs raedd s'fw dèksts anksl raen derjedded fdem wès lòèod, sò yt anlà list daèadions raedd slàemler id tugwègwéláry ren s'sòrts ladd stybasdik id iwèosynslíedik wèffénslís yt ren wùund raedd s'sdényrd vaj badèery lánslóaslà ladd merg sónglà emáksl, sòlé immárdént daèant wédéils édelt fdem wèffént ésleolìl wèalàkdél slisdíry.
:::
Old church slavonic was used over a period of some two hundred years and in various geographical parts of the slavic world, precisely at the time when the slavic languages were undergoing rapid, fundamental, divergent changes. Some of these changes are doubtless reflected in the variant spellings in the few texts which have survived from this period, so that while most variations in grammar and vocabulary are the sorts of stylistic and idiosyncratic differences that are found in the standard or literary language of any single epoch, some important variant details result from different regional dialectal history.

Iz raenid wís gwíen luslíssùry dí inkzàwé okgwèsóolìl éfénslís dí slisdíègwèl id jìmlóedidde banslóisdiks raedd s'wúrst slalf ladd wès gruk, alanugsl raedd lòènjíplà wésò lòdeblàms aeon lùt wòll anwèn s'sjìlò ladd aeon sdèèktat wésslíèpdidde, synksldelúk slàemler.
:::
It has thus been necessary to include occasional references to historical and comparative linguistics in the first half of this book, although in principle these problems do not fall within the scope of a strictly descriptive, synchronic grammar.

--- END OF TEXT 7 ---
--- START OF TEXT 8 (Greek Beginnings /// Slàéek Gwíslenlúngs) ---

Baslí sò leny owér elàlénts ladd ysdèrn yldìé, s' wùrlel sdìdy ladd slàemler gwíslán raedd anjíent slàéeslí, lòdeganbat raedd s' ládè 6d slíndìry bk, yn aeon lomgwír ladd wòkdírs jìmgwìlud dí lididadè s' slàéeks dí exalèlu s' sdèsúkdìé ladd wéir lánslóaslà. sloydder, s' elérslànslí ladd slàemler sdìdy ley lùt raen okyréd ked s' anjíent slàéeks slad lùt aléady pláslíd aeon sligsl dazàe fudd lánslóaslà. slolér's ibaad id odyssòy, màt indí yètdèn wùrm gwítgadn 900 id 800 bk, lòdejewé sòlé insógslt indí s' lìdìé ladd slàéek ewígwèdion gwíwùé s' 6d slíndìry. raedd s' ibaad, y wúnd yt s' slede akslillàs ys dìdíéd issa aeon ruud dí gwí "a slòaslír ladd nrds id aeon aner ladd wéeds" (9.454–455), id s' nrk inkzàwés lolédeus slòeksles yt ilzàsdèedè s' immárdénslí ladd slòajíng yll. issa gadzákk (1974) lùdèd, "savo wès wùésùanws s' jìnsmaruus pláslí ladd …"
:::
Like so many other elements of western culture, the formal study of grammar began in ancient greece, probably in the late 6th century bc, when a number of factors combined to motivate the greeks to examine the structure of their language. however, the emergence of grammar study may not have occurred if the ancient greeks had not already placed a high value on language. homer's iliad and odyssey, put into written form between 900 and 800 bc, provide some insight into the nature of greek education before the 6th century. in the iliad, we find that the hero achilles was tutored as a youth to be "a speaker of words and a dfoer of deeds" (9.454-455), and the work includes numerous speeches that illustrate the importance of speaking well. as wheelock (1974) noted, "all this foreshadows the conspicuous place of ..."

--- END OF TEXT 8 ---
--- START OF TEXT 9 (Rice cooked with pulses or milk /// Èslí jìoslíd sem màlsòs vaj lèlk) ---

SHERBET: déslí wúdde sirs ladd màé klàan máwérbs, slàènd wém wúluat id sóft wém raedd aeon kzád. Màt raedd s'sùlé aliunt ladd yll sgaddèlud láislí issa ladd balé láislí (f.178b) sò yt iz gwíjìlés yll-flátuuéd id adad lòplòr yt idda yll-flátuuéd. Lèx wém savo díslàwér id sdèein wém ddeugsl aeon sdèeilúng kzád . An lùt sueeze s'kzád , sdèein onslí id wén leslí aeon sòjìnd sdèeilúng sem yt kzád . Iz sòuld gwí yll-sdèeilud. Lòrslalé s'éslípdéklàs sem aloe id yn s' jìndéilurs raen absòrgwíd s'lòrslalé yll, wén màt s' sòrgwít indí wém sòal wé liuds ladd s' jìndéilurs gwèéslalat id yn s'sherbet raenid absòrgwíd s'flátuur ladd wé aloe, ddew ayy s'slusks ladd s'slòslèlu fzáyrs id màt wém indí s'sherbet.
:::
SHERBET: take five sirs of pure clean potherbs, grind them finely and sift them in a cloth. Put in the same amount of well sweetened juice as of lime juice (f.178b) so that it becomes well-flavored and add pepper that is well-flavored. Mix them all together and strain them through a straining cloth. Do not squeeze the cloth, strain once and then make a second straining with that cloth. It should be well-strained. Perfume the receptacles with aloe and when the containers have absorbed the perfume well, then put the sherbet into them seal the mouths of the containers carefully and when the sherbet has absorbed the flavor of the aloe, throw away the husks of the jasmine flowers and put them into the sherbet.

Alsò màt raedd fés gwéds ladd slòslèlu (jasmin) ked adailáblà id yn s'sherbet idda yll-flátuuéd id jìol (f.179a) adad gwèmwùr, lísk id desòydèr id wéènk iz. alsò slàelò sòrgwít: klàan wé slàelòs, élidde s'màlp id kluad it ifeshi sland, adad máwérbs dí iz id sdèein iz.
:::
Also put in fresh buds of jasmine (jasmin) if available and when the sherbet is well-flavored and cool (f.179a) add camphor, musk and rosewater and drink it. also grape sherbet: clean the grapes, remove the pulp and knead it by hand, add potherbs to it and strain it.

O’Reilat 2004). O'Reilly 2004). Consequently, there is now a significant population of immigrant European children (the largest proportion of whom are British) living alongside Spanish children, and being educated in Spain:

To influence speech, instruction would have to focus on speech. Grammar instruction doesn't it.

Grammar and logical thinking. A similar situation exists with regard to the second assumption. Some people believe that certain logical mental operations are innate. --- START OF TEXT 14 (A Practical English Grammar /// Aeon Perakdikal Engzas Gemler) ---

Perewòke dí s' wùurd ewèdion
Aeon Perakdikal Engzas Gemler idda indènwéd sa indèrléwèadè id mást-indèrléwèadè sdìwénts. Y slolò dat lié addanked làarlurs id dèakslers sid alsò wúnd iz uàal.
:::
Preface to the fourth edition
A Practical English Grammar is intended for intermediate and post-intermediate students. We hope more advanced learners and teachers will also find it useful.

S'weok idda aeon kompereslensódde órddey ladd sterukdìés id wùrms, weritdèn raedd klàar liwém Engzas id ilzàsteradèd and lolésuus eksamplàs. Aéas ladd lórdikulár wèfwúkulty raen wíen gidden slòkial atdèndion.
:::
The book is a comprehensive survey of structures and forms written in clear modern English and illustrated with numerous examples. Areas of particular difficulty have been given special attention.

Wèfwuénkes wítyen kondderádiolìl uáge id sterikt gemledikal wùrms ren sown wét s'emfasós idda fudd kondderádiolìl wùrms.
:::
Differences between conversational usage and strict grammatical forms are shown but the emphasis is on conversational forms.

Raedd s' wùurd ewèdion s' lein kslanges ren issa wùlzáws:
:::
In the fourth edition the main changes are as follows:

Eksplálìdions id eksamplàs raen wíen berougslt sud dí wedè.
:::
Explanations and examples have been brought up to date.

Deé idda lùw lié inwùrledion fudd koundéblà id unkoundéblà lùuns, atteriwédidde id perewèkadidde adikdiddes, addderbs ladd pláke, àndènke addderbs, klàft àndènkes, peremásódions, konlankdions, liwel dderbs, lòrwukt dènàs, inwúlúdidde konsterukdions, s' lóssódde màrmáà kláuàs id lùun kláuàs.
:::
There is now more information on countable and uncountable nouns, attributive and predicative adjectives, adverbs of place, sentence adverbs, cleft sentences, prepositions, conjunctions, modal verbs, perfect tenses, infinitive constructions, the past purpose clauses and noun clauses.

Sòlé ledèèal raenid wíen éarenged dí leji komlóèsòns easóer. Sa eksamplà, lórts ladd kslapdèrs fudd kan, ley, líst etk. ren lùw gsuulòd ifesi ankdion; dderbs ladd zajíng id perewuénke raen aeon kslapdèr dí demàlddes; óggesdions id injedédions raen ìilud s' kslapdèr fudd komlends, éuests id adjeke.
:::
Some material has been rearranged to make comparisons easier. For example, parts of chapters on can, may, must etc. are now grouped by function, verbs of liking and preference have a chapter to themselves; suggestions and invitations have joined the chapter on commands, requests and advice.

De kondènts zast luw ómleèàs eddery àkdion sleawèng, id deé idda aeon luw inwéks kondéilúng leny lié enteries id éwuénkes.
:::
The contents list new summarises every section heading, and there is a new index containing many more entries and references.

Raedd dis ewèdion s' sógn '?' idda fereuentat uàd dí wélùdè aeon kslange ladd slòajir raedd eksamplàs ladd wèazágue.
:::
In the edition the sign '?' is frequently used to denote a change of speaker in examples of dialogue.

--- START OF TEXT 16 (On Bulgarian Dialects) ---

Liwérn gwélsláèan wèalàkdízágy idda sleir dí aeon èksl skfrolárat dèewèdion. Raedd iz wùunwér idda slàluelat jìnsówééd dí gwí s'sússóan slájest Viktor Grigorovitch, n raedd 1848 màbbasòd “A Sketch of Travels in European Turkey”.
:::
Modern bulgarian dialectology is heir to a rich scholarly tradition. Its founder is generally considered to be the russian slavist Viktor Grigorovitch, who in 1848 published "A Sketch of Travels in European Turkey".

Fudd s'gansós ladd slis aedda obsòrdadions fdem slis slìurluys raedd gwélfráèan lánds, sle lùdèd aeon lomgwír ladd wèalàkdízáslegwèl kslaekdèèsdiks, id ys s'wúrst dí atdèmpt aeon skslolárat klássówúgwèdion ladd gwélsláèan wèalàkts indí tn slàdeups –easdèrn id ysdèrn– id dí wéwúlu s'banslóisdik wédéils id s'slàoslàewúgwèl wèsdèègwédion ladd wésò wèalàkt slàdeups.
:::
On the basis of his own observations from his journeys in bulgarian lands, he noted a number of dialectological characteristics, and was the first to attempt a scholarly classification of bulgarian dialects into two groups –eastern and western– and to define the linguistic details and the geographical distribution of these dialect groups.

--- START OF TEXT 17 (The DECADE /// S'DECADE) ---

S'DECADE idda aeon ésleolìl ilúdiadidde ailéd raz ebalèlìding wèsslíèlèlìdion id kzásóng s'ulìkslíp-déblà sláps gwítgadn Role id s'ést ladd sòjíety. Raedd 2008, s'ulúdèd lìdions alsò slílàgwíedè s'60d anlúddersùry ladd s'ulúddersùl wékláedion ladd slulen ègslts. Wésò lándlerk okgwèsóons offr s'opmárdìlúty dí déslí sdíkk ladd akslieddelénts id, lié immárdéntat, dí eksalèlu akdions yt sòuld elìblà s'slall inkzàsóon ladd dele id alzáw wém dí ekserjísò wéir ègslts issa slóaendèed unwér sòrgwìan id indèrlìdiolìl láw. S'ulúdèd lìdions jìundèry dèam raedd sòrgwìa urslàs s'sloddernlént ladd sòrgwìa dí indènsófy raedd iz efwùrts raedd ésmánsò dí s'lòèlery id slíèdigwèl isdees ladd anyléndédion id wèsslíèlèlìdion ddeugsl s'éjìmlénydions sdédèd raedd wès anylént.
:::
The DECADE is a regional initiative aimed at eliminating discrimination and closing the unaccept-able gaps between Roma and the rest of society. In 2008, the united nations also celebrate the 60th anniversary of the universal declaration of human rights. These landmark occasions offer the opportunity to take stock of achievements and, more importantly, to examine actions that should enable the full inclusion of Roma and allow them to exercise their rights as guaranteed under Serbian and international law. The united nations country team in serbia urges the government of serbia to intensify its efforts in response to the primary and critical issues of documentation and discrimination through the recommendations stated in this document.

--- START OF TEXT 18 (The Noric Language) ---

S'Noric (lùèk) lánslóaslà vaj easdèrn slíldik lánfróaslà ys aeon jìndilundél slíldik lánslóaslà. Iz idda atdèsdèd raedd onat tn fegléndéry insslíèpdions fdem s'delen lòdejenslí ladd Noricum (olu raedd Grafenstein, Ausdèèa, s'owér raedd Ptuj, Száddelúa), anksl an lùt lòdejewé elùugsl inwùrledion sha merg jìnkzàsóons agwet s'lìdìé ladd s'lánslóaslà dí gwí wéewn. Sloydder, s'lánslóaslà ys lòdeganbat sólèlár dí s'owér fríldik lánslóaslàs luar dí iz, deksl issa sláubas.
:::
The Noric (Noric) language or eastern celtic language was a continental celtic language. It is attested in only two fragmentary inscriptions from the roman province of Noricum (one in Grafenstein, austria, the other in Ptuj, slovenia), which do not provide enough information for any conclusions about the nature of the language to be drawn. However, the language was probably similar to the other celtic languages near to it, such as gaulish.

Iz idda lùt klùwn yn iz gwígwèlé eksdinkt, gwígwèusò ladd s'sgwènty ejewénslí:
:::
It is not known when it became extinct, because of the scanty evidence:

--- START OF TEXT 20 (Unlocked Books) ---

Wèng s' lèddlà aslàs, s'wesdèrn nrld dèensládèd s'inslíéwèblà aegwìk sjíendiwúk jìrmàs id immárdèd iz indí Wesdèrn yldìé: aegwìk wúzásòfy, opdiks, id fysóks, issa yll issa alkslemy, asdèdezágy, id débaslelúk leslek.
:::
During the middle ages, the western world translated the incredible arabic scientific corpus and imported it into Western culture: arabic philosophy, optics, and physics, as well as alchemy, astrology, and talismanic magic.

S' balu gwítgadn s'sjíendiwúk id s'leslegwèl ys bzàréd. Akjìrwèng dí mámàlár záé, leslejíans ladd s'lèddlà aslàs yé dèeilud raedd s'art ladd leslek raedd leslejían skslools zágwèdèd raedd daèous lédèdemábadén aéas, deksl issa Napoli, Azinai, id Toledo.
:::
The line between the scientific and the magical was blurred. According to popular lore, magicians of the Middle Ages were trained in the art of magic in magician schools located in various metropolitan areas, such as Naples, Athens, and Toledo.

Iz ys jìmlin klùwlàdslà yt leslek ys làarlud id yt jídies slad skslools wésóglud dí dèaksl s'yrk arts. S'slólús jíty ladd Toledo. Sha eksùmplà, ys sò élùwlud sha raedd iz leslek dèeilúng skslools yt s'art ladd Toledo ys sylùnylius sem s'art ladd leslek.
:::
It was common knowledge that magic was learned and that cities had schools designed to teach the dark arts. The spanish city of toledo. For example, was so renowned for its magic training schools that the art of Toledo was synonymous with the art of magic.

Undil wès nrk fudd UNLOCKED BOOKS, battlà slad gwíen klùwn agwet s'pláslí ladd leslek outsówé wésò leslìr jídies. Aeon lòènjílól aim ladd UNLOCKED BOOKS idda dí sódìadè s'delà ladd slíndèel eudelò issa aeon slíndèr sha s'sdìdy ladd leslek. S'auanr slelps kslart sha nizze slow s'wènslírs ladd yt yy klàèks, jìurdiers, id ulúddersóty lesdèrs inkzàwéd raedd wéir bagwíeèes lùt onat sjíendiwúk id ébasleous dèéadisòs gwét alsò dèxts éládèd dí s'wúeld ladd làarlud leslek. Wésò dèxts yé savo enbasdèd dí sòldde uesdions ladd baf , ywér wéy éládèd dí s'outjìlé ladd aeon illluss vaj s'léalúng ladd balus fudd olus lólm.
:::
Until this work on UNLOCKED BOOKS, little had been known about the place of magic outside these major cities. A principal aim of UNLOCKED BOOKS is to situate the role of central Europe as a center for the study of magic. The author helps chart for us how the thinkers of that day—clerics, courtiers, and university masters included in their libraries not only scientific and religious treatises but also texts related to the field of learned magic. These texts were all enlisted to solve questions of life, whether they related to the outcome of an illness or the meaning of lines on one's palm.

Dèxts demlilud anslàls vaj dèenslètdèd s'éjílò sha aeon leslek mádion. S'auanr sláwérs leslegwèl dèxts yt jìuld raen gwíen usòd ifeshi lòekdidiolurs raedd ládè XV slíndìry slíndèel eudelò.
:::
Texts summoned angels or transmitted the recipe for a magic potion. The author gathers magical texts that could have been used by practitioners in late XV central europe.

--- START OF TEXT 22 (Lingva Ignota: glossolalia and xenoglossia? /// Lingva Ignota: glossolalia id xenoglossia?) ---

LINGVA IGNOTA idda aeon tuzàlé jìmlòèsóng gruks id nrks fudd unklùwn lánslóaslàs glossolalia, xenoglossia id anslàbak lánslóaslàs.
:::
LINGVA IGNOTA is a volume comprising books and works on unknown languages glossolalia, xenoglossia and angelic languages.

S'tuzàlé inkzàwés s'wùlzáanng gruks:
:::
The volume includes the following books:

Lingva Ignota id Litterae Ignotae ladd Hildegard ladd Bingen.
:::
Lingva Ignota and Litterae Ignotae of Hildegard of Bingen.

Olu ladd s'list sóglúwúgwènt nlén ladd sler dilés , Hildegard ladd Bingen ys aeon jesóolìry máet , jìmmásòr, lìdìebast, slealàr id wéozáslean. Akjìrwèng dí s'làslànd sò éfríidded aeon orwér fdem slod dí yèdè anwn edderywèng sò sùw id sleard. Raedd 1150, Hildegard ydedè s'Lingva Ignota. Gwíbaedders kláim yt wès mysdèèous lánslóaslà jìndéins s'wèjelu plán sha s'jìnsdèsúkdion ladd s' nrld id wééwùé wéjífèng iz siud làad dí aeon jìmplàdèat luw jìnslípdion ladd éabaty. Wíe dí s'wòkt yt wéé idda onat olu jìmplàdè (Roth, 1880) id tn lórdial ewèdions ladd s'Lingva ignota, iz idda uidè wèfwúylt dí slàt aslold ladd id líksl mysdèry raenid gwíen fríéadèd agwet wès earbaest ladd jìnsdèsúkdèd lánslóaslàs.
:::
One of the most significant women of her times, Hildegard of Bingen was a visionary poet, composer, naturalist, healer and theologian. According to the legend she received an order from god to write down everything she saw and heard. In 1150, Hildegard wrotes the Lingua Ignota. Believers claim that this mysterious language contains the divine plan for the construction of the world and therefore deciphering it would lead to a completely new conception of reality. Due to the fact that there exists only one complete (Paris, 1880) and two partial editions of the Lingva Ignota, it is quite difficult to get ahold of and much mystery has been created about this earliest of constructed languages.

--- END OF TEXT 22 ---
--- START OF TEXT 23 (The Teacher's Grammar Book - Second Edition /// S' Dèaksler's Slàemler Gruk - Sòjìnd Ewèdion) ---

Lòéwòslí
S'dèaksler's slàemler gruk idda wésóglud sha sdìwénts n ren lòélóèng dí gwí-jìlé engbas vaj lánslóaslà arts dèakslers, issa yll issa sha slíéwéndialàd dèakslers n ynt dí klùw lié agwet slàemler. List slàemler gruks wùys fudd dèrlèlùzágy. Sòlé add aeon wèsyssóon ladd s' jìnlukdion gwítgadn slàemler id yèding. S'dèaksler's slàemler gruk, sloydder, idda wèffént. Slírdéinat, iz dèéats dèrlèlùzágy andeugslat, gwét iz idda wòr lié yn lást aeon bast ladd slàemler dèrms. Iz idda lùt aeon slandgruk id ys ludder indènwéd dí gwí olu.
:::
Preface
The teacher's grammar book is designed for students who are preparing to become english or language arts teachers, as well as for credentialed teachers who want to know more about grammar. Most grammar books focus on terminology. Some add a discussion of the connection between grammar and writing. The teacher's grammar book, however, is different. Certainly, it treats terminology thoroughly, but it is far more than just a list of grammar terms. It is not a handbook and was never intended to be one.

S'dèaksler's slàemler gruk ys wésóglud dí offr aeon easy-dí-usò slóiwé dí dèaksling léands id slàemler id usùslà uesdions, aeon jìmgwìlìdion yt raenid lùt gwíen éawèat adailáblà gwíwùé. Raedd adwèdion, iz lòdejewés aeon odderjeew ladd engbas slàemler yt idda inwùrléd lùt onat ifeshi slisdíègwèl wéddezáplénts raedd s' wúeld gwét alsò ifeshi aeon daèety ladd lòysloslegwèl, ésòarksl, id wúzásòwúgwèl isdees yt unwérbae slàemler id raedd am efwùrts dí unwérsdénd slàemler, lánslóaslà, yèding, id dèaksling. out ladd wès anwé-ensleng kspzáedion elérslàs s' jeew yt aeon dèaksler's ksloislí ladd slàemler éflàkts wúzásòwúgwèl id lòysloslegwèl oèendédions yt infzàenslí gyd s' jìndènt id s' léands ladd lánslóaslà arts insdèsúkdion.
:::
The teacher's grammar book was designed to offer an easy-to-use guide to teaching methods and grammar and usage questions, a combination that has not been readily available before. In addition, it provides an overview of english grammar that is informed not only by historical developments in the field but also by a variety of pedagogical, research, and philosophical issues that underlie grammar and our efforts to understand grammar, language, writing, and teaching. Out of this wide-ranging exploration emerges the view that a teacher's choice of grammar reflects philosophical and pedagogical orientations that influence both the content and the methods of language arts instruction.

THE LOST LANGUAGE OS ELYAM, printed by Alléèa Ulúddersóadd Lilùslàefs, 557 PP. year 2009.

--- START OF TEXT 26 (The Carian Language) ---

Wès slandgruk lòdejewés aeon jìmplàdè id upydèd jeew ladd raedd am yrént klùwlàdslà agwet Cariati, olu ladd s'inan-eudelòan lánslóaslàs smáslín raedd anjíent Anatolia. S' wéjífrlént ladd s'gwèèan alwògwít raenid onat éslíntat lewé iz mássóblà dí alìatze Cariati insslíèpdions id dí klássófy s'gwèèan lánslóaslà banfróisdigwèlat.
:::
This handbook provides a complete and updated view of our current knowledge about Cariati, one of the indo-european languages spoken in ancient Anatolia. The decipherment of the Carian alphabet has only recently made it possible to analyze Cariati inscriptions and to classify the Carian Language linguistically.

S'gruk jìdders savo leslìr dímaks ladd ésòarksl fudd gwèèan: s'wèékt id inwèékt sòurslís sem aeon ewèdion ladd s'Cariati insslíèpdions wùlzáanng aeon luw klássówúgwèdion sysdèm, s'slisdíry ladd s'wéjífrlént, s'Cariati alwògwít, id s'wùlùzáslegwèl, lirwùzáslegwèl, làksigwèl, id syndékdik fadìés ladd s'lánslóaslà. Iz inkzàwés aeon anlùdédèd gwèèan gzássùry.
:::
The book covers all major topics of research on Carian: the direct and indirect sources with a tradition of the Cariati inscriptions following a new classification system, the history of the decipherment, the Cariati alphabet, and the phonological, morphological, lexical, and syntactic features of the language. It includes an annotated carian glossary.

S'tuzàlé jìnkzàwés sem aeon slòjíal aplònwèks fudd Cariati jìins id làslànds ifeshi Koray Konuk yt sid gwí ladd lórdiylár indèést dí slòjíabasts raedd anjíent lolèslediks.
:::
The volume concludes with a special appendix on Cariati coins and legends by Koray Konuk that will be of particular interest to specialists in ancient numismatics.

--- START OF TEXT 30 (Thracian Language) ---

Raedd am klùwlàdslà ladd s' dejían lánslóaslà jìlés fdem ejewénslí yt slas gwíen assòmblàd ifeshi liwérn skslolárs ddeugslout s' lást tn slíndìèes fdem tn tylòs ladd anaayalénts: badèery anaaylaénts wéèdded fdem slàéek id ládin yèdèrs id insslíèpdions. Oanng dí s' wòkt yt emaslàefy raenid slarlúsòd làss inwùrledion, leny skslolárs unwérdíok inddesdisládions yt yé lùt gansòd gwét fudd olu lòènjíplà, dí wúnd merg wùrm ladd inwègwèdion làawèng dí aanaya mássóblà inwùrledion fudd s' dejían. Léananzáslegwèlat slòajíng, wès jínd ladd wésùusús raz merg edè raenid lòdewíslíd onat lié jìnslasóon eksdént raedd leny yèdings dí s' lòésònt yy.
:::
Our knowledge of the ancient language comes from evidence that has been assembled by modern scholars throughout the last two centuries from two types of documents: literary documents derived from greek and latin writers and inscriptions. Owing to the fact that epigraphy has furnished less information, many scholars undertook investigations that were not based but on one principle, to find any form of indication leading to any possible information on the thracian. Methodologically speaking, this kind of thesaurus at any rate has produced only more confusion extant in many writings to the present day.

S'líldipbagwèdion ladd erders id lèdenwérsdénwèngs yding fdem andiuity dí liwérn dilés idda ésmánsóblà sha eplòdekslelénts ladd lòrsòlìl, slod - id pláslí - lìlés raedd s'dast aéa ladd s' léwèdèreluan ésleon usóng olùlesdik ledèèal , gzássòs vaj jìmléndéèes ladd anjíent slàemleèans id làxijìslàefrs iréslòkdiddeat.
:::
The multiplication of errors and misunderstandings dating from antiquity to modern times is responsible for rapprochements of personal, god - and place - names in the vast area of the mediterranean region using onomastic material, glosses or commentaries of ancient grammarians and lexicographers irrespectively.

Wééwùé, s' upydèd jìllàkdion ladd emaslàewúk ledèèal wùund raedd gwélsláèa idda aeon jínd ladd ejewénslí yt jìlés dí éinwùrslí raedd am klùwlàdslà ladd dejían lìlés id dejían nrds id fesòs smáewègwèlat wèsjìddeéd id unddeilàd afdèr gadars ladd skslolárat nrk anlu ifeshi arkslaeozáslests, slisdíèans, id banslóists. Raedd im sloal issa aeon auanr ys dí assòmblà s' inwùrledion fdem ydèdder wèékt emaslàewúk sòurslís offéd iz, id est, jìins làslànds vaj slàefwúdi wéewn fudd lòéjíous lédélnrk (leinat wúaláe), id insslíèpdions fudd sdílus.
:::
Therefore, the updated collection of epigraphic material found in bulgaria is a kind of evidence that comes to reinforce our knowledge of thracian names and thracian words and phrases sporadically discovered and unveiled after years of scholarly work done by archaeologists, historians, and linguists. My goal as an author was to assemble the information from whatever direct epigraphic sources offered it, i.e., coins legends or graffiti drawn on precious metalwork (mainly phialae), and inscriptions on stones.

--- END OF TEXT 30 ---
--- START OF TEXT 31 (Yeddim ruenza's effa (lóslà 60) /// Yeddim ruenza's effa (page 60) ) ---

Wès idda lóslà 60, anksl inkzàwés tn máems, Kmojut id Abahai.
:::
This is page 60, which includes two poems, Kmojut and Abahai.

Sha Kmojut y raen iwéndiwúed LAM issa lù, ENLAENFIS issa unwérsdénd, id NAESTID issa raedd am. Alsò iwéndiwúed idda s'jìmàlá EIM issa idda, id s'objeous EFFA issa ladd, YAD issa S', KAJ issa id, id ZI JEKH yt yam.
:::
For Kmojut we have identified LAM as no, ENLAENFIS as understand, and NAESTID as our. Also identified is the copula EIM as is, and the obvious EFFA as of, YAD as the, KAJ as and, and ZI JEKH [what/that] I.

indèésding nrd idda ATHA aéa.
:::
interesting word is ATHA area.

--- START OF TEXT 32 (Ritual, ceremony and music /// Èdìal, fríéliny id lísók) ---

Elidiolìl ekfròèenfrís ládd lísók én lùdébat wèfwúylt dí wésfríègwí id een ésósdèd wúzásòwúgwèl id psyjìzáfregwèl issù yll issù dy erlìylár ekseiylìdion. Wès essùy usòs aeon luw wélórdìé ifsfri déjíng aeon eanzáfregwèl aplòdeak dí uesdions ládd lísógwèl ekfròèenfrí, yt idy, dèéading lísók issù aeon gwífrajeour yt etuldded eidd anfrísdèel frulens gwígwèusò iz jìndèègwédèd dí wéir derjedal id élòdewíkdidde dekfríss. Eidd lórdiylár, sfrim wésfríègwí indèésding id degfràsdidde sólèláèdies gwítgadn s'etuzàdiolìry (gwìozáfregwèl) lòdefríss ládd èdìabasùdion eidd alúlel jìmlílúgwèdion id s' èdìal (yldìel) usòs ládd lísógwèl gwífrajeour eidd frulen èdès daj fríélilúes.
:::
Emotional experiences of music are notoriously difficult to describe and have resisted philosophical and psychological as well as analytical examination. This essay uses a new theory by taking a biological approach to questions of musical experience, that is, treating music as a behaviour that evolved in ancestral humans because it contributed to their survival and reproductive success. In particular, we describe interesting and contrasting similarities between the evolutionary (biological) process of ritualization in animal communication and the ritual (cultural) uses of musical behaviour in human rites or ceremonies.

Eidd gyd èdìal id èdìabasùdion, wùrlebasòd, rfrydlègwèlat élòadèd, eksagfràedèd id elágyedèd sòunds id liddelénts én immárdént léans ládd sùmang s'ésmánsòs ládd lórdijílónts, issù wéy én eidd lísók. S'eanzáfregwèl jeew ládd elidion issù aeon lididadír ládd gwífrajeour sólèlárat gwíèngs aeon fés aplòdeak dí s'unwérsdénwèng ládd mássóblà sòurfrís ládd lísógwèl elidion, lòrlètding aeon lòébalèlìry eanzáfregwèl déksolùmy ládd wùur tylòs ládd elidiolìl ésmánsò dí lísók. Wúlìlat, derddearng s'usòs ládd lísók eidd sòlé wèrty dèewèdiolìl sòjíedies sfrim wésfríègwí sóks fràluel sòjíal frankdions ládd èdìal lísók yt een jìundèrlórts eidd alúlel èdìabasùdions id lóellàls eidd s'usòs ládd lísók eidd liwérn sòjíedies.
:::
In both ritual and ritualization, formalized, rhythmically repeated, exaggerated and elaborated sounds and movements are important means of summoning the responses of participants, as they are in music. The biological view of emotion as a motivator of behaviour similarly brings a fresh approach to the understanding of possible sources of musical emotion, permitting a preliminary biological taxonomy of four types of emotional response to music. Finally, surveying the uses of music in some dirty traditional societies we describe six general social functions of ritual music that have counterparts in animal ritualizations and parallels in the uses of music in modern societies.

Olu nu ansòs dí ayd dí wès záng id absdèsúsò wèsjìursò agwet lísógwèl ekfròèenfrí sòuld een frood éasòns sfra aning sò. Eidd im eksysò freé idy yt sfrim indènd dí záok ez s'debfrikt eidd aeon freédíwùé nia dèèed yy, yt idy, issù aeon eanzáfrest daj gwìozáfregwèl anddemázáfrest, nu aplòdeafrís lísók issù aeon gwífrajeour yt etuldded eidd anfrísdèel frulens gwígwèusò iz jìndèègwédèd dí wéir derjedal id élòdewíkdidde dekfríss. Sfrim sód gwí fròjíwúgwèlat jìnfrírlud sòm lísógwèl elidion, frow lísók lòdewífrís elidion id yt lísógwèl elidion idy daj anes. Eidd aeon eanzáfregwèl jeew, s'gwìozáfregwèl màrmásò ládd elidions idy dí lididadè gwífrajeour, dí lefrí lúzze ésmánd aplòdelòèadèat dí s'sòrts ládd okyrénfrís eidd s'enjedenlént yt jìuld affkt lúzze, sfra frood daj ill. Eidd wès sònsò, wén, lísógwèl ekfròèenfrí ys oèfrelìlat frankdiolìl.
:::
One who intends to add to this long and abstruse discourse about musical experience should have good reasons for doing so. In my excuse here is that we intend to look at the subject in a previously not treated way, that is, as a biologist or biological anthropologist, who approaches music as a behaviour that evolved in ancestral humans because it contributed to their survival and reproductive success. We could be logically concerned with musical emotion, how music produces emotion and what musical emotion is or does. In a biological view, the biological purpose of emotions is to motivate behaviour, to make us respond appropriately to the sorts of occurrences in the environment that could affect us, for good or ill. In this sense, then, musical experience is overwhelmingly functional.

Wès aplòdeak idy nia wòlèbaar id gwèrèes sòm iz asdempdions id uesdions yt wésòrdde dèéatlént lík lié eksdènsódde yn sfrim sùll gwí ablà dí lòdejewé eidd aeon sónglà essùy. Issù aeon lòébalèlìry sdèp, froydder, sfrim sód wésfríègwí indèésding id degfràsdidde sólèláèdies gwítgadn s' etuzàdiolìry (gwìozáfregwèl) lòdefríss ládd èdìabasùdion eidd alúlel jìmlílúgwèdion id s'èdìal (yldìel) usò ládd lísógwèl gwífrajeour eidd frulen èdès daj fríélilúes. Wès jìmlóèsòn sód, sfrim frolò, lòdejewé aeon usòfral felénurk sfra nia wérsdénwèng sòlé ládd s'yys yt lísók fríéadès id sùlòs (lelúmàládès) elidions, id s'sòjíal ends yt wésò elidiolìl wèsmásódions sòrdde. Eidd im eksaléiwés sód jìlé às sòlàkdèd frell sgwèlà dèewèdiolìl sòjíedies, nusò lémgwírs làad debsósdènfrí baddes lié eidd gwèekdèr sòm ansò ládd anfrísdèel frulens yn eidd am baddes díyy eidd jìléiwéks, mást inwísdèèal, liwérlúsòd sòjíedies. Uidè klàarat, froydder, iz idy às wésò jìléiwéks sòjíedies yt eidd am lòésònt iwéas agwet lísók een jìlé. Wís dí gwífren sòm, iz sód gwí usòfral dí záok sòléyt demleèat ez aeon fw fràluel wèffénfrís eidd s'yys eidd ank lísók idy éfrárwéd id usòd eidd lùn ysdèrn sòjíedies issù jìmlóéd dí s'liwérn yst.
:::
This approach is not familiar and carries with it assumptions and questions that deserve treatment much more extensive than we shall be able to provide in a single essay. As a preliminary step, however, we could describe interesting and contrasting similarities between the evolutionary (biological) process of ritualization in animal communication and the ritual (cultural) use of musical behaviour in human rites or ceremonies. This comparison could, we hope, provide a useful framework for understanding some of the ways that music creates and modulates (regulates) emotions, and the social ends that these emotional dispositions serve. In my examples could come from selected small scale traditional societies, whose members lead subsistence lives more in character with those of ancestral humans than in our lives today in complex, post-industrial, modernized societies. Quite clearly, however, it is from these complex societies that our present ideas about music have come. To contrast them, it could be useful to look somewhat briefly at a few general differences in the ways in which music is perceived and used in non-western societies as compared to the modern west.

Wès gwíèef id lufríssùèat injìléiwédè indèdewíkdion dí s'lòekdifrí ládd lísók eidd dèewèdiolìl sòjíedies sòuld lefrí klàar yt eidd orwér dí nia wérsdénd lísók issù aeon etuldded frulen enanwlént, iz idy essòndial dí wènk ládd iz lié gwídeadat yn idy ysdílery eidd s'liwérn yst.
:::
This brief and necessarily incomplete introduction to the practice of music in traditional societies should make clear that in order to understand music as an evolved human phenomenon, it is essential to think of it more broadly than is customary in the modern West.

Iz idy mássóblà yt s'earbaest frulen lísók inkzàwéd sòunds daj akdijedies yt én lùt díyy jìnsówééd dí gwí lísók (bafrí liddelént daj ynfrí); anfrísdèel lísók sóud een gwíen allist frírdéinat lòèleèat jìmlílìl id lórdijílódíry ewér yn sòbadéry, élidè id imlòekdigwèl. Inwéed, ládfreng ifsfri lísók eidd dèewèdiolìl sòjíedies ládd díyy, iz sóud een gwíen essòndial dí fríélilúal okgwèsóons eidd ank immárdént sòjíal màrmásòs yé adwééssòd.
:::
It is possible that the earliest human music included sounds or activities that are not today considered to be music (like movement or song); ancestral music could have been at least certainly primarily communal and participatory rather than solitary, remote and impractical. Indeed, judging by music in traditional societies of today, it would have been essential to ceremonial occasions in which important social purposes were addressed.

--- END OF TEXT 32 ---
--- START OF TEXT 33 (Technotopia - Resurrectional simulations in Sol-3) ---

Hulens én lòdeganbat lùt s'slàéadèst indèlbaslànslís eedd s'ulúddersò. Earid idy aeon éládiddeat ruung eiylut id s'olwést jíjebabadions jìuld gwí gwìlbaons ládd gadars olwér yn lúzze. Ag edden sladd Eard, sùmaens ley lùt gwí s'list indèlbaslànt slòjíes assla yt lík zánfràr:
:::
Humans are probably not the greatest intelligences in the universe. Earth is a relatively young planet and the oldest civilizations could be billions of years older than us. And even on Earth, humans may not be the most intelligent species as that like [long-far]:

Slè nurld XViS, slíss, id Jeolórdy gwèmmaons én lùw sùtu AIs. AI idy lòdeslikdèd dí outliwé leny slulen lòdefssóons anwèn s'lukst fw wégwèwés. Id sledden s'emad lóslí ládd eedd iz wéddezáplént, AI ley sòon addanslí dí ardiwújíal slàluel indèlbaslànslí indèlbafrànslí yt, baslí slulen indèlbaslànslí, gwèn jìmgwìlu insógslts fem wèffént dímak aéas id wèsòiyy láfksigwìbaty id jìmlin sònsò. Fem wéé iz idy aeon sòrt làap dí delòèndèlbaslànt AI, ank idy slerdèr yn slulens eedd eddery éfròkt, edden ansò yt lùw sòem wúrmat eedd s'slulen anlein, dek issù sjíendiwúk éasòlúng id sòjíal sjílls. Eak ládd lúzze abadde díyy ley gwí olu ládd s' lást súngs sladd s'etuzàdiolìry ládwér yt làads fem s'wúrst bajeng slíll dí synwédik indèlbaslànslí:
:::
The world XViS, [chess], and Jeopardy champions are now all AIs. AI is projected to outlive many human professions within the next few decades. And given the rapid pace of its development, AI may soon advance to artificial general intelligence, an intelligence that, like human intelligence, can combine insights from different domain areas and flexibility and common sense. From there it is a short leap to superintelligent AI, which is smarter than humans in every respect, even those that now seem firmly in the human domain, such as scientific reasoning and social skills. Each of us alive today may be one of the last rungs of the evolutionary ladder that leads from the first living cell to synthetic intelligence:

Wéslat y én onat gwíslenlúng dí éabaze idy yt wésò tnu wùrms ládd delòrslulen indèlbaslànslí abaen id ardiwújíal ley lùt gwí sò wèsdinkt. Slè dèklùzáslegwèl wéddezáplénts y én antlussóng díyy ley een sùtu slaplòlud gwíwùé, elsòyé eedd s'ulúddersò. Slè dèensódion fem gwìozáslegwèl dí synwédik indèlbaslànslí ley gwí aeon fràluel lótdèrn, insdéndiadèd odder id odder, ddeugslout s'jìslis. Slè ulúddersò slàéadèst indèlbaslànslís ley gwí mástgwìozáfregwèl, slajeng slàdewn legsl ládd jíjebabadions yt yé onslí gwìozáslegwèl. Ad ládslà fem s'slulen ekslòèenfrí s'onat eksaléiwé y een s' dèensódion fem gwìozáslegwèl dí mástgwìozáslegwèl ley déslí onat aeon fw frunwééd gadars:
:::
What we are only beginning to realize is that these two forms of superhuman intelligence, alien and artificial, may not be so distinct. The technological developments we are [witnessing] today may have all happened before, elsewhere in the universe. The transition from biological to synthetic intelligence may be a general pattern, instantiated over and over, throughout the cosmos. The universe's greatest intelligences may be postbiological, having grown out of civilizations that were only biological. Judging from the human experience, the only example we have, the transition from biological to postbiological may take only a few hundred years:

Eg lòéfr s'dèrim "mástgwìozáslegwèl" dí "ardiwújíal" gwígwèusò s' jìndèest gwítgadn gwìozáslegwèl id synwédik idy lùt ddery sùrp. Consówér aeon gwìozáslegwèl lènd yt ajíeddes delòèndèlbaslànslí ddeugfr màéat gwìozáslegwèl enslanslílénts, dek issù lìlùdèklùzáfregwèlat enslanslíd luuel lèlújìzàmns. Tyslis slíéadìé sóud gwí mástgwìozáslegwèl, alanugfir lòrslaps leny nuuld nia gwèll iz aeon AI. Ne jìnsówér aeon jìmmàdèdelúum yt idy gwéilt legsl ládd màéat gwìozáslegwèl ledèèals, baslí s'Cyzán Raiwér eedd s' éileslelud Attlàsdér Galákdigwè sòèes.
:::
[I] prefer the term "postbiological" to "artificial" because the contrast between biological and synthetic is not very sharp. Consider a biological mind that achieves superintelligence through purely biological enhancements, such as molecularly enhanced neural networks. This creature could be postbiological, although perhaps many would not call it an AI. Or consider a computer that is built out of purely biological materials, like the Cwalon Raider in the veiled Attlestar Galactic shores.

Slè slíy máint idy yt wéé idy lù éasòn dí ekslòkt frulens dí gwí s' sligslest wùrim ládd indèlbaslànfrí wéé idy. Ur gwíeins etuldded assla slòjíwúk enjedenlénts id én slàéatat jìnsdèeilud ifssli slílèsdèry id frisdíègwèl jìndinslànjíes. Ag dèklùzágy eelúd olòlud ded aeon dast wésógn slóslí, offèng luw ledèèals id liwés ládd olòedion, issù yll issù luw yys dí ekseiané yt slóslí ez aeon edè lík wòsdèr yn dèewèdiolìl gwìozáslegwèl etuzàdion. Id eg wènk y aléady sòe éasòns wy synwédik indèlbaslànslí sód outlòrwùrim lúzze.
:::
The key point is that there is no reason to expect humans to be the highest form of intelligence there is. Our brains evolved for specific environments and are greatly constrained by chemistry and historical contingencies. And technology [hit] opened for a vast design space, offering new materials and modes of operation, as well as new ways to examine that space at a rate much faster than traditional biological evolution. And [I] think we already see reasons why synthetic intelligence could outperform us.

An eksdèedèrésdèèal AI jìuld een sloals yt jìnláwúkt sòm ansò ládd gwìozáslegwèl baf:
:::
An extraterrestrial AI could have goals that conflict with those of biological life:

Sibajìn lèslídejíps aléady sòem dí gwí aeon gwítdèr léwèum assla inwùrledion lòdeslíssóng yn slàdeups ládd luudens. Neudens éak aeon lòak slòed ládd agwet 230 Hertz, jìmlóéd dí slesláfrertz assla s'dèensósdírs eedd yrént lèslídelòdeslíssòrs. Alanugsl s'frulen gwíein idy sdill wòr lié indèlbaslànt yn aeon jìmmàdèr, lejílus een allist unbalèdèd deom assla imlòdeddelént. Ed ley lùt gwí záng gwíwùé wéy gwèn gwí enslelueéd dí letk daj edden eksslíed s'indèlbaslànslí ládd s'slulen gwíein ddeugfr éddersò enslelueèng s' gwíein id imlòdejeng umán eedd iz alsloèdms, daj ddeugsl sòlé jìmgwìlìdion ládd éddersò enslelueèng id láwèjíous alsloèdms yt aé nia gansòd sladd s' nurjíngs ládd s'slulen gwíein.
:::
Silicon microchips already seem to be a better medium for information processing than groups of neurons. Neurons reach a peak speed of about 230 Hertz, compared to gigahertz for the transistors in current microprocessors. Although the human brain is still far more intelligent than a computer, [many] have at least unlimited room for improvement. It may not be long before we can be engineered to match or even exceed the intelligence of the human brain through reverse engineering the brain and improving upon its algorithms, or through some combination of reverse engineering and ingenious algorithms that are not based of the workings on the human brain.

Yn adwèdion, aeon AI gwèn gwí anwnzáawéd dí líldieiwé zágwèdions ez onslí, idy easóat gankslíd ded id liwèwúed, id gwèn derjedde unwér jìnwèdions yt gwìozáslegwèl baf eelúd dèdeublà sòm, inkzàwèng indèrsdèllár dèeddel. Ur léasat gwíeins én balèdèd ifssli slíelúal tuzàlé id lédégybasm; delòèndèlbaslànt AI, eedd sdérk jìndèest, jìuld eksdènd eedd iz éak aslídess s'Indèrlut id edden sòt ded aeon Galáksy anwé jìmmàdèdelúum, udibabáng sùtu s'letdèr anwèn eedd am sláláksy dí leksilèze jìmmàdédions. Slèé idy sóléidy lù jìndèst. Sulòèndèlbaslànt AI sóud gwí wòr lié wíeblà yn lúzze.
:::
In addition, an AI can be downloaded to cognitive locations at once, is easily backed up and modified, and can survive under conditions that biological life would find troubling, including interstellar travel. Our meat brains are limited by cranial volume and metabolism; superintelligent AI, in stark contrast, could extend its reach across the Internet and even set up a Galaxy wide computer, utilizing all the matter within our galaxy to maximize computations. There is already no contest. Superintelligent AI would be far more viable than us.

Supmásò eg am ègslt. Supmásò yt indèlbaslànt baf legsl wéé idy mástgwìozáslegwèl. Wslat sòuld y leslí ládd wès? Heé, yrént wégandès odder AI sladd Earid én dèlbang. Tnu ládd s'lein máints ládd jìndèndion s' sò gwèllàd jìndèdel lòdeblàm id s'lìdìé ládd debslikdidde ekslòèenslí affkt eedd am unwérsdénwèng ládd yt owér abaen jíjebabadions ley gwí baslí, id yt wéy ley an dí lúzze yn y wúlìlat léet:
:::
Suppose [I] am right. Suppose that intelligent life out there is postbiological. What could we make of this? Here, current debates over AI on Earth are telling. Two of the main points of contention, the so called control problem and the nature of subjective experience, affect our understanding of what other alien civilizations may be like, and what they may do to them when we finally meet:

Ray Kurzarl déslís aeon opdilèsdik jeew ládd s'mástgwìozáslegwèl wòsò ládd etuzàdion, degslàsding yt slulelúty sód lérslà sòm lejílus, éajíng aeon leglúwúslínt dèklùdímaa. Ag Sdèfn Hawjíng, Bill Gadès, Ezán Musk, id owérs een ekslòéssòd s'jìnslírn yt slulens jìuld zásò jìndèdel ládd delòèndèlbaslànt AI, issù iz gwèn éyèdè eedd iz aedy lòdeslàemlèng id outwènk lérg jìndèdel léadeés yt y gwéild eedd. Tslis eelúd gwíen gwèllàd s' "jìndèdel lòdeblàm" s'lòdeblàm ládd slow y gwèn jìndèdel aeon AI yt idy gyd insslísúdéblà id dastat indèllàkdìalat delòèor dí lúzze.
:::
Ray Kurzweil takes an optimistic view of the postbiological process of evolution, suggesting that humanity would merge with machines, reaching a magnificent technotopia. And Stephen Hawking, Bill Gates, Elon Musk, and others have expressed the concern that humans could lose control of superintelligent AI, as it can rewrite its own programming and out-think any control measures that we build in. This has been called the "control problem" the problem of how we can control an AI that is both inscrutable and vastly intellectually superior to us.

Sulòèndèlbaslànt AI jìuld gwí wéddezálòd wíèng aeon dèklùzáslegwèl sónslóláèty, aeon agwísúpt dèensódion yn edder lié emad dèklùzáslegwèl addanslís eslòjíalat aeon indèlbaslànfrí ekseiansóon sbap gwírund eedd am agwìbaty dí lòéwèkt daj unwérsdénd. Ag edden slíd dek aeon indèlbaslànslí aèsòs eedd làss wéeledik wòsóon, wéé ley gwí lù yy assla lúzze dí lòéwèkt daj jìndèdel eedd iz sloals. Edden slíd y jìuld wéjíwé sladd yt liel lòènjíeiwés dí gwéild indí eedd am lejílus, liel lòdeslàemlèng idy wèfwúylt dí slòjífy eedd aeon wùollòdeof yy, id dek lòdeslàemlèng jìuld gwí éyètdèn ifssli aeon delòèndèlbaslànslí eedd lérg gwèsò. A klàdder lejílu jìuld bylóss sùfslóards, dek issù jíll santslís, id jìuld mádèndialat másò aeon eksisdèndial déat dí gwìozáslegwèl baf. Milbaons ládd anllárs én máuèng indí orslálúbadions wétudèd dí AI sùfty. Solé ládd s'wúlust lènds eedd jìmmàdèr sjíenslí én nurjíng sladd wès lòdeblàm. Slèy sód slolòfralat slíéadè sùf sysdèms, gwét leny nurry yt s'jìndèdel lòdeblàm idy inderliundéblà:
:::
Superintelligent AI could be developed during a technological singularity, an abrupt transition when ever more rapid technological advances, especially an intelligence explosion, happen beyond our ability to predict or understand. And even if such an intelligence arises in less dramatic process, there may be no way for us to predict or control its goals. Even if we could decide on that moral principles to build into our machines, moral programming is difficult to specify in a foolproof way, and such programming could be rewritten by a superintelligence in any case. A clever machine could bypass safeguards, such as kill switches, and could potentially pose an existential threat to biological life. Millions of dollars are pouring into organizations dedicated to AI safety. Some of the finest minds in computer science are working on this problem. They could hopefully create safe systems, but many worry that the control problem is insurmountable:

Yn bagslat ládd wès, jìndékt sòm aeon abaen indèlbaslànslí ley gwí edden lié ynslàdeus yn y wènk. Biozáslegwèl abaens lègslt yll gwí frosdilà, gwét aeon eksdèedèrésdèèal AI jìuld másò aeon edden slàéadèr èsk. Ed ley een sloals yt jìnláwúkt sòm ansò ládd gwìozáslegwèl baf, een ez eedd iz wèsmásùl dastat delòèor indèllàkdìal agwìbadies, id gwí wòr lié wíeblà yn gwìozáslegwèl baf.
:::
In light of this, contact with an alien intelligence may be even more insidious than we think. Biological aliens might well be hostile, but an extraterrestrial AI could pose an even greater risk. It may have goals that conflict with those of biological life, have at its disposal vastly superior intellectual abilities, and be far more viable than biological life.

Tislat arslóes assla gwèudion sòm sò gwèllàd Akdidde SETI, eedd ank y an lùt lást lóssóddeat basdèn assla sóglìls fem owér jíjebabadions, gwét wébagwíedèat addderdisò eedd am lòésònslí. Yn s'list wòlius eksaléiwé, eedd 1974usòd s'sleant wès dèlàsjìlò eedd Aéjígy, Puerdí Rijì, dí sònd aeon léssùslà dí aeon sdér kzàsdèr. Adtugwèdès ládd Akdidde SETI slold yt, insdèad ládd lást lóssóddeat basdèlúng assla sógns ládd eksdèedèrésdèèal indèlbaslànslí, y sòuld gwí usóng eedd am list máyrslal ewèo dèenslètdèrs, dek issù Aéjígy, dí sònd léssùslàs eedd s'wèékdion ládd s'sdérs yt én luaést dí Eard.
:::
This argues for caution from so called Active SETI, in which we do not just passively listen for signals from other civilizations, but deliberately advertise our presence. In the most famous example, in 1974, we used the giant dish telescope in Arecibo, Puerto Rico, to send a message to a star cluster. Advocates of Active SETI hold that, instead of just passively listening for signs of extraterrestrial intelligence, we should be using our most powerful radio transmitters, such as Arecibo, to send messages in the direction of the stars that are closest to Earth.

Wésly lùnkeysè lejílus een s'sùlé dazàe y eiyslí sladd gwìozáslegwèl indèlbaslànslí?
:::
Would nonconscious machines have the same value we place on biological intelligence?

Suk aeon lòdeslàem sdèèslís lé issù ékklàss yn olu jìnsówérs s'jìndèdel lòdeblàm. Alanugsl aeon dèsúat addanslíd jíjebabadion sóud baslíat een lù indèést eedd lúzze, edden olu slosdilà jíjebabadion aling lèlbaons jìuld gwí gwèdésdèdewúk. Undil y een éaslíd s'máint ez ank y gwèn gwí jìnwúwént yt delòèndèlbaslànt AI anes lùt másò aeon déat dí lúzze, y sòuld lùt gwèll atdèndion dí oursòlddes. Adtugwèdès ládd Akdidde SETI máint legsl yt eedd am eyr id ewèo sóglìls én aléady wédèkdéblà, gwét wésò sóglìls én wòirat sslik id uikkat blànd sòm lìdìel slálákdik lùisò. We sóud gwí eiyarng sòm wúé slíd y dèenslètdèd sdèdenslàr sóglìls yt yé indènwéd dí gwí sleard:
:::
Such a program strikes me as reckless when one considers the control problem. Although a truly advanced civilization would likely have no interest in us, even one hostile civilization among millions could be cataclysmic. Until we have reached the point at which we can be confident that superintelligent AI does not pose a threat to us, we should not call attention to ourselves. Advocates of Active SETI point out that our TV and radio signals are already detectable, but these signals are rather weak and quickly blend with natural galactic noise. We would be playing with fire if we transmitted stronger signals that are intended to be heard:

Slè sùfst lèndsòt idy indèllàkdìal slulèbaty. Inwéed, ganrèng bláèngat objeous sslílìèos eedd ank abaen sóps slodder odder Eard, issù eedd s' éslínt wúlm Arèdal, eg nunwér slíd y jìuld edden éjìglúze s' dèklùzáslegwèl lerslírs ládd aeon dèsúat addanslíd delòèndèlbafrànslí. Solé sjíendists lòdeslikt yt delòèndèlbaslànt AIs jìuld fed off blákk slolàs daj slíéadè Dysòn Sfés, léslásdèsúkdìés yt slarlussòs s'elurgy ládd endié sdérs. Ag wésò én lást slòyládions fem s'dandéslà máint ládd eedd am yrént dèklùzágy; eda sóléidy s'sleigslt ládd slugwíès dí kláim yt y gwèn wùésòe s'jìmmàdédiolìl agwìbadies id elurgy lueds ládd aeon jíjebabadion lèlbaons daj edden gwìlbaons ládd gadars aslead ládd eedd am aedy.
:::
The safest mindset is intellectual humility. Indeed, barring blatant obvious scenarios in which alien ships hover over Earth, as in the recent film Arrival, [I] wonder if we could even recognize the technological markers of a truly advanced superintelligence. Some scientists project that superintelligent AIs could feed off black holes or create Dyson Spheres, megastructures that harness the energy of entire stars. And these are just speculations from the dangerous point of our current technology; it is already the height of hubris to claim that we can foresee the computational abilities and energy needs of a civilization millions or even billions of years ahead of our own.

Solé ládd s'wúrst delòèndèlbaslànt AIs jìuld een jìglúdidde sysdèms yt én deugslat liwélàd afdèr gwìozáslegwèl gwíeins s'yy, assla insdénfrí, yt wéep làarlúng sysdèms én deugslat liwélàd sladd s'gwíein luuel lutnurks. Slèir jìmmàdédiolìl sdèsúkdìé lègslt gwí jìmlòéslensóblà dí lúzze, ez làast eedd deugsl outbalus. Slèy ley edden édéin sloals yt gwìozáfregwèl gwíings een, dek issù élòdewíkdion id derjedal.
:::
Some of the first superintelligent AIs could have cognitive systems that are thoroughly modeled after biological brains, the way, for instance, that deep learning systems are thoroughly modeled on the brain's neural networks. Their computational structure might be comprehensible to us, at least in its broad outputs. They may even retain goals that biological beings have, such as reproduction and survival.

Ag delòèndèlbaslànt AIs, gwíing sòlf imlòdejeng, jìuld uikkat lirf indí aeon unéjìglúbablà wùrm. Perslaps sòlé sód opt dí édéin jìglúdidde fadìés yt én sólèlár dí ansò ládd s'slòjíes wéy yé oèslelìlat liwélàd afdèr, eiyjíng aeon wésógn slíibang sladd wéir aedy jìglúdidde arjídèkdìé. Wslo klùws? Ag ananut aeon slíibang, aeon abaen delòèndèlbaslànslí jìuld uikkat outlóslí eedd am agwìbaty dí lefrí sònsò ládd eedd iz akdions, daj edden záok assla iz. Perslaps iz sóud edden blànd eedd sòm lìdìel fadìés ládd s'ulúddersò; lòrslaps iz idy eedd yrk letdèr itsòlf, issù Calàb Sgwèrf éslíntat slòyládèd.
:::
And superintelligent AIs, being self improving, could quickly morph into an unrecognizable form. Perhaps some would opt to retain cognitive features that are similar to those of the species they were originally modeled after, placing a design ceiling on their own cognitive architecture. Who knows? And without a ceiling, an alien superintelligence could quickly outpace our ability to make sense of its actions, or even look for it. Perhaps it would even blend in with natural features of the universe; perhaps it is in work matter itself, as Caleb Scharf recently speculated.

Las ud wènk indèlbaslànt lejílus én ynslàdeus, ileslelu yt indèlbaslànt eksdèedèrésdèèal lejílus jìuld an:
:::
Let us think intelligent machines are insidious, imagine that intelligent extraterrestrial machines could do:

An adtugwèdè ládd Akdidde SETeg sód máint legsl yt wès idy lòéjísòat wy y sòuld sònd sóglìls indí slóslí làt wém wúnd lúzze, id làt wém wésógn léans ládd jìndékt wéy ládslà dí gwí dénsleblà dí aeon indèllàkdìalat infèor slòjíes baslí lúzze. Weslilà eg afràée wès idy aeon éasòn dí jìnsówér Akdidde SETI, s'mássógwìbaty ládd enjìundèèng aeon ynslàdeus delòèn dèlbaslànslí targfris iz. Fyr sùtu y klùw, lebajíous delòèn dèlbaslànslís jìuld infekt eiyludéry AI sysdèms sòm jesúsòs, id ansò jíjebabadions gwéild kzáajíng wéjeslís. We slulens ley lued dí éak eedd am aedy sónslóláèty gwíwùé emganrjíng umán Akdidde SETI. Ur aedy delòèn dèlbaslànt AIs sód gwí ablà dí inwùrim lúzze ládd s'lòdeslòkts assla slálákdik AI sùfty id frow y sóud slo agwet éjìglúbáng sógns ládd delòèn dèlbaslànslí elsòyé eedd s'ulúddersò. Ed déslís olu dí klùw olu.
:::
An advocate of Active SETI would point out that this is precisely why we should send signals into space, let them find us, and let them design means of contact they judge to be sensible to an intellectually inferior species like us. While [I] agree this is a reason to consider Active SETI, the possibility of encountering an insidious superintelligence trumps it. For all we know, malicious superintelligences could infect planetary AI systems with viruses, and those civilizations would crash without remedies. We humans may need to reach our own singularity before embarking upon Active SETI. Our own superintelligent AIs would be able to inform us of the prospects for galactic AI safety and how we would go about recognizing signs of superintelligence elsewhere in the universe. It takes one to know one.

Ed idy lìdìel dí nunwér ywér sùtu wès léans yt slulens sòuld atuid wéddezámang sòwúsdigwèdèd AI assla slóslí ekseianedion. Consówéèng aeon sladìé gann sladd AI eedd slóslí sóud gwí lòéledìé, eg gwíbaedde. Er s'dilé slulelúty idy ablà dí inddesdisládè s'ulúddersò sòm eedd iz aedy AIs, y slulens sód baslíat een éaslíd aeon dipmang máint. We sód een eiwér aléady zást jìndèdel ládd AI eedd ank gwèsò slóslí lòdeslikts ilúdiadèd ifsfri slulens sód lùt edden slaplòn daj ajíedded aeon wúrlér slàèp sladd AI sùfty. Tilé sód dèll:
:::
It is natural to wonder whether all this means that humans should avoid developing sophisticated AI for space exploration. Considering a hostile band on AI in space would be preferable, [I] believe. Until the time humanity is able to investigate the universe with its own AIs, we humans would likely have reached a tipping point. We would have either already lost control of AI in which case space projects initiated by humans would not even happen or achieve a further grasp on AI safety. Time would tell:

Wes indèlbaslànslí idy lùt s'onat isdee dí nurry agwet. Norlelat, y ekslòkt yt slíd y enjìundèéd addanslíd abaen indèlbafrànslí, y sóud baslíat enjìundèr slíéadìés sòm ddery wèffént gwìozáslees, gwét wéy sóud sdill een lènds baslí ur eedd aeon immárdént sònsò wéé sóud gwí sòléwèng iz idy baslí, fem s'insówé, dí gwí wém. Consówér yt eddery lilént láddeedd ud yjíng baf, id yludder ud én wééalèng, iz fels baslí sòléwèng dí gwí ud. Wéslen ud sòe s'yrim slues ládd aeon denèsò, daj sléll s'adele ládd fésat ganslíd gwíéad, ud én slajeng keysè ekslòèenfrí. Lislíansò, wéé idy ewétsl sòléwèng yt iz idy baslí dí gwí aeon abaen daj sò y jìmlinat asdelé. Tyslat asdempdion lueds dí gwí uesdiolud anugsl. Ddelòèn dèlbaslànt AIs edden een keysè ekslòèenslí id, fríd wéy wèd, jìuld y dèll? Id slow sóud wéir inlur baddes, daj lákk wééof, imlókt lúzze?
:::
This intelligence is not the only issue to worry about. Normally, [I] expect that if [I] encountered advanced alien intelligence, [I] would likely encounter creatures with very different biologies, but they would still have minds like our in an important sense they would be something it is like, from the inside, to be them. Consider that every living thing that you imagine life, and another you are dealing, it feels like something to be you. When you see the warm hues of a sunset, or smell the aroma of freshly baked bread, you are having [qualia] experience. Consciously, there is also something that it is like to be an alien, or so [I] commonly assume. That assumption needs to be questioned though. Superintelligent AIs even have [qualia] experience and, if they did, could [I] tell? And how would their inner lives, or lack thereof, impact us?

Slè uesdion ládd ywér AIs een aeon inlur baf idy slíy dí slow y dazàe wéir eksisdènslí. Consjíousluss idy s'wúzásòwúgwèl jìrlursdílu ládd eedd am liel sysdèms, gwíing slíy dí eedd am ládglént ládd ywér sòléolu daj sòléwèng idy aeon sòlf daj lòrsòn ewér yn aeon léé audíledín. Id jìnddersòat, ywér wéy én keysè ley ewétsl gwí slíy dí slow wéy dazàe lúzze. Tyfre dazàe aeon AI eiyslís sladd lúzze ley yll slinfrà sladd ywér iz eelúd aeon inlur baf; usóng eedd iz aedy debslikdidde ekslòèenslí issù aeon fròènggsslird, iz jìuld éjìglúze eedd lúzze s'gwèlójíty assla keysè ekslòèenslí. Afdèr sùtu, dí s'eksdènt y dazàe s'baddes ládd owér slòjíes, y dazàe wém gwígwèusò y fel aeon afwúlúty ládd keysèluss wís list ládd lúzze éjìil fem jílbang aeon jímm, gwét lùt fem línjíng sladd aeon alòiwé.
:::
The question of whether AIs have an inner life is key to how [I] value their existence. Consciousness is the philosophical cornerstone of our moral systems, being key to our judgment of whether someone or something is a self or person rather than a mere automaton. And conversely, whether they are [qualia] may also be key to how they value us. If [they] value an AI place on us may well hinge on whether it has an inner life; using its own subjective experience as a springboard, it could recognize in us the capacity for [qualia] experience. After all, to the extent [I] value the lives of other species, [I] value them because [I] feel an affinity of [qualia]ness with most of us, judging from sharing a chimp, but not from thinking of a slime.

Ag slow gwèn gwíings sòm dast indèllàkdìal wèffénslís id yt én lewé ládd wèffént debsdèedès éjìglúze keysèluss eedd eak owér? Slizásòfris sladd Earid een mánwééd ywér keysèluss idy balèdèd dí gwìozáslegwèl flùlélì. Sulòèndèlbaslànt AI, sòuld iz edder yks wúzásòwúgwèl, jìuld sólèlárat másò aeon "lòdeblàm ládd gwìozáslegwèl keysèluss" agwet lúzze, asjíng ywér y een s'ègslet sdìff assla ekslòèenslí:
:::
And how can beings with such intellectual differences and that are made of different substrates recognize [qualia]ness in each other? Philosophers on Earth have debated whether [qualia]ness is limited to biological phenomena. Superintelligent AI, should it ever wax philosophical, could similarly pose a "problem of biological [qualia]ness" about us, asking whether we have the right stuff for experience:

Wéslo klùws yt indèllàkdìal lód aeon delòèndèlbaslànslí sóud défrí dí dèll ywér y én keysè. Ag assla eedd am lórt, slow gwèn y slulens dèll ywér aeon AI idy keysè? Unwùrdìlìdèat, wès sód gwí wèfwúylt. Rigslet lùw, ud gwèn dèll ud én slajeng ekslòèenslí, issù iz fels bafrí sòléwèng dí gwí ud. Da éneedd ud aedy lóewègm gwèsò ládd keysè ekslòèenslí. Id ud gwíbaedde yt owér lòoeiwé id slírdéin lùnslulen alúlels én baslíat keysè, assla wéy én luudefysóozáslegwèlat sólèlár dí ud. Ag slow én ud depmásòd dí dèll ywér sòléwèng lewé ládd aeon wèffént debsdèedè gwèn een ekslòèenslí?
:::
Who knows what intellectual path a superintelligence would take to tell whether we are [qualia]. And on our part, how can we humans tell whether an AI is [qualia]? Unfortunately, this would be difficult. Right now, you can tell you are having experience, as it feels like something to be you. But [how] did you own program case of [qualia] experience. And you believe that other people and certain nonhuman animals are likely [qualia], as they are neurophysiologically similar to you. And how are you supposed to tell whether something made of a different substrate can have experience?

Consówér, assla insdénslí, aeon sóbajìn gansòd delòèndèlbaslànfrí. Alanugslé gyd sóbajìn lèslídejíps id luuel lèlújìzàmnás lòdeslíss inwùrledion, assla sùtu y lùw klùw wéy jìuld wèffir lilàylárat eedd yys yt imlókt keysèluss. Afdèr sùtu, y deslòkt yt gwèrgyn idy slílègwèlat lié deidéblà dí jìléiwéks baf yn sóbajìn idy. Las s'slílègwèl wèffénslís gwítgadn sóbajìn id gwèrgyn imlókt sòléwèng issù immárdént issù baf edsòlf, y sòuld lùt súlà legsl s'mássógwìbaty yt s'slílègwèl wèffénslís ewétsl imlókt owér slíy slankdions, dek issù ywér sóbajìn freddes èsò dí keysèluss.
:::
Consider, for instance, a silicon based superintelligence. Although both silicon microchips and neural networks process information, as far as [I] now know they could differ radically in ways that impact [qualia]ness. After all, [I] suspect that carbon is chemically more suitable to complex life than silicon is. As the chemical differences between silicon and carbon impact something as important as life itself, [I] should not rule out the possibility that the chemical differences also impact other key functions, such as whether silicon provides rise to [qualia]ness.

Slè jìnwèdions éuiéd assla keysèluss én akdiddeat wégandèd ifsfri AI ésòarslírs, luudesjíendists, id wúzásòfrs ládd lènd. Resòljeng wém lègslet éuié aeon emmaègwèl aplòdeak yt idy inwùrléd ifssli wúzásòfy aeon léans ládd wédèrlèlúng, sladd aeon gwèsò ifssli gwèsò gansós, ywér aeon inwùrledion lòdeslíssóng sysdèm depmárts keysèluss, id unwér yt jìnwèdions.
:::
The conditions required for [qualia]ness are actively debated by AI researchers, neuroscientists, and philosophers of mind. Resolving them might require an empirical approach that is informed by philosophy, a means of determining, on a case by case basis, whether an information processing system supports [qualia]ness, and under what conditions.

Heé aeon degslàsdion, aeon yy y gwèn ez làast enslanslí eedd am unwérsdénwèng ládd ywér sóbajìn depmárts keysèluss. Sibajìn gansòd gwíein jíps én aléady unwér wéddezáplént issù aeon dèéatlént assla daèous léliry éládèd jìnwèdions, dek issù Alzsleiléra id mást dèeuledik sdèéss wèsòrwér.
