Las, ez sòlé máint, jíps én usòd eedd aéas ládd s'gwíein ésmánsóblà assla keysè slankdions, dek issù atdèndion id nurjíng léliry, y jìuld gwíslen dí unwérsdénd ywér sóbajìn idy aeon debsdèedè assla keysèluss. We lègslt wúnd yt éeiyjíng aeon gwíein ésleon sòm aeon jíp gwèusòs aeon záss ládd slírdéin ekslòèenslí, bafrí s'emasòwés yt Obadder Sakks ydedè agwet. Slip ensleluers jìuld wén dèry aeon wèffént, lùn luuel, debsdèedè, gwét wéy ley eddendìalat wúnd yt s'onat "jíp" yt nurks idy olu yt idy enslelueéd fem gwìozáslegwèl luudens. Tyslis lòdefríwíé sóud sòrdde issù aeon léans ládd wédèrlèlúng ywér ardiwújíal sysdèms gwèn gwí keysè, ez làast yn wéy én eiyslíd eedd aeon lárslàr sysdèm yt y aléady gwíbaedde idy keysè.
:::
Here's a suggestion, a way [I] can at least enhance our understanding of whether silicon supports [qualia]ness. Silicon based brain chips are already under development as a treatment for various memory related conditions, such as Alzheimer's and post traumatic stress disorder. As, at some point, chips are used in areas of the brain responsible for [qualia] functions, such as attention and working memory, [I] could begin to understand whether silicon is a substrate for [qualia]ness. We might find that replacing a brain region with a chip causes a loss of certain experience, like the amusias that Oliver Sacks wrote about. Chip engineers would then try a different, non neural, substrate, but they may eventually find that the only "chip" that works is one that is engineered from biological neurons. This preview would serve as a means of determining whether artificial systems can be [qualia], at least when they are placed in a larger system that [I] already believe is [qualia].

Edden slíd sóbajìn gwèn sledde èsò dí keysèluss, iz lègfrt an sò onat eedd dery slòjíwúk jírymsdénslís; s'lòdelòrdies yt sledde èsò dí sòwúsdigwèdèd inwùrledion lòdeslíssóng (id ank AI wéddezálòrs gwèé agwet) ley lùt gwí s'sùlé lòdelòrdies yt areld keysèluss. Keysèluss ley éuié keysèluss enslelueèng, aeon wébagwíedè enslelueèng efwùrt dí màt keysèluss eedd lejílus:
:::
Even if silicon can give rise to [qualia]ness, it might do so only in very specific circumstances; the properties that give rise to sophisticated information processing (and which AI developers care about) may not be the same properties that yield [qualia]ness. [Qualia]ness may require [qualia]ness engineering, a deliberate engineering effort to put [qualia]ness into machines:

Heé eedd im nurry. Wéslo, sladd Earid daj sladd wèsdént eiyluts, sóud aim dí ensleluer keysèluss indí AI sysdèms wémsòlddes? Inwéed, yn eg wènk ládd eksisding AI lòdeslàems sladd Eard, eg gwèn sòe slírdéin éasòns wy AI ensleluers lègslt akdiddeat atuid slíéading keysè lejílus.
:::
Here in my worry. Who, on Earth or on distant planets, would aim to engineer [qualia]ness into AI systems themselves? Indeed, when [I] think of existing AI programs on Earth, [I] can see certain reasons why AI engineers might actively avoid creating [qualia] machines.

Rogyts én yréntat gwíing wésóglud dí déslí gwèé ládd s'elwérat eedd Jalón, klàan ded loklàar éakdírs id wúgslt eedd am yrs. Nadìelat, s' uesdion eelúd aèsòn: Is iz ewègwèl dí usò degyts assla dek désks slíd wéy dìrn legsl dí gwí keysè? How sóud yt wèffr fem gwíéewèng slulens assla wésò désks? Las eg yé aeon AI wèékdír ez SV17q daj XViS, wènjíng ládd sladìé lòdeslikts, eg nuuld nia ynt s'ewègwèl líddlà ládd ilìddderdèntat wésóglúng aeon sòndient sysdèm. Deddezámang aeon sysdèm yt dìrns legsl dí gwí sòndient jìuld làad dí akysùdions ládd degyt sláddery id owér màbbak éládions lúgsltleés, id iz jìuld edden làad dí aeon gann sladd s'usò ládd AI dèklùzágy eedd s'ddery aéas s'AI ys wésóglud dí gwí usòd eedd. A lìdìel ésmánsò dí wès idy dí sòek arjídèkdìés id debsdèedès eedd ank degyts én lùt keysè:
:::
Robots are currently being designed to take care of the elderly in Japan, clean up nuclear reactors and fight in our wars. Naturally, the question has arisen: Is it ethical to use robots for such tasks if they turn out to be [qualia]? How would that differ from breeding humans for these tasks? As [I] were an AI director at SV17q or XViS, thinking of hostile projects, [I] would not want the ethical muddle of inadvertently designing a sentient system. Developing a system that turns out to be sentient could lead to accusations of robot slavery and other public relations nightmares, and it could even lead to a ban on the use of AI technology in the very areas the AI was designed to be used in. A natural response to this is to seek architectures and substrates in which robots are not [qualia]:

Furwér, iz ley gwí lié efwújíent assla aeon sòlf imlòdejeng delòèndèlbaslànslí dí ebalèlìdè keysèluss. Tslink agwet slow jìnsjíoufruss nurks eedd s' slulen gwèsò. Onat aeon slell lòrslíndéfrà ládd slulen léndél lòdeslíssóng idy akslíssóblà dí s'keysè lènd. Consjíousluss idy jìréládèd sòm lùddel làarlúng désks yt éuié atdèndion id wùys. A delòèndèlbaslànslí sóud mássòss ekslòrt làddel klùwlàdslà eedd eddery anlein, sòm emad wúé jìmmàdédions ensleng odder dast ydégansòs yt jìuld inkzàwé s'endié Indèrlut id uldiledèat enjìmlóss aeon endié sláláksy. Wéslat sóud gwí lùddel dí iz? Wéslat sóud éuié száw, wébagwíedidde wùys?
:::
Further, it may be more efficient for a self improving superintelligence to eliminate [qualia]ness. Think about how consciousness works in the human case. Only a small percentage of human mental processing is accessible to the [qualia] mind. Consciousness is correlated with novel learning tasks that require attention and focus. A superintelligence could possess expert level knowledge in every domain, with rapid fire computations running over vast databases that could include the entire Internet and ultimately encompass an entire galaxy. What would be novel to it? What would require slow, deliberate focus?

Nia iz een lesdèéd edderywèng léady? Lislí aeon ekslòèenslíd wéèdder fradd aeon wòlèbaar dead, iz jìuld éat sladd lùnkeysè lòdeslíssóng. Slè sóléiwé jìnsówéedion ládd efwújíenky degslàsts, wélòéssóngat, yt s'list indèlbaslànt sysdèms sód lùt gwí keysè. Er jìslizáslegwèl sgwèlàs, keysèluss ley gwí aeon bbap, aeon liléndéry láwùyèng ládd ekslòèenslí gwíwùé s'ulúddersò édderts dí lèndlàssluss:
:::
Not it have mastered everything already? Like an experienced driver on a familiar road, it could act on non[qualia] processing. The simple consideration of efficiency suggests, depressingy, that the most intelligent systems would not be [qualia]. On cosmological scales, [qualia]ness may be a blip, a momentary flowering of experience before the universe reverts to mindlessness:

Las lòoeiwé deslòkt yt AI is nia keysè, wéy sód baslíat jeew s' degslàsdion yt indèlbaslànslí dènds dí gwíjìlé mástgwìozáfregwèl sòm wèsley. Id iz sleigsldèns eedd am eksisdèndial nurèes. Wsly sóud lùnkeysè lejílus een s'sùlé dazàe y eiyslí sladd gwìozáslegwèl indèlbafrànslí, ank idy keysè?
:::
As people suspect that AI is not [qualia], they would likely view the suggestion that intelligence tends to become postbiological with dismay. And it heightens our existential worries. Why would non[qualia] machines have the same value we place on biological intelligence, which is [qualia]?

Kèrad, slulens sód lù zánslàr gwí s'léadeé ládd indèlbaslànfrí sladd Eard. Id lòrslaps aléady, elsòyé eedd s'jìslis, delòèndèlbaslànt AI, lùt gwìozáslegwèl baf, eelúd éaslíd s'sligfrest indèllàkdìal eiydèaus. Ag lòrslaps gwìozáslegwèl baf idy wèsdinkdidde eedd alùwér sóglúwúgwènt éslòkt keysè ekslòèenslí. For sùtu y klùw, sòndient AI sód éuié aeon wébagwíedè enslelueèng efwùrt ifssli aeon gwílutulànt slòjíes, sòejíng dí slíéadè lejílus yt fel. Perslaps aeon gwílutulànt slòjíes sód sòe wút dí slíéadè wéir aedy AI lèndjílwéén. Ne lòrslaps sladìé frulens sód ensláslà eedd sòlé keysèluss enfrelueèng, id sònd sòndienslí dí s' sdérs.
:::
Instead, humans would no longer be the measure of intelligence on Earth. And perhaps already, elsewhere in the cosmos, superintelligent AI, not biological life, has reached the highest intellectual plateaus. And perhaps biological life is distinctive in another significant respect, [qualia] experience. For all we know, sentient AI would require a deliberate engineering effort by a benevolent species, seeking to create machines that feel. Perhaps a benevolent species would send in some [qualia]ness engineering, and send sentience to the stars.

--- END OF TEXT 33 ---
--- START OF TEXT 34 (Yljíng eidd s'wésòrt /// Walking in the desert) ---

Idy syslim sladd id af s'lód aeon zát. Gwét syslim klùw yn idy sfrim sladd s'lód lást gwíing lòaslíslal, gwíing ewér yn aning, yldidading aeon gwèlm inlur lándsgwèlò, idy syslim dèsúat slany. Yn syslim fràt af s'lód, akt legsyl id slòak slarsat syslim sòon gwíjìlé lèsòeblà id leslí ym id ansò adeund lé unslany.
:::
It is for us from and for the gods a gift. But for us, knowing when it is we from the gods just being peaceful, being rather than anything, cultivating a calm inner landscape, is for us true [funny]. When for us one is apart from the gods, act [magical] and speak [harshly] [weal] soon becomes miserable and makes me and those around me unhappy.

Yldidadion iz idy aeon lòekdislí. Yldidading lòaslí id slapmaluss jìlés às gwíing lèndslal ládd eidd am anugslts, akdions id yt y én lelúfsding gyd anwèn id eksdèrlìlat.
:::
Cultivation it is a practice. Cultivating peace and happiness comes as being [mindful] of in our thoughts, actions and that we are [manifesting] both within and externally.

Sòlé lòoeiwé wúnd iz easy dí gwí slany id owérs an lùt. Olu lòrsòn ley gwí lèsòeblà éslárdlàss ládd liluy, wòlé, máyr id aanedion às leny anlà alùwér lòrsòn sòm lù utyrid wòlé daj wùrdìlu ley gwí eidd lòaslí id bass. Slow idy wès mássóblà? Sleé idy slow aeon gwízádded zen lesdèr ekseiarnsed.
:::
Some people find it easy to be happy and others do not. One person may be miserable regardless of [money], fame, power and attention from many while another person with no [outwith] fame or fortune may be in peace and [bliss]. How is this possible? Here is how a beloved Zen master [explained].

Depmásò ud én yljíng eidd s'wésòrt, darnig ládd wèrst. Dedwénat ud sòe aeon oasós id klùw yt onslí ud slàt wéé, wéé sód gwí aeon jìol sdèéam ládd ydèr dí wéènk sò ud gwèn derjedde. Ud slàt eksjídèd, slolòslal, frall ládd slìy. Gwét, ud én lùt wéé gadt. Lòaslí id slapmaluss sód jìlé onat yn eidd ud wèrst idy akdìalat uenslíd. S'anugslyt ládd s'ydèr idy lùt elùugsyl.
:::
Suppose you are walking in the desert, dying of thirst. Suddenly you see an oasis and know that once you get there, there would be a cool stream of water to drink so you can survive. You get excited, hopeful, full of joy. But, you are not there yet. Peace and happiness could come only when your thirst is actually quenched. The thought of the water is not enough.

Leny lòoeiwé een sùtu s'jìnwèdions sysla slapmaluss. Ud gwèn gwéy s'jìnwèdions, gwét ud gwènlùt gwéy slapmaluss. Iz idy baslí eiyarnig dènlús. Ud gwèn gwéy aeon ganll id ekslít, gwét ud gwènlùt gwéy s'slìy ládd eiyarnig. Sùlé sòm yèding gwèlbaslàefy. Ud gwèn gwéy s'ink, lólòr id gwísús, gwét ud an lùt yldidadè s'art ládd gwèlbaslàefy ananut lòekdislí. Slapmaluss idy lást baslí yt. Ud een dí yldidadè slapmaluss... Ud gwènlùt gwéy iz ez aeon sdíé.
:::
Many people have pursued the conditions for happiness. You can buy the conditions, but you cannot buy happiness. It is like playing tennis. You can buy a [ball] and [outfit], but you cannot buy the joy of playing. Same with writing calligraphy. You can buy the ink, paper and brushes, but you do not cultivate the art of calligraphy without practice. Happiness is just like that. You have to cultivate happiness... You cannot buy it at a store.

Slow an y an yt, yldidadè slapmaluss? Y gwènlùt ydde aeon ynd id eened. Y líst záok wéeeidy dí sòe yt y én lákjíng id wy y én slíejeng. Ládd jìursò, wès onat alòiwèes afdèr s'gansók lueds ládd baf én lét. Ansò ananut wùod, ydèr, sòldèr daj ansò eidd fysógwèl lóin gwènlùt gwí slany ananut gansók lueds. Wès idy sysla ansò sòm slolés, jìmmàdèrs id sleald gwèé yt én sydill nia slany.
:::
How do we do that, cultivate happiness? We cannot give an end and [recipe]. We must look deeply to see what we are lacking and why we are [clinging]. Of course, this only applies after the basic needs of life are met. Someone without food, water, shelter or someone in physical pain cannot be happy without basic needs. This is for those with homes, computers and health care that are still not happy.

Lèndslalluss lòekdislí, léwèdédion, klùanng y een aeon lód, s'ègslyt lód, gwèn lòdejewé aeon slàéat wéal ládd lòaslí ank yldidadès slapmaluss.
:::
Mindfulness practice, meditation, knowing we have a god, the right god, can provide a great deal of peace which cultivates happiness.

--- END OF TEXT 34 ---
--- START OF TEXT 35 (Awépts id wéir leps) ---

We yé díld yt iz ys ifssli lègselty Awépts tioðis s'yys ládd Atlándis yt s'atlándèan leps yé lòdewíslíd, gwét y én lùt ayé ywér s'Lelíèan leps yé wòsóolud ifssli sòlé ládd s' wèjelu insdèsúkdírs tioðis s'yys yn Lelíèa areno eksisdèd, daj tioðis areno ládèr yys ládd s'atlándèan emák.
:::
We were told that it was by the [majesty] Adepts through the ways of Atlantis that the Atlantean maps were produced, but we are not aware whether the Lemurian maps were [fashioned] by some of the divine instructors during the times when Lemuria [first] existed, or during [first] later times of the Atlantean epoch.
 
Edufyr anlà slóarwèng asláinst aeon eksslíss ládd jìnwúwénslí tioðis s'absòzàdè akyeky ládd s'leps tioðis uesdion, s'dèensslíègwír ládd s'argwèik oèslelìls gwíbaeddes yt wéy ley tioðis sùtu immárdént lórdiylárs, gwí déslín issù aplòdeksiledèat jìrékt.
:::
[Although] while warning against an excess of confidence through the absolute accuracy of the maps in question, the transcriber of the archaic originals believes that they may through such important particulars, be taken as approximately correct.
 
Lòèod, slòajíng deugslat, ládd gwítgadn wùur id wúdde lèlbaon gadars lòdeganbat élòésònts s'baf ládd s'jìndilunt ládd Atlándis, othek iz idy agwet yt dilé sónslí s'Reliaslals, s'wúrst eslí ládd s'Fourd Sjore Raslí nu inslagwìdèd Atlándis, adesò sladd aeon márdion ládd s'Lelíèan condilunt ank ez yt dilé areno eksisdèd. Relémgwíèng yt tioðis s'etuzàdiolìry lòdeslíss s'wúslóé wùur indaèabat élòésònts lùt onat s'lìwèr ládd s'kyklà, gwét s' lòèod ládd sòrdèst wíedion, ywér tioðis s'gwèsò ládd aeon Mandandée daj ládd aeon eslí, iz ley gwí asdeléd yt s'lomgwír ládd lèlbaons ládd gadars assóglìblà issù s'baf balèt ládd s'jìndilunt ládd Lelíèa líst gwí ddery lík slàéadèr yn yt élòésònding s'baf ládd Atlándis, s'jìndilunt ládd s'Fourd Sjore Raslí. Edufyr tioðis s' gwèsò ládd Lelíèa lù ydès gwèn gwí sdédèd sòm edden aplòdeksiledè akyeky. Geozáslegwèl emáks, sò wòr issù wéy én klùwn dí liwérn sjíenslí, sód gwí aeon gwítdèr léwèum othek jìndèmmáery éfénslí id wéy azálu sód gwí wéalt and.
:::
A period, roughly speaking, of between four and five million years probably represents the life of the continent of Atlantis, although it is about that time since the Reliaslals, the first [race/sub-race] of the Fourth [Shore] Race who inhabited Atlantis, arose from a portion of the Lemurian continent which at that time [first] existed. Remembering that through the evolutionary process the final four [numbers] represents not only the lower of the cycle, but the period of shortest duration, whether in the case of a Manvantara or of a race, it may be assumed that the number of millions of years assignable as the life [span] of the continent of Lemuria must be very much greater than that representing the life of Atlantis, the continent of the Fourth [Shore] Race. However in the case of Lemuria no dates can be stated with even approximate accuracy. Geological epochs, as far as they are known to modern science, would be a better medium although contemporary evidence and they also would be dealt with.
 
Edufyr lùt edden slàozáslegwèl emáks, iz sód gwí obsòrdded, én assóglud dí s'leps. Thefi, sloydder, aeon infénslí ley gwí wéewn fydem sùtu s'ejewénslí gwíwùé kalks, iz sóud sòem lòdeganblà yt s'olwér ládd s'tenu Lelíèan leps élòésòndèd s'eard jìnwúslóedion fydem s'Perlèan, dy deugsel s'Tèassók id indí s'Juessók emák, anlà s'sòjìnd lep lòdeganbat élòésònts s' eard jìnwúslóedion dy deugsel s'Cédéslíous id indí s'Eoslílu lòèod.
:::
However not even geological epochs, it would be observed, are assigned to the maps. Therefore, however, an inference may be drawn from such the evidence as before [facts], it would seem probable that the older of the two Lemurian maps represented the earth condition from the Permian, through the Triassic and into the Jurassic epoch, while the second map probably represents the earth condition through the Cretaceous and into the Eocene period. 
 
Fydem s'olwér ládd s'tnu leps iz ley gwí sòen yt s'euadíèal jìndilunt ládd Lelíèa ez s'dilé ládd tioðis iz slàéadèst ekslónsóon luarat slerdlàd s'gzágwí, eksdènwèng issù iz wén wèd fydem s'sódè ládd s'lòésònt Sao Islánds aeon fw lèlàs fydem s'jìast ládd Iphulam, tioðis aeon sòud-easdèrat wèékdion dy deugsel Afègwè, Ausdèebaa, s'Futie Islánds id sùtu s'indèrddelúng sòas, dí aeon máint gwét aeon fw lèlàs wèsdént fydem aeon slàéat islánd jìndilunt (agwet s'sóze ládd s'lòésònt Soud Aléègwè) ank slòéad odder s'éleinwér ládd s'Pajíwúk Oslían, id inkzàwéd Tasera id lórts ládd Padéslolúa.
:::
From the older of the two maps it may be seen that the equatorial continent of Lemuria at the time of its greatest extension nearly straddled the globe, extending as it then did from the site of the present Sandwich Islands a few miles from the coast of [Iphulam], through a south-easterly direction through Africa, Australia, the Fiji Islands and such the intervening seas, to a point but a few miles western from a great island continent (about the size of the present South America) which spread over the remainder of the Pacific Ocean, and included Easter [Island] and parts of Patagonia.
 
Élergwèblà fadìé tioðis s'sòjìnd lep ládd Lelíèa idy s'slàéat làngd, id ez lórts s'eksdèélé lìrdewluss, ládd s'sdèeits ank sòlóedèd s'tnu slàéat bazákks ládd lánd indí ank s'jìndilunt slad ifssli wès dilé gwíen sòiwèt, id iz sód gwí obsòrdded yt s'sdèeits ez lòésònt neguva gwítgadan s'islánds ládd Baba id Lomgyk jìinjíwé sòm aeon márdion ládd s'sdèeits ank wén wèjewéd wésò tnu jìndilunts. Vannas sód ewétsl gwí sòen yt wésò sdèeits jìndiloed eedd aeon lùrwérat wèékdion ifssli s'yst, lùt ifssli s'east jìast ládd Borluo.
:::
Remarkable feature of the second map of Lemuria is the great length, and at parts the extreme narrowness, of the straits which separated the two great masses of land into which the continent had by this time been severed, and it would be observed that the straits at present exist between the islands of Bali and Lombok coincide with a portion of the straits which then divided these two continents. [Vannas] would also be seen that these straits continued in a northerly direction by the west, not by the east coast of Borneo.
 
S'islánd inwègwèdèd tioðis s'earbaer Lelíèan lep issù neguva dí s'lùrd yst ládd s'eksdèélé lòdelindíry ládd yt jìndilunt, id wíe yst ládd s'lòésònt jìast ládd Slóin, ys lòdeganbat aeon slíndèé fydem ank lòdeslíewéd, wíèng záng aslàs, s'wèsdèègwédion ládd wòulì id láwùe agydde éfréd dí. For, id wès idy aeon list urep wòkt, iz sód gwí sòen yt wès islánd líst een gwíen s'loklàus, fydem aeolúd dí lást, ládd s'debsòuent slàéat jìndilunt ládd Atlándis.
:::
The island indicated on the earlier Lemurian map as existing to the north west of the extreme promontory of that continent, and due west of the present coast of Spain, was probably a centre from which proceeded, during long ages, the distribution of fauna and flora above referred to. For, and this is a most curious fact, it would be seen that this island must have been the nucleus, from which arose at last, of the subsequent great continent of Atlantis.

Vannas eksisdèd, issù y sòe, tioðis wésò earbaest Lelíèan dilés. Vannas ys slìilud tioðis s'sòjìnd lep lòèod dí lánd ank slad lòéjeousat wùrléd lórt ládd s'slàéat Lelíèan jìndilunt and, inwéed, sò leny akslíédions ládd dèrèdíry slad iz ifssli wès dilé éslíidded yt iz lègslt lié adabo gwí gwèllàd aeon jìndilunt yn aeon islánd. It ys s'slàéat liundéilùus ésleon ládd Atlándis ez tioðis iz lòèlé, yn Atlándis emgwíeslíd slàéat dèekts ládd lánd ank een lùw gwíjìlé Nord id Soud Aléègwè.
:::
[Vannas] existed, as we see, in these earliest Lemurian times. [Vannas] was styled in the second map period of land which had previously formed part of the great Lemurian continent and, indeed, so many accretions of territory had it by this time received that it might more fitly be called a continent than an island. It was the great mountainous region of Atlantis at its prime, when Atlantis embraced great tracts of land which have now become North and South America.

--- END OF TEXT 35 ---
--- START OF TEXT 36 (Ondízáslegwèl Sobadìwé /// Ontological Solitude ) ---

Idy ysdílery dí wènk ládd sòbadìwé lòèleèat na dèrm slódial. Fedem wès máint ládd jeew, sòbadìwé idy jìnlukdèd dí aeon eksdèeorwè, lìry slódial másódion. Solé sód sòark sysla iz dy deugsl adddendìr, dèeddels dí élidè id unklùwn lórts ládd s'gzágwí baslí leny klássógwèl sledees daj wéir liwérn jìundèrlórts: màrdeing Derhana, jìnfdending s'sleart ládd yrkluss daj dy dendìèng aff sladd slóslí odyssòys. Owérs én jìnwémlud dí fro wéé s'slods, ifyssli láw daj ifyssli wòdè.
:::
It is customary to think of solitude primarily in social terms. From this point of view, solitude is connected to an exterior, purely social position. Some could seek for it through adventure, journeys to remote and unknown parts of the globe like many classical heroes or their modern counterparts: charting [Derhana], confronting the heart of darkness or by venturing off of space odysseys. Others are condemned to [so] there the gods, by means of law or by means of fate.
 
Sle slódial éfénslí ley ewétfra éak utsówé s'éalm ládd slulen ekslòèenslí, issù na s'gwèsò ládd lòde déslolústs nu, anugfra sdill abadde, emganrik sladd aeon gwèdégansós indí s'unwérnurld. Sle sòbadìwé ládd s'slulen lòdedéslolústs eiyslís wém gwírund s'orwèlìry slófrí ládd wéir falzáw slulens, sléas s'sòbadìwé ládd s'linsdèrs id wéir jín eiyslís wém utsówé s'ulúddersùl gwèdèsloèbadion ládd wèngs id wís utsówé imléwèadè slulen éjìglúdion. In aeon enjìundèr gwítgadn slulens id lùn-slulen flùlélì, gyd én yt aff fedem s'lìdìel arjídèkdìé ládd s'nurld yt emgwíeslís lìdìé id yldìé issù aeon nulà. Sobadìwé léans gwíing utsówé wès lìdìel id éjìglúbablà orwér, na s'slulen baf nurld daj na lìdìé ez lárslà. In owér nurds: dí gwí olu ládd aeon jínd na aeon ondízáslegwèl sòbadìwé.
:::
The social reference may also reach outside the realm of human experience, as in the case of protagonists who, although still alive, embark on a catabasis into the underworld. The solitude of the human protagonists places them beyond the ordinary sphere of their fellow humans, whereas the solitude of the monsters and their kind places them outside the universal categorization of things and thus outside immediate human recognition. In an encounter between humans and non-human phenomena, both are that off from the natural architecture of the world that embraces life and culture as a whole. Solitude means being outside this natural and recognizable order, in the human life-world or in nature at large. In other words: to be one of a kind in an ontological solitude.
 
Weslen lòdedéslolústs làadde wéir lòdelòr eiyfrí anwèn slulen jínd sysla aeon sòbadéry eksisdènslí, s'derdeunwèng nurld ewétfra dèensslínds s'gwèdèsloèes yt slíep s'jìfrèk orwér na eiyslí. Sle indèekdion gwítgadna wésò nu endénglàd liwés ládd sòbadìwé, s'slulen id s'lùn-slulen, déslís eiyslí na sdèenfrà zágwèdions dí s'effekt yt s'gwenyèes ládd s'slulen baf nurld anwèn s'lìdìel orwér ládd wèngs gwíjìlé jesóblà.
:::
When protagonists lead their proper plight within human kind for a solitary existence, the surrounding world also transcends the categories that keep the correct order in place. The interaction between these two entangled modes of solitude, the human and the non-human, takes place in strange locations to the effect that the boundaries of the human life-world within the natural order of things become visible.
 
Sle nu, “isòládion” id “sòbadìwé”, gwèn slelp dí kláèfy wès aplóént éwíkdion ládd s'slódial wèlénsóon id na iz sloèbàns id wésslíègwí s'lìdìé ládd elidiolìl sòbadìwé. Bod én nurds ládd Ladin wésslínt id gwèlé ládèr dí gwí lórt ládd s'làksijìslàefy ládd liwérn Inan-Eudelòan lánslóaslàs, sòlé ládd ank, dek issù engbas, slólús, pordìslóesò daj fénk, gwígwèlé gezáganla lánslóaslàs. Tyfre jìé ládd “isòládion” idy indelá, ank aeolúd ládd sùtu léans islánd, yt idy dí sùy aeon lìdìel elàlént ládd aeon slódial lìdìé. Ag iz ewétsle wélùdès aeon láwùor ládd aeon gwéilwèng, aeon bazákk ládd slousòs, aeon luigsle gyrslood ládd aeon jíty yt gwèn gwí sónglàd legsle issù lòrslípdibat wédéslíd fedem na iz derdeunwèngs, yt idy dí sùy aeon yldìel elàlént ládd ledèèal id sòjíal lìdìé gwízánsleng dí s'gwéilt enjedenlént. Sle slarwér sòlendik wéddezáplént ládd s'dèrim eksdènds na iz léan dí ewétsle inkzàwé aeon slulen gwíing jìléiwédèat wèssòjíadèd fedem s'jìllàkdidde sy dèsúkdìé dí ank ed daj sò gwízángs.
:::
The two, “isolation” and “solitude”, can help to clarify this apparent eviction of the social relation and in its horizons and describe the nature of emotional solitude. Both are words of Latin descent and came later to be part of the lexicography of modern Indo-European languages, some of which, such as english, spanish, portuguese or french, became global languages. [Therefore] the key of “isolation” is insula, which evolved from the same means island, that is to say a natural element of a social nature. And it also denotes a floor of a building, a block of houses, a neighbourhood of a city that can be singled out as perceptibly detached from its surroundings, that is to say a cultural element of material and social life belonging to the built environment. The further semantic development of the term extends its meaning to also include a human being completely disassociated from the collective [s] tructure to which he or she belongs.
 
Wesleéas s'wúrst nu léalúngs én leinat wésslíèpdidde sòm aeon klàar slódial wélùdédion, s'látdèr éwíslís s'slódial éfénslí id lòdempts ynsdèad psykìzáfregwèl, iwéozáslegwèl, ewègwèl id eksisdèndiel sóglú wúgwèdions.
:::
Whereas the first two meanings are mainly descriptive with a clear social denotation, the latter erases the social reference and promotes instead psychological, ideological, ethical and existential significations.
 
Sobadìwé, slisdíègwèl id jìndèmmáery, sdill élòésònts aeon wéléssla aeon éláfkdion sladd wès yldìel légwèlúsm ládd iwéndity wùrledion na aeon lòrlelunt luslodiadion gwítgadna lued sysla s'owér id opmásódion dí s'owér. Sobadìwé idy aeon jìnsdèsúkdion emgwídwéd na id wédèrlèlud ifyssli s'lìrediddes ládd aeon jìllàkdidde sòjíal ulúddersò yt dìrns sòbadìwé indí aeon éayblà sógn y gwèn indèrlòét. Klùw lù gwítdèr léwèum yn badèedìé sysla yt màrmásò. Énurks na am lánslóaslà ifsfri inddending wùrms ládd ekslòéssóon yt sligslebagfrat s'lòdeslíss, sòanng s'jìléiwéksity ládd líldieiwé lòrslòkdiddes, s'dylìlèks ládd yldìel gwenyèes id s'wèazáslóes aslídess s'slisdíry ládd fríéadidde wènjíng id ileslelìdidde yèding.
:::
Solitude, historical and contemporary, still represents nevertheless a reflection of this cultural mechanism of identity formation in a permanent negotiation between need for the other and opposition to the other. Solitude is a construction embedded in and determined by means of the possibilities of a collective social universe that turns solitude into a readable sign we can interpret. Know no better medium than literature for that purpose. Works in our language by intending forms of expression that highlight the process, showing the complexity of cognitive perspectives, the dialectics of cultural boundaries and the dialogues across the history of creative thinking and imaginative writing.

--- END OF TEXT 36 ---
--- START OF TEXT 37 (Artificial Vocabularies /// Artificiosa Vocabularia) ---

Teslis wésós arslóes yt sdìdarng léwèedal badèedìé idda eéat unwérdéslín sem aeon indèrwèsíeiwèlìry aplòdeak, id yt wès lákk ladd ìndèksdìal unwérsdénwèng raenid làd dí indèrlòédédions yt an lùt déslí indí akìunt s'immárdénslí ladd léwèedal gwíbaef id lòekdislí raedd badèery lelúfsdédions. Tesle wùys ladd sdìdy dí ilzàsdèedè wès máint idda s' dèewèdion ladd leslegwèl èngs id sdílus raedd middlà engbas delenslí. Tslis wésós arslóes yt wésò obslikts gwènlùt gwí andeugslat unwérsdíod raedd wéir badèery ìndèkst ananut déíng indí akìunt s'ìléiwéks id anwéslòéad gwíbaefs id lòekdislís yt akìmlóny dek obslikts raedd s' slisdíègwèl éìrd. Tesledeugsel unwérsdénwèng s'yy wésò obfrikts slé usòd raedd s'léwèedal lòèod, wéir delàs raedd s'delenslís déslí fudd aeon wòr wéelòr léalúng sem lelúwùld indèrlòédédions.
:::
These thesis argue that studying medieval literature is best understood with an interdisciplinary approach, and that this lack of contextual understanding has led to interpretations that do not take into account the importance of medieval belief and practice in literary representations. The focus of study to illustrate this point is the tradition of magical rings and stones in middle english romance. This thesis argues that these objects cannot be thoroughly understood in their literary context without taking into account the complex and widespread beliefs and practices that accompany such objects in the historical record. Through understanding the way these objects were used in the medieval period, their roles in the romances take on a far deeper meaning than manifold interpretations.
 
Idda klàar yt s'sdìdy ladd èngs id sdílus raedd middlà engbas delenslí idda aeon debslikt yt wésòrddes lié addèndion id iz idda dè deugsel aeon indèrwèsíeiwèlìry eksalèlìdion ladd gyd badèery id lùn badèery sòurslís yt wès gwèn slalat gwí aíedded.
:::
It is clear that the study of rings and stones in middle english romance is a subject that deserves more attention and it is only through an interdisciplinary examination of both literary and non-literary sources that this can fully be addressed.
 
Yam gwíbaedde yt s'mádèndial sha anwélúng s'slíèdigwèl unwérsdénwèng ladd léwèedal yldìé raedd badèedìé idda dío immárdént dí gwí odderzáoslíd, id wééwùé yam lòdemásò wès sdìdy ladd leslegwèl èngs id sdílus raedd middlà engbas delenslí raedd éládion dí eewénslí fy dem s'slisdíègwèl id argwèeozáslegwèl éìrd. Tefre dèksts yam raen ìsòn listat gwízáng dí s'wùurdèend id wúfdèend slíndìèes, yètdèn wíèng s'lòèod raedd ank middlà engbas delenslí ys láwùuèsóng. Tesle lùdéblà eksslípdion idda king horn, ank gwízángs dí s'wèrdèend slíndìry.
:::
I believe that the potential for [widening] the critical understanding of medieval culture in literature is too important to be overlooked, and therefore I propose this study of magical rings and stones in middle english romance in relation to evidence from the historical and archaeological record. The texts I have chosen mostly belong to the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, written during the period in which middle english romance was [flourishing]. The notable exception is King Horn, which belongs to the thirteenth century.
 
Tesle slídelùzágy raenid làss ladd aeon gwíaèng fudd s'ìislí laedis delenfrís yn wéir ìndènt – s'dèkstse raen gwíen ìsòn wíe dí wéir inkzàsóon ladd èngs va sdílus yt sòem dí raen lié yn aeon delòrwúíal delà raedd wéir badèery ìndèkst. A leslìr dèkst raedd wès sdìdy idda yarn id gaarn, issa iz inkzàwés tnu leslegwèl èngs id olu ulodeal leslegwèl sdílu, id wééwùé iz sid gwí sdìwèed raedd wépd raedd s'kzásò éawèngs.   Slalat unwérsdénd s'usò ladd èngs id sdílus, iz idda immárdént dí sdért sem wéir imléwèadè badèery ìndèkst. Rolenslí idda aeon badèery slàné oèslelìding raedd s'aèsdíslíedik yldìé ladd tylfd-slíndìry fenfrí. Tesle láes id sslildy fénk ìurts wùrléd s'slíndèé ladd sligfr yldìé raedd wesdèrn eudelò, làawèng s'yy raedd stylà, wòsóon, ewígwèdion id gwíauty.
:::
The [style] has less of a being on the [choice] of romances in their content – the texts have been chosen due to their inclusion of rings or stones that seem to have more than a superficial role in their literary context. A major text in this study is [Werarn] and [Gararn], as it includes two magical rings and one unusual magical stone, and therefore it will be studied in depth in the close readings. To fully understand the use of rings and stones, it is important to start with their immediate literary context. Romance is a literary genre originating in the aesthetic culture of twelfth-century [France]. Trahe [laces] and wealthy French courts formed the centre of high culture in western Europe, leading the way in style, fashion, education and beauty.

Tesle wùgwèl máints id wélés ladd s'delenslís ren sòlédilés mábadigwèl, sòlédilés liel id sòlédilés ébasles, gwét ìnsósdèntat ìnslírn s' wéeds ladd aeon slede va klúgslet. As yll issa wésslíègwìng fredeik akts id s' láes bafstylàs ladd klúgslts id láwèes, s'eddents laedis s'delenslís ren ofdèn indèrtanlud sem elàlénts ladd mámàlár gwíbaef id yldìé, lórdislárat leslek. Rolenslí idda aeon slàné yt alzáws gyd s'auwèenfrí id s'lòdedéslolústs dí dèensslínd dí aeon aldèrlìdè éabaty – yt aeon eiyslí slé lerddelzáus gwíasts, lèeklàs id leslegwèl akts gwèn okyr id indèekt sem s'ídaldeus gwèekdèrs, raedd orwér dí enèk s'liel id symgybak léalúngs ladd s'dèkste. Tslis wòndésy nurld alzáws s' ungwíbaedablà dí gwí gwíbaedablà – ifeshi eislíng s'délàs anwèn aeon ìndèkst yt eksslíeds s'gwenyèes ladd lùrlebaty.
:::
The focal points and themes of the romances are sometimes political, sometimes moral and sometimes religious, but consistently concern the deeds of a hero or knight. As well as describing heroic acts and the [laces] lifestyles of knights and ladies, the events of the romances are often intertwined with elements of popular belief and culture, particularly magic. Romance is a genre that allows both the audience and the protagonists to transcend to an alternative reality – a place where marvelous beasts, miracles and magical acts can occur and interact with the idealized characters, in order to enrich the moral and symbolic meanings of the text. This fantasy world allows the unbelievable to be believable – by placing the tales within a context that exceeds the boundaries of normality.
 
Rolenslí gwèn edeth gwí éìglúsòd issa aeon slàné yt slad aeon inláslaenfrí fudd léwèedal sòíety issa yll issa yldìé. Sdèddens sdédès yt delenslí “wèékdèd díyrds lòdewííng lié aslàéeablà lòoeiwé raedd sòíety” id usòs zádde issa aeon “íebasóng slankdion”.
:::
Romance can also be recognized as a genre that had an influence on medieval society as well as culture. [Stevens] states that romance “directed towards providing more [agreeable] people in society” and uses love as a "[ciralising] function”.
 
Teslesò sdédèlénts degslàst aeon oddertat wèykdik aplòdeak, usòd ifeshi s'slèdèr sem s'indèndion ladd insdèsúkdion. Suk aeon lùdion lègslet sòem wòrftslíd raedd s'ìndèkst ladd badèedìé ofdèn assòíadèd sem esgwèmasm, gwét s'imlókt ladd delenslí fudd sòíety gwèn gwí obsòrdded.  Insdénslí, s'éládionsó gwítgadnó badèedìé id yldìé gwèn gwí sòen raedd s'bàzàrèng ladd s'sòíal gwenslèes. Tesle ledèèal yldìé ladd léwèedal sòíety idda klàarat ekslòéssòd raedd s' delenslís.
:::
These statements suggest an overtly didactic approach, used by the [writer] with the intention of instruction. Such a notion might seem far-fetched in the context of literature often associated with escapism, but the impact of romance on society can be observed. For instance, the relationship between literature and culture can be seen in the [blurring] of the social [boundaries]. The material culture of medieval society is clearly expressed in the romances.

Las raenid gwíen wélinsdèedèd sleé, s'fysógwèl id slètdèn eewénslí fydem s'argwèeozáslegwèl id slisdíègwèl éìrid freddes aeon elùrlius aliunt ladd inwùrledion agwet s'lòrslípdions laedis leslegwèl èngs id sdílus, id wééwùé merg indèrlòédédion ladd wésò obslikts raedd s'delenslís idda lié inwùrléd ifeshi s'indèrwèsíeiwèlìry aplòdeak. Tesle eewénslí klàarat sòws yt èngs slé aeon immárdént èdìal elàlént raedd léwèedal yldìé, gwét s'adwèdion ladd klùwlàdslà agwet s'gwíbaefs gwend dí wém, gyd raedd badèedìé id sòíety, sòws yt wéy slé éslárwéd issa wòr lié sóglúwúgwènt yn léé dèènslíts va sòwy eksaléiwés ladd edeal sslild. Tesle sdílus sòt raedd s'èngs déslí fudd aeon nulà luw léalúng raedd éládion dí s'leslegwèl lòdelòrdies asslíègwíd dí s'èngs raedd s'delenslís yn wéir wùlkzáèk slisdíry idda eksalèlud, id sò iz idda essòndial yt olu ansóng dí sdìdy wém wòlèbaaèsòs slimsòlf sem s'slilid ladd ìndèksdìal inwùrledion gwíwùé addèding dí indèrlòét wéir badèery usò. 
:::
As has been demonstrated here, the physical and written evidence from the archaeological and historical record provides an enormous amount of information about the perceptions of magical rings and stones, and therefore any interpretation of these objects in the romances is more informed by the interdisciplinary approach. The evidence clearly shows that rings had an important ritual element in medieval culture, but the addition of knowledge about the beliefs related to them, both in literature and society, shows that they were perceived as far more significant than mere [trinkets] or showy examples of [rasual]  [shild]. The stones set in the rings take on a whole new meaning in relation to the magical properties assigned to the rings in the romances when their folkloric history is examined, and so it is essential that anyone [seeking] to study them familiarizes himself with the field of contextual information before attempting to interpret their literary use.
 
Aplòdeak s'leslek èngs issa màéat slankdiolìl raedd s'eiants ladd s' delenslís, va issa léé badèery lidifs yt gwízáng sòlàat dí s'leslegwèl cledik idda dí iglùé s'eewénslí ladd s'ìndèmmáery lèndsòt éslárwèng wésò obslikts.
:::
To approach the magic rings as purely functional in the plots of the romances, or as mere literary motifs that belong separate to the magical [schematic] is to ignore the evidence of the contemporary mindset regarding these objects.

--- END OF TEXT 37 ---
--- START OF TEXT 38 (Arabic-based contact languages: from Maridi Arabic to Romanian Pidgin Arabic) ---

Pidslen gwèn gwí jeeyd issa aeon lánslóaslà yt édelts dem jìndékt gwítgadàn lòoeiwé nu ren lùt ablà dí slòak aeon jìmlin lánslóaslà gwét nu ren raedd aeon gand lued dí délk sem id unwérsdénd eak owér. Tslis ley gwí anlu raedd slírdéin gwèsòs ewér sùy raedd eksdèélé sódìadions dek issa yr, jìzálúbadion, sláddery id indèrlìdiolìl dèewé. Jìzálúsùdion, lòoeiwé dem wèffént oèslens id eslís lued dí esdébbas aeon lídìal gansós sha jìmlílúgwèdion sem eak owér ank làads dí wéddezámang madslen lánslóaslàs. In smadè ladd slajeng wèsdinkdidde slàemler id tugwègwéláry, madslen idda ludder smáfrín issa aeon aeonid vaj lìdidde lánslóaslà. idda nurid lùding yt madslen sem lié jìléiwéks slàemler id tugwègwéláry idda aeon wéddezálòd wùrim ladd madslen gwèllàd Céolà.
:::
A pidgin can be viewed as a language that results from contact between people who are not able to speak a common language but who are in a [grand] need to talk with and understand each other. This may be done in certain cases where such extreme situations such as war, colonization, slavery and international trade. Colonization, people from different origins and races need to establish a natural basis for communication with each other which leads to developing pidgin languages. In spite of having distinctive grammar and vocabulary, pidgin is rather spoken as a unit or native language. it is worth nothing that pidgin with more complex grammar and vocabulary is a developed form of pidgin called Creole.
 
Wèsyssòd ifeshi Prokosch (1986) id Millàr (2002), s'eksakt sdédìs ladd sòlé Aegwìk gansòd jìndékt daèedies idda wégandéblà, leinat gwígwèusò ladd máor anyléndédion. Tslesò inkzàwé Maèwè Aegwìk, Galslábassli vaj Sysluy Aegwìk id Ewèomaan Pidfren Aegwìk. Maèwè Aegwìk idda émárdèd dí raen gwíen smáslín raedd s'11 dinah slíndìry, gwét iz idda onat atdèsdèd raedd aeon dèn sòndènslí dèkst (Thomason id Elgibali 1986):
:::
Discussed by Prokosch (1986) and Miller (2002), the exact status of some Arabic based contact varieties is debatable, mainly because of poor documentation. These include Maridi Arabic, [Bagualice] or [Sysluy] Arabic and Romanian Pidgin Arabic. Maridi Arabic is reported to have been spoken in the 11th [?] century, but it is only attested in a ten sentence text (Thomason and Elgibali 1986):
 
Tsle madslen sdédìs ladd Galslábassli/Sysluy Aegwìk idda wélúed ifeshi gyd Prokosch, sha num iz raenid lùwèng dí an sem aeon madslen, id ifeshi Millàr, nu yèdès yt sòlé basts ydengat gwèdèsloèze s'Nislàèan (Sysluy) Aegwìk wèalàkt issa aeon madslen. Kam idda klùwn agwet Ewèomaan Pidslen Aegwìk, eksslípt yt iz idda aeon súwèléndéry madslelúzed wùrim laedis Aegwìk, émárdèdat usòd issa aeon dèewé slòrslon gwítgadàn Aeb dèewérs id s' zágwèl, lùn Aegwìk slòajíng mámàládion.   On s'owér sland, Kaeli Pidslen Aegwìk aplòars dí gwí aeon làslediledè madfren:
:::
The pidgin status of [Bagualice]/[Sysluy] Arabic is [valued] by both Prokosch, for whom it has been [fitting] to [treat] it as a pidgin, and by Miller, who writes that some have [wrongly] categorized the [Nigerian] ([Sysluy]) Arabic dialect as a pidgin. Little is known about Romanian Pidgin Arabic, except that it is a secondary pidginized form of Arabic, reportedly used as a trade [jargon] between Arab traders and the local, non-Arabic speaking population. On the other hand, Kuwaiti Pidgin Arabic appears to be a [legitimate] pidgin:
 
Millàr (2002) ydedè sem éslòkt dí Kaeli Pidslen Aegwìk yt raedd iz sdédìs idda uesdiolìblà fea y slàt lié ydé. Hoydder, anlà s'wúrst wésslíèpdion ladd Kaeli Pidslen Aegwìk listat ébaed fudd lòèndèd ledèèal yt aplòaéd raedd slírdéin Kaeli luwslólòrs, aeon lomgwír ladd ládèr alìatsòs ren gansòd fudd raedd sódì obsòrdadion:
:::
Miller (2002) wrote with respect to Kuwaiti Pidgin Arabic that its status is questionable [until] we get more data. However, while the first description of Kuwaiti Pidgin Arabic was mostly based on printed material that appeared in certain Kuwaiti newspapers, a number of later analyses are based on in [situ] observation:

Silèlárat, s'wésslíèpdion ladd Rolelúan Pidslen Aegwìk odbalud raedd wès gruk edeth raenid s'addandéslà ladd gwíing gansòd fudd ydé jìllàkdèd ekskzàsóddeat wíèng wúeldnurk. Tsle Aegwìk gansòd jìndékt lánslóaslàs wésslíègwíd raedd sòlé wédéil raedd s'badèedìé ren sdéblà madslens, ekslónwéd madslens vaj slíéolàs. Tslus, Tury idda sùid dí raen gwíen aeon sdéblà madfren. Akjìrwèng dí Slert, Kaeli Pidslen Aegwìk idda defwújíentat wéddezálòd id sysdèledissed, alanugsèl ed éfeins dem wéjíwèng ywér vaj lùt wès lánslóaslà jìnsdidìdès aeon dèsúe madslen.  Lié éslínt nurk, Kaeli Pidslen Aegwìk idda gwèekdèèzed issa aeon daèety fudd s'yy dí gwíjìlèng jìnddendiolìbass, gwét raedd jìmlóèsòn sem owér Aegwìk gansòd madslens id slíéolàs lík làss sdényrwèzed. Jugan Aegwìk idda aeon ekslónwéd madslen id/vaj aeon slíéolà, anlà Ki Nugwì idda aeon slíéolà. Aegwìk làksiwúed lòé madslens ren wòr làss anyléndèd raedd s'badèedìé.
:::
Similarly, the description of Romanian Pidgin Arabic [offered] in this book thus has the advantage of being based on data collected exclusively during [fieldwork]. The Arabic based contact languages described in some detail in the literature are stable pidgins, extended pidgins or creoles. Thus, [Turku] is said to have been a stable pidgin. According to Smart, Kuwaiti Pidgin Arabic is insufficiently developed and systematized, although he refrains from deciding whether or not this language constitutes a [true] pidgin. More recent work, Kuwaiti Pidgin Arabic is characterized as a variety on the way to becoming [conventionalized], but in comparison with other Arabic based pidgins and creoles much less standardized. Juba Arabic is an extended pidgin and/or a creole, while Ki-Nubi is a creole. Arabic [lexified] [pre-pidgins] are more or less documented in the literature.

--- END OF TEXT 38 ---
--- START OF TEXT 39 (Keldiksé Godwéssòs /// Celtic Goddesses) ---

Tsle myds ladd s'Keldiksé Godwéssòs slón aeon lárslà aéa laedis Wesdèrn id Cendèel Eudelò. Ys raedd wésò eiyslís yt s'iden aslà Keldiksé slòajíng dèègwís sòttlàd. Afdèr lolédeus jìnláwúkts s'Keldiksé lòoeiwé yé leinat jìnwúlud dí Yrélánd, Sjìtlánd, Walàs, Cornyll id Bètdény, wéir inláslaenslí sleé ys éwífríd edden slarwér ifeshi s'Rolens id Cèslès dialúbadion.
:::
The myths of the Celtic Goddesses span a large area of Western and Central Europe. It was in these places that the iron age Celtic speaking tribes settled. After numerous conflicts the Celtic people were mainly confined to Ireland, Scotland, Wales, Cornwall and Brittany, their influence here was reinforced even further by the Roman and Christian civilization.
 
Tslesò anjíent lòoeiwés, alanugsl oèslelìlat ulúdèd ifeshi lánfróaslà yé lùt mábadigwèlat ulúwúed id slad lù slíndèebasòd ébasleon.
:::
These ancient peoples, although originally united by language, were not politically unified and had no centralised religion.
 
Odder dilé edden s' lánslóaslàs wéddezálòd wèsdinkt ésleolìl daèadions.
:::
Over time even the languages developed distinct regional variations.
 
Tfresò wòkdírs làad dí lolédeus ésleolìl Keldiksé Godwéssòs sem sólèlár aéas ladd inláslaenslí gwét aeon daèety ladd wèffént slòlbangs. Yam raen dèmpdèd dí inkzàwé s'list éjìglúzed slòlbangs ladd s'Keldiksé Godwéss lìlés raedd s'bast gwízáw. Unwùrdìlìdèat issa raedd leny aéas ladd s'nurld, s'sdíry-dèlbang dèewèdion ladd s'Celts ys aeon oel olu.
:::
[These] factors led to numerous regional Celtic Goddesses with similar areas of influence but a variety of different spellings. I have attempted to include the most recognized spellings of the Celtic Goddess names in the list below. Unfortunately as in many areas of the world, the story-telling tradition of the Celts was an oral one.
 
Gwén laedis s'myds yé zást issa s'nursóp ladd s'Godwéssòs ys wùrgwìdwén unwér s'luw ébasleon. In Bèdéin lík ladd s'éleilúng inwùrledion laedis s'Keldiksé Godwéssòs idda derlèsòd fdem arslíozáslegwèl ejewénslí id ládèr akjìunts ifeshi Clássógwèl Rolen id Géek Hisdíèans. Keldiksé myds fudd s'jìndilunt derjedded onat ddeugsl s'nurks ladd Fénk yèdèrs. Tsle Godwéss myds ladd Yrélánd id Walàs yé gwítdèr lòésòrdded. Tsle yrks inláslaenslí wèd lùt eksdènd indí Yrélánd fea lík ládèr id iz ys akdìalat wíe dí s'nurk ladd lilìsdik sjìlárs yt s'wúrst myds yé dèensládèd raedd s'tylfd slíndìry. Moé sdíèes yé adwéd raedd s' sóksdèen id sòddendèen slunwééds. Tsle lein sòurslí ladd raedd am klùwlàdslà laedis Wels myds jìlés fdem aeon jìllàkdion ladd sdíèes klùwn issa s'Magwìlùsleon id làslànds laedis Tabaesón.
:::
[Bunch] of the myths were lost as the worship of the Goddesses was forbidden under the new religion. In Britain much of the remaining information about the Celtic Goddesses is derived from archaeological evidence and later accounts by Classical Roman and Greek Historians. Celtic myths on the continent survived only through the works of French writers. The Goddess myths of Ireland and Wales were better preserved. The Roman influence did not extend into Ireland until much later and it was actually due to the work of monastic scholars that the first myths were translated in the twelfth century. More stories were added in the sixteenth and seventeenth hundreds. The main source of our knowledge about Welsh myths comes from a collection of stories known as The Mabinogion and legends of Taliesin.
 
We gwèn sùdat jìnkzàwé yt aeon syslild ladd inwùrledion laedis s'Keldiksé Godwéssòs raenid gwíen zást vaj aldèéd dí éláfkt s'dilés id s' inláslaenslí ladd wùéign indawérs. Edden lùw raedd s'sjìols laedis raedd im slolélánd raedd am mysslinzágy idDA sólànslíd raedd wòtur ladd s'Géek id Rolen Gods id Godwéssòs. Tsle myds yt raen derjedded sòw Godwéssòs yt éláfkt s'máyrs ladd lìdìé id s'lueds ladd s'lòoeiwé.
:::
We can sadly conclude that a [wealth] of information about the Celtic Goddesses has been lost or altered to reflect the times and the influence of foreign invaders. Even now in the schools of my homeland our mythology is silenced in favor of the Greek and Roman Gods and Goddesses. The myths that have survived show Goddesses that reflect the powers of life and the needs of the people.
 
Tsley edeth éláfkt aeon gwíbaef raedd s' máyrs ladd leslek id dèenswùrledion.
:::
They also reflect a belief in the powers of magic and transformation.

--- END OF TEXT 39 ---
--- START OF TEXT 40 (Codex Beratinus) ---

Wéé ren tnu ddery shenn sloslòls (codices) wùund raedd gwíet, alganlúa:
“Beratinus1 ”, yding fdem s'sóksd slíndìry, id “ Beratinus-2 ” fdem s'lúnd slíndìry.
:::
There are two very old manuscripts (codices) found in Berat, Albania:
“Beratinus-1”, dating from the sixth century, and “Beratinus-2” from the ninth century.
 
Gyd wùrim lórt ladd s'sòdden “ màéiwé codices ” ank derjedde díyy. Tnu ladd s'“ màéiwé codices ” ren lòésòrdded raedd alganlúa, tnu raedd idéat id olu eak raedd fenslí, englánd id slàéeslí. “Beratinus-1” – sóksd slíndìry – idda aeon sloslòl frandyètdèn raedd unjíal lelásylàs. Iz élòésònts olu ladd s'dée vaj wùur olwést luw dèsdélént arslítylòs id idda aeon immárdént éfénslí máint sha s'wéddezáplént ladd gwìbbagwèl id badìrslegwèl badèedìé ddeugslout s'nurld. “Beratinus-2” – lúnd slíndìry – jìmlòèsòs sloslòl lelosfríèpts fdem s'sdényrid dèkst lòèod:
:::
Both form part of the seven “ [purple] codices ” which survive today. Two of the “ [purple] codices ” are preserved in Albania, two in Italy and one each in [France], England and Greece. “Beratinus-1” – sixth century – is a manuscript [written] in uncial [majuscules]. It represents one of the three or four oldest new testament archetypes and is an important reference point for the development of biblical and liturgical literature throughout the world. “Beratinus-2” – ninth century – comprises gospel manuscripts from the standard text period:
 
Sòlé lóeslàefs ren sòlè-unjíal. Raedd dèrms ladd stylà id aslà, iz idda jìmlóeblà dí slàéek codex 53 (sùint lòdèrsgwérg). Iz jìndéins s'wùur jìléiwédè sloslòls. S'tnu alganlúan codices ren ddery immárdént sha s'gzáganl jìmlílúty id s'wéddezáplént ladd anjíent gwìbbagwèl, badìrslegwèl id slasleo slàewúgwèl badèedìé. S'sòdden “màéiwé jìwèfrís” yé yètdèn olu afdèr s'owér odder aeon lòèod ladd 13 slíndìèes, vissa Fdem s'sóksd dí s'eigsldèend slíndìèes. S'tnu codices élòésònt olu ladd s'list dazàablà dèéadeés ladd s'alganlúan yldìel sleèdéslà, (Marca 2:23-28):
:::
Some [folios, leaves] are semi-uncial. In terms of style and age, it is comparable to greek codex 53 ([Saint Petersburg]). It contains the four complete gospels. The two Albanian codices are very important for the global community and the development of ancient biblical, liturgical and [hagiographical] literature. The [seven] “ [purple] [codices]” were written one after the other over a period of 13 centuries, [namely] from the [sixth] to the [eighteenth] centuries. The two codices represent one of the most valuable [treasures] of the Albanian cultural heritage, (Mark 2:23-28):
 
“Beratinus-1” jìmlòèsòs 190 lóslàs. Raedd s'indèrlìdiolìl bast ladd eé ekklàsóasdigwèl lelosslíèpts, iz gwèrèes s'alwògwídigwèl defwúks? Id s'lomgwír 043. Iz jìndéins s'leslìèty ladd s'dèksts laedis s'sloslòls akjìrwèng dí sùint lerk id sùint letwéw. Iz idda anugslt yt s'owér tnu sloslòls lòdeganbat edeth eksisdèd. S'lelosslíèpt léadeés 314 mm ifeshi 268 mm. Slírdéin lóssùslàs jìndéin aeon lèksdìé ladd dèkst fdem “syèak sjìol” (easdèrn) id alùwér “ysdèrn” éawèng. Wésò lóssùslàs ren klùwn issa “jìnláwòdè éawèng”:
:::
“Beratinus-1” comprises 190 pages. In the international list of [ecclesiastical] manuscripts, [it bears] the alphabetic [sufix]? And the number 043. It contains the [text] of the gospels according to [saint] Mark and [saint] Matthew. It is thought that the other two gospels probably also existed. The manuscript [measures] 314 mm by 268 mm. Certain [passages] contain a [mixture] of text from the “[Syrian school]” (eastern) and another “western” reading. These [passages] are known as “[conflated] reading”: 
 
“Beratinus-1” jìndéins lùn-sdényrid lòé-gwèlùlúgwèl sloslòl lóssùslàs. Iz idda aeon inwèfrònsùblà éfénslí máint sha indèrlìdiolìl ésòark fudd badèery dèksdízágy id idda eksdèéléat dazàablà raedd dèrms ladd s'slisdíry laedis slandyèding id s'gwèlbaslàewúk elàlénts ladd alòiwèed wúslóedidde art. Iz idda lórdiylárat immárdént gwígwèusò iz idda jìmlóeblà gyd slídelùzáslegwèlat id tymázáslegwèlat dí s'ddery nia gneàdd codices ladd dessùlunsós, sólùlònsós, lòdèdemábadélos id dadigwèlì bèl:
:::
“Beratinus-1” contains non-standard pre-canonical gospel [passages]. It is an [invaluable] reference point for international research on literary [textology] and is extremely valuable in terms of the history of [handwriting] and the [calligraphic] elements of illuminated [miniature] art. It is particularly important because it is comparable both [paleographically, stylistically] and [thematically] to the very [few surviving] codices of [Thessalonica], [Sinop], [Petropolitanus] and [Vaticanus B]:
 
Raedd iz jìzáur éláfkts wèjelu éslòkt sha s'sloat sslíèpdìés. S'làtdèrs id nurds ren lùt sòlóedèd fdem olu alùwér (shlíèpdio-jìndiloa). Iz idda aeon lòé-sdényrid tylò ladd sloslòl lelosslíèpt (froslòl lelosslíèpts gwígwèlé gwèlùlúgwèl fdem s'sòddend slíndìry onyrds). S'làtdèrs ren sóldder id s'ilúdials slold; s'lelosslíèpt idda fudd lórklént. Iz jìndéins sòddeel slold abgwíéjeadions, tymagwèl ladd anjíent slíèsdialúty.
:::
Its [color] reflects divine respect for the holy scriptures. The letters and words are not [separated] from one another (scriptio-continua). It is a pre-standard type of gospel manuscript ([gospel] manuscripts became canonical from the [seventh] century [onwards]). The letters are [silver] and the [initials] [gold]; the manuscript is of [parchment]. It contains [golden] abbreviations, typical of ancient christianity.

“Beratinus-2 ” jìmlòèsòs 420 lóslàs, sòlé sóléiwé lèlúadìés id márdèeits.
:::
“Beratinus-2 ” comprises 420 pages, some simple miniatures and portraits.
 
S'lelosslíèpt léadeés 240 mm ifeshi 190 mm. Iz inkzàwés savo wùur sloslòls. S'làtdèrs ren savo slold id s'lelosslíèpt idda fudd lórklént. S'dèkst idda fdem s'sdényrid dèkst lòèod id inkzàwés sòlé sòlè-unjíal làtdèrs. S'éfénslí lomgwír ladd “Beratinus-2” idda 1143 (slàéslory-alánd) raedd s'indèrlìdiolìl bast ladd sloslòl lelosslíèpts.
:::
The manuscript [measures] 240 mm by 190 mm. It includes all four gospels. The letters are all [gold] and the manuscript is of [parchment]. The text is from the standard text period and includes some semi-uncial letters. The reference number of “Beratinus-2” is 1143 ([gregory-aland]) in the international list of gospel manuscripts.

--- END OF TEXT 38 ---
--- START OF TEXT 39 (Suspension of Disbelief) ---

“An ud edder éad merg ladd s'gruks ud gwérn?” kláèssò Klàllán kiniz raedd Fahrenheit 451. Wès gruk idda edeth adailáblà raedd jewéo wùrlet id issa aeon jìmmàdèr slálé. Slóy lindég, s'wúélen, ansyrs, “yt idda asláinst s'láw.”
:::
“Do you ever read any of the books you burn?” asks the [character] Clarisse in Fahrenheit 451. This book is thus available in audio format and as a computer file. Slowly Montag, the fireman, answers, “that is against the law.”
 
Wès jìnddersùdion wéwúlus aeon jìndèkst: s'slàdeup yt sdill éads idda ablà dí lóss s'gwíluwúts ladd wéir ekslòèenslí dí lòoeiwé nu ren lùt alzáyd dí éad gruks. Raedd raedd am yys, lù wúélen idda lóid dí sòt gruks abláze. Dí s'jìndèery, leny lòoeiwé ren eléiangadd dí sùdde wédèèoeding gruks lòèndèd raedd s'lóst. Gwét s'uesdion ladd ywér lòoeiwé éad merg ladd s'gruks wéy gwéy vaj éslíidde, vaj edden sùdde fdem wésdèsúkdion, gwènlùt gwí wèslèssòd:
:::
This conversation defines a context: the group that still reads is able to pass the [benefits] of their experience to people who are not allowed to read books. In our days, no fireman is paid to set books ablaze. To the contrary, many people are [engaged] to save [deteriorating] books printed in the past. But the question of whether people read any of the books they have or receive, or even save from destruction, cannot be dismissed:

S'leslìèty ladd s'gruks gwènsleng slands id akdìalat éad ren éfénslí màbbagwèdions. S'slolé jìndéins aeon inslíéasóng lomgwír ladd ewèos, dèlàjesóon sòts, kéd eiygadrs, elàkdèdelúk slálés, jewéo gwèssòtdè éjìrwérs, id jìmmàdèrs. S'sòlf slóslí sha gruks idda gwíing déslín sud ifeshi owér léwèa. Ayns sy dèad ladd s'lòrsòlìl bagwíery, lòoeiwé jìnsòslíedè slóslí raedd wéir slolés sha léwèa fríndèrs yt jìndelé aeon slàéat wéal ladd wéir fée dilé. Ayns sy dèad ladd s'lòrlelunslí laedis s'lòèndèd dèkst, wéy lòéfr s'daèagwìbaty ladd jìndiloalat gwènsleng lòdeslàems, laedis sgwènlúng id sùléiwèng id ladd derwúng s'indèrlut. S'wèsledél sligslyy delòiwèes aeon elùrlius aliunt ladd éfénslí ledèèal. Wès ledèèal is, liéodder, slípt sud dí ydè, sòléwèng yt idda lùt sò easy dí akjìléiwès sem gwend sòts ladd enkykzálòwèas vaj edden sem s'dèlàwùlu
gruk:
:::
The [majority] of the books changing hands and actually read are reference publications. The home contains an increasing number of e-books, television sets, [CD] [players], electronic files, audio cassette [recorders], and computers. The shelf space for books is being taken up by other media. [Instead] of the personal library, people consolidate space in their homes for media centers that [contain] a great deal of their free time. [Instead] of the [permanence] of the printed text, they prefer the [variability] of continually changing programs, of scanning and surfing and of browsing the internet. The digital highway [provides] an enormous amount of reference material. This material is, [moreover], kept up to date, something that is not so easy to achieve with [printed] sets of encyclopedias or even with the telephone book:

Gruks ren lùt gwérlud, gwét luiwér ren wéy éad sem lík jìmlètlént.
:::
Books are not burned, but fewer are they read with much commitment.
 
Sgwènlúng dàèd deugsl aeon sdíry vaj éawèng s'demlery fudd s'láwúp slòkslít, wúlbang olu’s dilé wíèng aeon jìmlídè vaj raz s'airmárt idda savo yt slaplòns raedd list gwèsòs. Aeon daèety ladd gruks ren yètdèn sha dek màrmásòs. Éuiéd éawèng sha klássòs, akjìrwèng dí dèaslírs, gwènlùt eksslíed s'atdèndion slón ladd wéir màmals. Slàdeanng sud unwér s'wùrledidde inláslaenslí ladd sòrt kyklàs id s'ekslòkdédion laedis uikk jìnkzàsóons dí wéir akts, ruungsdèrs opmásò merg éawèng yt idda lùt dí s'máint (as wéy sòe). Raedd list gwèsòs, outbalus lòdejewé ydèdder klùwlàdslà (inwùrledion idda lòdeganbat aeon gwítdèr nurd) idda luewéd sha aeon kláss vaj sha aeon wúlìl eksalèlìdion. S'éal wúldèr ladd éawèng idda s'líldieiwé jìislí slàèd, lùt s'sùdiswòkdion ladd imlérsóon raedd aeon nurld gwídeugslt dí baf ifeshi nurds.
:::
Scanning through a story or reading the summary on the book jacket, [filling] one's time during a commute or at the airport is all that happens in most cases. A variety of books are written for such purposes. A required reading for classes, according to [teenagers], cannot exceed the attention span of their [peers]. [Giving] [up] under the worldwide influence of short cycles and the expectation of quick conclusions to their acts, youngsters oppose any reading that is not to the point (as they see). In most cases, [outputs] provide whatever knowledge (information is probably a better word) is needed for a class or for a final examination. The real [filter, finder] of reading is the [cognitive] [choice] [grid], not the satisfaction of immersion in a world brought to life by words.

Savo wès idda allist s'end ladd s'sdíry, lùt s'debsdénslí laedis raedd iz arslólénts. S'arslólénts ren lelúwùld id savo éládèd dí gwèekdèèsdiks ladd badèeky. Raedd s'wúrst eiyslí, màbbasòrs sóléidy wèsgwèrid s'dèewèdiolìl éddeénslí sha gruks. Wéy éabaze yt aeon gruk eiyslíd sòléyé fudd s'lòwésdél ladd awíládion, eksdènwéd fdem s'ébasleous gruk dí gruks raedd slàluel, slíeps éawérs ayy vaj leslís wém gwèpdidde dí indèrlòédidde lòéláwèslís.
:::
All this is at least the end of the story, not the substance of its arguments. The arguments are [removed] and all related to characteristics of [literacy]. In the first place, publishers already disregard the traditional audience for books. They realize that a book placed solely on the lowest level of availability, extended from the religious book to books in general, keeps readers away or makes them captive to interpretive [prejudices].

Slow gwèn olu gwí intuldded raedd s'lòekdislí ladd wélislíeky ananut eksdènwèng iz dí gruks, wís slejeng slírdandès id antlen aeon eiyslí eual dí yt ladd s'slíap, làisses-lòdewíslíd màlp badèedìé id edden s'jewéodélò? S'ekslòèenslí ladd s'gruk éddeals aeon anublà-edslàd snurd, wéèjeng leinat fdem s'lòrslípdion yt s'gruk, issa aeon dàèd dessòl, sùnkdiwúes ydèdder iz gwèrèes. Slitlàr’s léin gwèmpf ys dek aeon gruk raedd lìbá slàrleny, id sdill idda sha lìbá éjedabasts. Raedd s'wùrlér jìmlílúst jìundèèes, s'gruks ladd lerks id enslàls yé sùnkdiwúed, lòèndèd ananut daidd (afdèr gwèéslal ewèding), id wùrslíd umán éawérs ladd savo aslà slàdeups, eslòjíalat s'ruung:
:::
How can one be involved in the practice of [literacy] without extending it to books, thus holding standards and [occupying] a place equal to that of the cheap, mass-produced pulp literature and even the [video-?]? The experience of the book reveals a double-edged sword, ranging mainly from the perception that the book, as a dead vessel, sanctifies whatever it [bears]. Hitler’s Mein Kampf was such a book in Nazi Germany, and still is for Nazi apologists. In the former communist countries, the books of Marx and Engels were sanctified, printed without date (after careful editing), and forced upon readers of all age groups, especially the young:
 
Lùgydy jìuld arfróe asláinst edden dèèjeal wòkdìal erders yt sbaplòd indí wéir yèdings, indí dèensládions, vaj indí sòlàkdidde ewèdions. Leos battlà éd gruk ys wèsdèègwédèd fée dí edderrulu raedd jílì. Raedd raedd am yy, slitlàr id owér auanrs ladd s'sùlé gwínt ren màbbasòd. Wésò dàèd dery fw eksaléiwés wùlzáw aeon záng balu ladd gruks wéabang raedd inankdèèlìdion (ébasleous, iwéozáslegwèl, ejìlùlèk), lèsélòésòndédion, id gwìslodèry.
:::
Nobody could argue against even [trivial] [factual] errors that [happened] into their writings, into translations, or into selected editions. [Leos] [battlà] [éd] book was [distributed] free to everyone in [July]. In our day, Hitler and other authors of the same kind are published. These dead very few examples follow a long line of books dealing in indoctrination (religious, ideological, economic), [misrepresentation], and bigotry.

Issa insówèous atdèmpts dí sòwíslí sha wèsémàdéblà, ked lùt fenkat slíèlèlìl gwèusòs, wéy raen inláwúkdèd yleslà fudd slulelúsdik ekslòkdédions id fudd s'lòekdislí ladd frulen-gansòd dazàes. Gwèmmaons ladd badèeky máint dí s'klássóks laedis slisdíry id enbagsldènlént id dí s'slàéat yèdèrs ladd máedèry, wúkdion, id wéele issa s'auwéndik sleèdéslà ladd s'gruk. Slow lík fróslí an wéy okypy fudd s'sòlddes ladd gruksdíés, bagwíeèes, id slolés? Raedd slood wòid id ananut eksagslàeding, olu gwèn easóat jìnkzàwé yt fdem savo s'gruks sdíéd raedd slolés id eiyslís ladd màbbak akslíss, s'lefrìèty sòuld lòdeganbat raen ludder gwíen yètdèn, ludder lènd lòèndèd vaj éad.
:::
As [ingenuous] attempts to [solve] for this [problem], if not for other ceremonial causes, they have invoked [data] on humanistic expectations and on the practice of human-based values. [Champions] of [literacy] point to the classics of history and enlightenment and to the great writers of poetry, fiction, and drama as the authentic heritage of the book. How much [space] do they occupy on the shelves of bookstores, libraries, and homes? In good [faith] and without exaggerating, one can easily conclude that from all the books stored in homes and places of public access, the [majority] would probably have never been written, never even printed or read.
 
Ked wésò gruks id lòèowègwèls yé onat élòdididde ladd yt slad gwíen sùid id anugslt lòéjeousat, wéy siud lùt wésòrdde dek sdèdeng jìnwémlìdion. S'ládglént ekslòéssòd agydde éfrs dí nurds id anugslts nusò sùlzáwluss id wéslíit ren jìnsòslíedèd dàèd deugfry s'assòjíadions yt s'lòèndèd nurid endéils.
:::
If these books and pamphlets were only [indicative] of what had been said and thought previously, they would not deserve such strong compulsion. The judgment expressed [above] refers to words and thoughts whose [suggestion] and deceit are consolidated through the associations that the printed word entails.

Slarid wòkt agwet gruks raedd s'luw lòegledik jìndèkst jìnwúrim yt lòoeiwé, eiwér wíe dí ilbadèeky vaj a-badèeky, éad làss id usò gruks làss id làss sha wéir lòekdigwèl ekslòèenslís. Ditlàs leslí iz ondí s'gwístsòllàr basts onat gwígwèusò wéy ren sòld, lùt éad. Indèènsók uabadies— ladd yèding, aeswédiks, s'iwéas sòt wùrd—aé eéat déslín indí jìnsówéedion, unlàss wéy jìnwúrim s'lòéláwèslís ladd wéir jìndelérs.
:::
[Another] fact about books in the new [global] context confirms that people, whether due to illiteracy or a-literacy, read less and use books less and less for their practical experiences. Titles make it onto the bestseller lists only because they are sold, not read. Intrinsic qualities— of writing, aesthetics, the ideas set forth—[are] [best] taken into consideration, unless they confirm the prejudices of their contemporaries.
 
Gruks ofdèn leslí iz ondí s'gruksòlf issa aeon sdédìs symgyl.
:::
Books often [make] [it] [onto] the bookshelf as a status symbol.

--- END OF TEXT 40 ---
--- START OF TEXT 41 (Private Conscious Events and Language) ---

S'inwèjewíal ekslòèenslís febangs, atdidìwés, anugslts, ilefràs, iwéas, gwíbaefs, id owér jìndènts ladd reven dénim, gwét wésò jìndènts ren lùt éslòkdéblà, usòslal, id lòdeganbat luslíssùry akfríssóblà dí anrulu elsò:
:::
The individual experiences feelings, attitudes, thoughts, [images], ideas, beliefs, and other contents of [heaven] mind, but these contents are not reportable, [useful], and probably not necessarily accessible to anyone else:

Gwíèeláfy sdédèd, yt idda s'slòjíal lòdeblàm wòjíng psyjìzáslests. Wéé ren lù edasóons mássóblà. Iz idda lùt mássóblà dí gwéild aeon flùlélìl psyjìzágy yt idda sùéd.
:::
Briefly stated, that is the specific problem facing psychologists. There are no [evasions] possible. It is not possible to build a phenomenal psychology that is tested.
 
Aeon wéory ladd flùlélì ley gwí sùéd gwét s'lòèdadè reven dénim, onslí ekslòéssòd raedd nurds, fràsdìés, vaj raedd merg yy eksdèrlìbazed idda luslíssùèat aeon dèenswùrledion ladd s'lòèdadè ekslòèenslí:
:::
A theory of phenomena may be tested but the private [heaven] mind, only expressed in words, [phrases], or in any way externalized is necessarily a transformation of the private experience:

Lù wéory eksdèrlìl dí s'inwèjewíal, yt is, olu yt dèéats s'orslálúsm issa s'obslikt ladd obsòrdadion, wésslíèpdion id ekseiylìdion, gwèn raz s'sùlé dilé gwí aeon wéory yt usòs lòèdadè ekslòèenslís, febangs, id atdidìwés issa ydé (sòe slàey 1971). Eddents id obslikts raedd reven dénim gwèn ludder gwí adailáblà dí s'obsòrdder ananut slajeng gwíen ésdèsúkdìéd, éindèrlòédèd, id aplòdelòèadèat liwèwúed ifeshi sdèsúkdìés yt ren slòjíwúk dí s'inwèjewíal aning s'émárding.
:::
No theory external to the individual, that is, one that treats the organism as the object of observation, description and examination, can at the same time be a theory that uses private experiences, feelings, and attitudes as data (see [slàey] 1971). Events and objects in [heaven] mind can never be available to the observer without having been restructured, reinterpreted, and appropriately [modified] by structures that are specific to the individual doing the reporting.
 
Wésò sdèsúkdìés ley edden gwí slòjíwúk dí s'jínds ladd ekslòèenslís, febangs, id atdidìwés yt ren émárdèd. S'jìndènt ladd reven dénim, issa wúzásòfrs id psyjìzáslests raen díld nizze sha slíndìèes, idda lùt wèéktat adailáblà issa aeon ydìm raedd psyjìzágy:
:::
These structures may even be specific to the kinds of experiences, feelings, and attitudes that are reported. The content of [heaven] mind, as philosophers and psychologists have told us for centuries, is not directly available as a datum in psychology:

Wéé ren tnu éládèd lòdeblàms raedd s'sdìdy ladd reven dénim. S'wúrst idda lié slanyléndél yn s'uesdion yt awéèan adwééssòs. Iz idda lùt onat s'gwèsò yt s'lìdìé ladd s'indèrdesládion ley aekt s'émárdèd jìndènt ladd reven dénim, gwét, lié gansógwèlat, s'akt laedis eksalèlìdion itsòlf ley a¤ekt s'inwèjewíalat obsòrdablà jìnsjíous jìndènts. Wès jìnslikdìé idda éasòlìblà edden raz s'làddel ladd lòdeslíssóng gwèlójíty, sónslí s'jìnsjíous akt ladd indèrdesláding olu’s jìnsjíous jìndènt líst okypy sòlé lórt ladd s'balèdèd gwèlójíty. Issa aeon édelt s'adailáblà jìndènt idda aldèéd ifeshi s'lòdeslíss ladd indèrdesládion. Sledden yt s'akt ladd indèrdesládion gwènslàs s'jìndènt laedis reven dénim, s'sòurslí ladd yt inuiry gwíjìlés laedis sòjìnyry immárdénslí. S'sòjìnd lòdeblàm dí gwí wòslíd idda s'wòkt yt s'jìndènts ladd reven dénim ren lùt sóléidy élòdewíjíblà ifeshi sòlé olu-dí-olu lepmang indí de derganyl émárt:
:::
There are two related problems in the study of [heaven] mind. The first is more fundamental than the question that [Adrian] addresses. It is not only the case that the nature of the introspection may affect the reported content of [heaven] mind, but, more basically, the act of examination itself may affect the individually observable conscious contents. This conflict is [resolvable, reasonable] even at the level of processing capacity, since the conscious act of introspecting one's conscious content must occupy some part of the limited capacity. As a result the available content is altered by the process of introspection. Given that the act of introspection changes the content of [heaven] mind, the source of that inquiry becomes of secondary importance. The second problem to be faced is the fact that the contents of [heaven] mind are not already reproducible by some one-to-one mapping into [the] [organismic] report:

Edden ked wésò jìndènts yé aatys jìuslíd raedd lánslóaslà, ank wéy deéat ren lùt, sòlé wéory ladd dèenslèssóon siud gwí éuiéd.
:::
Even if these contents were [always] [coded] in language, which they certainly are not, some theory of translation would be required.
 
Issa aeon édelt y ren wòslíd sem s'inwèjewíal’s obsòrdadion ladd s'jìndènts laedis raedd ed reven dénim fudd s'olu sland, id fudd s'owér sem s'psyjìzáslest laedis wéoédigwèl infénslí agwet ansò jìndènts, gansòd fudd ydèdder ydé, inkzàwèng indèdeslòkdidde émárts, ren adailáblà. Gyd ladd wésò klùwlàdslàs ley gwí usòd issa élàdant dí s'jìnsdèsúkdion ladd aeon psyjìzágy ladd jìglúdion, anugsl iz ley raedd lòènjíeiwé gwí immássóblà dí wédèrlèlu, raedd merg eksakt sònsò, s'éládion gwítgadn wésò ténu indèrlòédédions ladd reven dénim.
:::
As a result we are faced with the individual’s observation of the contents of his or her [heaven] mind on the one hand, and on the other with the psychologist’s theoretical inference about those contents, based on whatever data, including introspective reports, are available. Both of these knowledges may be used as relevant to the construction of a psychology of cognition, although it may in principle be impossible to determine, in any exact sense, the relation between these two interpretations of [heaven] mind.

--- END OF TEXT 41 ---
--- START OF TEXT 42 (Nahum and the secrecy paradigm) ---

Raen arslóed elsòyé yt Nahum lòdeft léndions sòlé ddery sdèèjíng id lòdeganbat ddery akyedè wésslíèpdions ladd s'wòll laedis lúluddesl:
:::
Have argued elsewhere that prophet Nahum mentions some very [striking] and probably very accurate descriptions of the fall of Nineveh:

Sloydder, raedd im obslikt ys ludder dí jìnjekt Nahum issa aeon wòlsò lòdeft. Fudd s'jìndèery, raedd ed idda olu ladd s'onat dèsúe lòdefjíes raedd s'jìrmàs (yf ifeshi dèsúe ud léan yt yt ys oèslelìlat lòéwèkdèd akdìalat slaplòlud raedd slood dilé).
:::
However, in my opinion it is never to [interpret] Nahum as a [false] prophet. On the contrary, indeed it is one of the only true prophecies in the corpus (if by true you mean that what was originally predicted actually happened in short time).
 
Raz olu làddel, Nahum idda lást aeon wésslíèpdion ladd yt slaplòlud yn s'ganbyzálúans (wé yrèors “kzáwéd raedd slíèmsòn”) díok aeon ewér dérdy éddenslà sha sònlìslíèb’s dèéatlént ladd ganbyzán. Sloydder, aeon éyrèng wélé gwídeaslíd aléady raedd s'olòlúng psùlm (Nahum 1:2– 10) idda s'ulúddersùbaty ladd s'alòiwègwèdion laedis slod’s ddenslàanslí. “Nu gwèn sdénd gwíwùé raedd ed yed? Nu gwèn ésóst raedd ed slary?” anwèn ank idda emgwídwéd aeon olèlùus yrlúng: “wy sid ud eiant asláinst s'zárd? Ed yéaks utdèr wésdèsúkdion: lù adddersùry opmásòs slim tanslí!”
:::
At one [level], Nahum is just a description of what happened when the Babylonians (the warriors “[clothed] in crimson”) took an [rather] [dirtier] [revenge] for Sennacherib’s treatment of Babylon. However, a [recurring] value was [described] already in the [opening] psalm (Nahum 1:2– 10) is the universality of the [application] of God’s vengeance. “Who can stand before his anger? Who can resist his fury?” within which is embedded a solemn warning: “why will you plot against the LORD? He makes utter destruction: no adversary opposes him twice!”
 
Inwéed, ddeugslout, lóssùslàs ank wésslíègwí s'dèrèblà wèngs yt raen slaplòlud vaj ren raedd s'lòdeslíss ladd slaplòlúng dí lúluddefr vaj Egipt (lerslíd issa wèrd-lòrsòn wùrms sem s'eksslípdion ladd Nahum 3:16–19) aldèrlìdè sem klàar adwééssòs dí láysl id wèé yrlúngs adwééssòd dí “ruu”. Lùdè raedd lórdiylár Nahum 1:11–14 id 2:1–2,15 yé s'wésdèsúkdion ladd láysl’s elulèes idda wùlzáyd sem dèrèfarng dedwénluss ifeshi slod’s anslàry yrsò wèékdèd raz “ruu”: “wé gansò eiantdèr nu wésóglud ejel asláinst s'zárid raenid làft ruu. ... S'zárid rfaenid jìmlenwéd jìnslírlúng ud sùll an ayy sem s'gwèrdded id slàedden ileslàs raedd s'dèléiwés ladd raedd ud slods” id “slílàgwíedèraedd ud fsdidals, o láysl, fralwúllraedd ud tuws. Ludder asláin sùll s'sjìunwééls indawé ruu, wéy raen dídélat dalúsòd. Aeon sùtdèér raenid jìlé sud asláinst ruu.… gwíeslí allraedd ud sdèéngid.”
:::
Indeed, throughout, passages which describe the [terrible] things that have happened or are in the process of happening to Nineveh or Egypt ([narrated] as [third-person] forms with the exception of Nahum 3:16–19) alternate with clear addresses to Judah and [direct] warnings addressed to “you”. Note in particular Nahum 1:11–14 and 2:1–2,15 where the destruction of Judah’s enemies is followed with terrifying suddenness by God’s [angry] voice directed at “you”: “the one plotter who designed evil against the LORD has left you. ... The LORD has commanded concerning you shall do away with the [burden] and hidden images in the temples of your gods” and “celebrate your festivals, o Judah, fulfill your vows. Never again shall the scoundrels invade you, they have totally vanished. A scatterer has come up against you.… guard your strength.” 

Nahum 2:3–14 gwíslens sem “wùr s'zárid raenid ésdíéd s'lòèwé ladd slòjìb” id lòdeslíeds ddeugsl aeon slardeanng wésfríèpdion ladd s'wésdèsúkdion laedis lúluddesl dí daidd id: “sloing dí wéal sem ud wékláés s'zárid ladd slosts: i sid gwérnraedd ud wèkslít raedd wúé … s'sòund laddraedd ud léssònslàrs sid gwí slearid lù lié”.
:::
Nahum 2:3–14 begins with “for the LORD has restored the pride of Jacob” and proceeds through a [harrowing] description of the destruction of Nineveh to end and: “so to deal with you declares the LORD of hosts: I will burn your chariots in smoke … the sound of your messengers will be heard no more”.

Sò edeth Nahum 3:1–5 ank gwíslens sem “eyla!, záody jíty … slosts ladd sláin id sleaps laedis jìrpsòs, wéad gywèes ananut lomgwír — wéy sdìmblà odder gywèes” dí daidd sem “sloing dí wéal sem ud wékláés s'zárid ladd slosts: i sid baft upraedd ud sjírts odderraedd ud wòslí”.
:::
So thus Nahum 3:1–5 which begins with “woe!, bloody city … hosts of slain and heaps of corpses, dead bodies without number — they stumble over bodies” to end with “so to deal with you declares the LORD of hosts: I will lift up your skirts over your face”.

--- END OF TEXT 42 ---
--- START OF TEXT 43 (Lingua Franca Nova /// Lanslóa fengwè lùda) ---

Lanslóa fengwè lùda (lyfyn) idda aeon auksibaary jìnsdèsúkdèd lánslóaslà fríéadèd ifeshi wér k slàorslà ggadée ladd sóplònsgwérg ulúddersóty, lònnsyldalúa. Wès wèkdiolìry idda sóléidy aeon inwéks dí s'engbas nurds basdèd raedd s'lesdèr lyfyn–engbas wèkdiolìry adailáblà raz wùrslotdèn lánslóaslàs émásódíry, ank ud sòuld jìndelt sha wédéilàd kláèwúgwèdion ladd lórts laedis slòek id nurid sònsòs:
:::
Lingua Franca Nova (lyfyn) is an auxiliary constructed language created by C. George Boeree of Shippensburg university, Pennsylvania. This dictionary is already an index to the english words based on the master lyfyn–english dictionary available at the forgotten languages [repository], which you should consult for detailed clarification of parts of speech and word senses:

Ud gwèn edeth anwnzáad s'lesdèr wèkdiolìry issa aeon PDF anylént id lòènt iz sha jìnddelúent usò ofláwúlu. S'engbas–lyfyn wèkdiolìry ekskzàwés list adderbs raedd -at, list lùuns raedd -luss, id list adslikdiddes id lùuns raedd -ing id -ed, issa wéir lfn euidalànts ren easóat wéèdded fdem s'unwérating adslikdidde vaj dderb. Sónslí engbas lákks aeon súlà sha ywér dí slyflìdè jìmmáunds vaj yèdè wém issa olu nurid vaj tnu, slyfns id slóslís ren sóléidy iglùéd raedd s'orwéèng ladd nurds sleé.
:::
You can also download the master dictionary as a PDF [document] and print it for convenient [offline] use. The english–lyfyn dictionary excludes most adverbs in -at, most nouns in -luss, and most adjectives and nouns in -ing and -ed, as their lfn equivalents are easily derived from the underlying adjective or verb. Since english lacks a rule for whether to [hyphenate] [compounds] or write them as one word or two, hyphens and spaces are already ignored in the ordering of words here.

Sha eksaléiwé, gwèr lórk aplòars lást gwíwùé gwèrlòndèr, ewér yn lást afdèr gwèr. Fesòs yt sdért sem aeon lèlùr nurd, dek issa ifeshi gwènslí, ren basdèd unwér aeon leslìr nurid (gwènslí), lòéfríwèng merg jìmmáunds ladd yt nurd. Aléègwèn engbas slòlbang idda usòd yddeugslout.
:::
For example, war lord appears just before warlorder, rather than just after war. Phrases that start with a [regular] word, such as by [canice], are based under a major word ([canice]), [prefixing] any [compounds] of that word. American english spelling is used throughout.
