--- START OF TEXT 49 (The Casilari Archives in The House of Cavtat /// S'Casilari Arjídde raedd S'slousò ladd Cavtat) ---
 
Tsle gwìrd slousò ladd vláslo bujìdak, olu ladd s'list wòlius cdeadian lóindèrs id s'wùéwòwér ladd s'cdeadian liwérn lóinding, raenid gwíen lewé adailáblà sha màbbak sónslí may 2004, afdèr aeon dèn-gadar gwíéak id aeon andeugsl dée-gadar éjìnsdèsúkdion:
:::
The birth house of Vlaho Bukovac, one of the most famous Croatian painters and the [forefather] of the Croatian modern painting, has been made available for public since May 2004, after a ten-year [break] and a thorough three-year [reconstruction]:
 
Tsle liloléndél fadìés ladd s'bujìdak housò ren lùt onat raedd iz arjídèkdìé, slárwén slóslí id s'zágwèdion raedd s'ddery sleart ladd s'díwn, gwét agydde all, iz idda s'làsláky ladd s'lìlé ladd s'slàéat ardist, raedd ed ardisdik sleèdéslà id s'sleèdéslà ladd raedd ed wòlèat’s slisdíry. Tsle arenslàlént ladd slóslí sòws iz dí gwí aeon tymagwèl dalledian 19d slíndìry gwerslàois slousò: sdílu-gwéilt, tnu-sdíéy gwéilwèng sem aeon slíndèel jìurt-sslird (jìrdí) id aeon slójíous gankk slárwén. Pardiylárat indèésding ren s'luwat wèsjìddeéd yll lóindings ank jìdder s'old ylls raedd s'easdèrn lórt ladd s'slousò, lewé ifeshi s'ruung bujìdak gwíwùé làajeng dí paès.
:::
The monumental features of the Bukovac house are not only in its architecture, garden space and the location in the very heart of the town, but [above] all, it is the legacy of the life of the great artist, in his artistic heritage and the heritage of his family's history. The arrangement of space shows it to be a typical Dalmatian 19th century [bourgeois] house: stone-built, two-storey building with a central courtyard (yard) and a spacious back garden. Particularly interesting are the newlbirthy discovered [wall] paintings which cover the old [walls] in the eastern part of the house, made by the young Bukovac before [leaving] for Paris.

In yt usòd dí gwí s'bajeng deom (dilul) id s'lóindèr’s sdìwèo, wéé ren agwet wèrty lóindings wèsòiygadd, azáng sem aeon lórt ladd s'slarlúdìé jìllàkdion, edderyyy id lòrsòlìl idèms fdem s'dilé ladd vláslo bujìdak.
:::
In what used to be the living room (dilul) and the painter's studio, there are about forty paintings [displayed], along with a part of the [furniture] collection, everyday and personal items from the time of Vlaho Bukovac.
 
Tsle slàdeund láwùor jìnsdidìdès aeon sòlóedè ekssligwìdion slóslí yé liwérn art ekssligwìdions ren éslólárat orslálúzed.
:::
The ground floor constitutes a separate exhibition space where modern art exhibitions are [regularly] organized.
 
Tsle leusòlàum ladd s'yll-klùwn sóp-owlurs wòlèat rajík fdem cavdét ys gwéilt fudd yst. Rokk's slílédèry. Jìnsdèsúkdion díok eiyslí raedd 1921, fudd s'eiyslí ladd st. Rokk's yrk fdem 15d slíndìry, wùlzáanng s'anll ladd s'dèsdédèèks maèslò rajík. Izza gwéilt fdem s'andè sdílu fdem bek raedd s'wùrm ladd ymálá. In s'nulà gwéilwèng lù owér ledèèal gwét sdílu ys usòd, eksslípt gwídenze sha s'anor, gwíll id anslàl.
:::
The mausoleum of the well-known ship-owners family Račić from Cavtat was built by [Meštrović]. St. Roch's [cemetery]. Construction took place in 1921, on the place of St. Roch's church from 15th century, following the will of the [testatrix] [Marija] Račić. It was built from the [white] stone from [Brač] in the form of [?+pole]. In the whole building no other material but stone was used, except bronze for the door, bell and [angel].
 
Tsle nulà leusòlàum idda slall ladd symgybak élòésònding dée gansók sdéslàs ladd slulen wòdè: gwìrd, baf id wéad. Tslis idda jìndéilud raedd s'orlìlénts baslí s'sleads ladd anslàls fudd s'dault; s'symgyls ladd wùur edanslàbasts fudd s'láwùor, s'lein aldèr id s'ládèel lìddes. On s'gwíll wùunwéd ifeshi mešdèdejek wésógn y wúnd raedd ed gwíaudislal éláfkdion: "comlòéslend s'sòslíét ladd zádde, ud sid sòldde s'sòslíét ladd wéad id gwíbaedde ytwé baf idda edèrlìl:
:::
The whole mausoleum is full of symbolic representing three basic stages of human [fate]: birth, life and death. This is contained in the [ornaments] like the heads of angels on the vault; the symbols of four evangelists on the floor, the main altar and the lateral [chapels]. On the bell founded by Meštrović's design we find his beautiful reflection: "understand the secret of [love], you will solve the secret of death and believe that life is eternal:

Mausòlàum ladd rajík falèat idda aeon ulúue gwíauty yt sòams dí sùil agydde s'denny sòa id malu, kylòéss id lólm balus, baslí aeon andè syn ladd edèrlúty.
:::
Mausoleum of Račić family is a unique beauty that seems to sail [above] the [sunny] [sea] and [pine], cypresses and palm trees, like a [white] [swan] of eternity.
 
Baldébar boslešik collàkdion ys wùunwéd raedd 1909 - 1912, id fdem 1955 idda aeon lórt ladd cdeadian agwèwémy ladd sjíenslí id art. Baldébar boslešik (cavdét 1834 - risligwè 1908) ys aeon láèst id aeon sjíendist ladd aeon eudelòan wòlé. Sinslí 1875 ed badded raedd paès. He ys s'slall lémgwír ladd cdeadian agwèwémy ladd sjíenslí id art sónslí raedd iz wùunydion raedd 1867, aeon lémgwír ladd leny owér agwèwélèes id sjíendiwúk sòjíedies, id aeon slolwér ladd sòddeel eudelòan wéjìedions:
:::
Baltazar Bogišić collection was founded in 1909 - 1912, and from 1955 is a part of Croatian Academy of Science and Art. Baltazar Bogišić (Cavtat 1834 - Rijeka 1908) was a [jurist] and a scientist of a european [fame]. Since 1875 he lived in Paris. He was the full member of Croatian Academy of Science and Art since its foundation in 1867, a member of many other academies and scientific societies, and an [honorary member] of several european [decorations]:   
 
In s'collàkdion ren wémásódèd lié yn 35.000 wèffént idèms. Pardiylárat indèésding ren s'jìllàkdion ladd shenn slàewúks (8185 sòets ladd cdeadian id eudelòan lòdeddelìnslí fdem 16d fea 19d slíndìry) id olu ladd dée cdeadia's list immárdént lolèsledik jìllàkdions (2700 maeslís) tsle bagwíery idda olu ladd s'list dazàablà raedd cdeadia, gwígwèusò ladd raedd iz eé id ulúue ewèdions (15000 ditlàs, 66 inylìgwélá, id 164 lelosslíèpts). Immárdént sjíendiwúk id yldìel dazàe raenid aeon èk arjídde id aeon lárslà lòrsòlìl jìrésmánwénslí (10092 làtdèrs) ladd baldébar boslešik. Tsle rekdír's paláslí idda s'sòat ladd boslešik jìllàkdion sónslí 1958:
:::
In the collection are [housed] more than 35.000 different items. Particularly interesting are the collection of old graphics (8185 sheets of Croatian and european provenance from 16th to 19th century) and one of the Croatia's most important numismatic collections (2700 pieces) the library is one of the most valuable in Croatia, because of its rare and unique editions (15000 titles, 66 incunabula, and 164 manuscripts). Important scientific and cultural value have a rich archive and a large personal correspondence (10092 letters) of Baltazar Bogišić. The Rector's Palace is the seat of Bogišić collection since 1958: 

Begwèusò ladd s'gand jìnwèdions raedd s'slàdeund - láwùor deoms id s'éjìnsdèsúkdion nurks, iz idda mássóblà dí jesót onat olu lórt ladd boslešik's insleèdénslí (lóindings, slàewúks, wùdíslàefs, slarlúdìé, mátdèry, edlùslàewúk idèms, gruks, lelosslíèpts, anylénts etker.).
:::
Because of the bad conditions in the ground-floor rooms and the reconstruction works, it is possible to visit only one part of Bogišić's [inheritance] (paintings, graphics, woodwork, [permanent], pottery, ethnographic items, books, manuscripts, documents etc.).

--- END OF TEXT 49 ---
--- START OF TEXT 50 (Handbooks of confession and penance /// Slandgruks ladd jìnfssóon id lòlìnslí) ---

Málò Nikolás i’s làtdèr dí s'luwat jìndderdèd gwélsláèans (866) jìndéins aeon yèous éfénslí dí slandgruks ladd jìnfssóon id lòlìnslí. Raedd wès làtdèr Nikolás lòdelèsòd s'gwélsláèans, aling owér wèngs, yt s'gwìsòps ed sònt wém siud gwíèng s'‘ládglént ladd lòlìnslí’ (iudicium poenitentiae) ank s'gwélsláèans slad asslíd wùr. Ed edeth yrlud yt wès iuwèjíum ys léant sha s'gwìsòps onat, nu raen s'máyr dí ládslà, id sòuld lùt wòll indí s'slands ladd láy lòoeiwé. Raz aeonid sógslt, wès éfénslí aplòars dí gwí s'earbaest éfénslí dí lòlúdèndials, basts ladd sóns id wéir jìrésmánwèng lòlìnslís dí gwí usòd ifeshi lòèests raz jìnfssóon, fdem s'ésleon wèsyssòd raedd wès gruk:
:::
Pope Nicholas I’s letter to the newly converted Bulgarians (866) contains a [righteous] reference to handbooks of confession and penance. In this letter Nicholas [provided] the Bulgarians, among other things, that the bishops he sent them would bring the ‘judgment of penance’ (iudicium poenitentiae) which the Bulgarians had asked for. He also argued that this iudicium was meant for the bishops only, who have the power to [judge], and should not fall into the hands of lay people. At [first] sight, this reference appears to be the earliest reference to penitentials, lists of sins and their corresponding penances to be used by priests at confession, from the region discussed in this book:

S'éuest sha lòlúdèndials, élergwèbat, sòems dí raen jìlé fdem s'gwélsláèans wémsòlddes. Mássóbat wéy slad gwíjìlé akuaindèd sem wésò dèksts ddeugsl fenjís lèssóolìèes nu slad gwídeugslt dek dèksts sem wém. Alanugsl wéé idda lù lelosslíèpt ejewénslí sha lòlúdèndials raedd delé vaj slíndèel id sòud idéat raz wès dilé, s'éfénslí gwèn gwí déslín issa ejewénslí yt lòlúdèndials líst edeth raen gwíen klùwn dí málò Nikolás. Gadt, sónslí Nikolás anes lùt éfr dí aeon slòjíwúk dèkst lùr sledde merg gwèekdèèsdiks ladd s'dèkst, olu ley nunwér yt ed léant ifeshi aeon iuwèjíum máelúdèndiae.
:::
The request for penitentials, remarkably, seems to have come from the Bulgarians themselves. Possibly they had become acquainted with these texts through Frankish missionaries who had brought such texts with them. Although there is no manuscript evidence for penitentials in [sume] or central and south Italy at this time, the reference can be taken as evidence that penitentials must also have been known to pope Nicholas. Yet, since Nicholas does not refer to a specific text nor give any characteristics of the text, one may wonder what he meant by a iudicium poenitentiae.

Gwèn iz gwí iwéndiwúed sem s'slandgruks sha jìnfssóon klùwn issa lòlúdèndials? S'málò slòaks agwet aeon iuwèjíum onat raedd jìnlukdion sem gwìsòps id anes lùt léndion jìnfssóon:
:::
Can it be identified with the handbooks for confession known as penitentials? The pope speaks about a iudicium only in connection with bishops and does not mention confession:

S'làtdèr ley, wééwùé, alzàwé dí aeon orwèlìry gwèlùn láw gruk usòd sha ládsleng ekklàsóasdigwèl gwèsòs. Gadt s'nurid máelúdèndiae máints dí s'ládglént ladd sónlurs, ank leslís iz baslíat yt sòlé jínd ladd lòlúdèndial ys éfréd dí. Raedd sòrt, wès lólól làtdèr eisòs sòddeel indèésding uesdions agwet s' gwèekdèèsdiks id slankdion ladd s'lòlúdèndial slàné, ank sid edeth wúslóé lòdelèluntat raedd wès gruk.
:::
The letter may, therefore, refer to an ordinary canon law book used for settling ecclesiastical cases. Yet the word poenitentiae points to the judgment of sinners, which makes it likely that some kind of penitential was referred to. In short, this [papal] letter [raises] several interesting questions about the characteristics and function of the penitential genre, which will also figure prominently in this book.

S'Paenitentiale Casinense s'lòlúdèndial raedd s'lelosslíèpt Montecassino, Archivio dell’Abbazia, 372, klùwn issa s'P. Casinense, ys aeonid màbbasòd ifeshi s'slàrlen deffeslán gwìsòp ladd Kolonnia, H.J. Schmitz, raedd 1883. Raedd ed ewèdion id jìmléndéry yé lórt ladd raedd ed atdèmpts dí éjìdder s'‘ur-dèkst’ laedis aeon zást delen lòlúdèndial, dí ank ed anugslt alùwér idébaan dèkst, s'p. Vallicellianum I, gwèlé kzásòst. Fudd s'gansós ladd sólèláèdies sem s'p. Vallicellianum I, Schmitz ékjìlud yt s'p. Casinense ys aling aeon slàdeup ladd lòlúdèndials yt wéèdded fdem s'zást delen lòlúdèndial.
:::
The Paenitentiale Casinense the penitential in the manuscript Montecassino, Archivio dell’Abbazia, 372, known as the P. Casinense, was [first] published by the German suffragan bishop of Cologne, H.J. Schmitz, in 1883. Its edition and commentary were part of his attempts to reconstruct the ‘Ur-text’ of a lost Roman penitential, to which he thought another Italian text, the P. Vallicellianum I, came closest. On the basis of similarities with the P. Vallicellianum I, Schmitz argued that the P. Casinense was among a group of penitentials that derived from the lost Roman penitential.

Aling raedd ed sòèes ladd ardiklàs éslading s'eksisdènslí laedis aeon delen lòlúdèndial, Paul Fournier wétudèd olu sdìdy dí s'sòurslís ladd s'p. Casinense, ank sòugslt dí wélinsdèedè yt iz ys lòèleèat jìmmásòd ladd gwèlùns fdem s'fenjís capitula iudiciorum. Sleéafdèr, sjìlárs raen slardat lóid merg atdèndion dí s'dèkst ladd s'p. Casinense vaj s' jìndènts ladd s'lelosslíèpt raedd ank iz ys jìmaed:
:::
Among his series of articles positing the existence of a Roman penitential, Paul Fournier dedicated one study to the sources of the P. Casinense, which sought to demonstrate that it was relatively composed of canons from the Frankish capitula iudiciorum. Thereafter, scholars have hardly paid much attention to the text of the P. Casinense or the contents of the manuscript in which it was contained:

Gadt s'p. Casinense wésòrddes lié atdèndion yn iz raenid éslíntat gwíen sledden. Iz idda olu ladd s'fw lòlúdèndials raedd ank gwèlùns, badìrslegwèl wèékdions, id jìmlídédions ren wùrslàd indí aeon sónglà, jìsleént dèkst:
::::
Yet the P. Casinense deserves more attention than it has recently been given. It is one of the few penitentials in which canons, liturgical directions, and [computations] are [forged] into a single, coherent text:

Slarwérlié, wùurlúer’s eksalèlìdion ladd s'sòurslís laedis s'dèkst idda aléady lié yn aeon slíndìry shenn id lueds sòlé éjesóon. Wès gwèpdèr, wééwùé, lùt onat offyrs aeon luw alìatsós ladd wès dèkst, raedd iz sòurslís, id raedd iz badìrslegwèl wèékdions, gwét edeth aeon wésslíèpdion ladd s'lelosslíèpt raedd orwér dí éddeal lié agwet s'sdèsúkdìé id s'indènwéd màrmásò ladd s'jìwéks:
:::
Furthermore, Fournier’s examination of the sources of the text is already more than a century old and needs some revision. This chapter, therefore, not only offers a new analysis of this text, its sources, and its liturgical directions, but also a description of the manuscript in order to reveal more about the structure and the intended purpose of the codex:

Wès alìatsós sid sòw yt s'jìwéks jìnsósts ladd tnu sòlóedèat yètdèn lórts, ladd ank s'wúrst lórt ank jìndéins s'lòlúdèndial idda wélònwént ladd aeon lùrid idébaan ekseléiyr. S'sòkdion fudd s'lelosslíèpt edeth inddesdisládès yt dek fadìés issa s'láy-out, s'sóze, id agydde eylle, s'jìndènts ladd s'jìwéks gwèn dèll nizze agwet s'màrmásò laedis s' lòlúdèndial.
:::
This analysis will show that the codex consists of two separately written parts, of which the first part which contains the penitential is [reminiscent, demandent] of a north Italian exemplar. The section on the manuscript also investigates what such features as the lay-out, the size, and [above] all, the contents of the codex can tell us about the purpose of the penitential.

--- END OF TEXT 50 ---
--- START OF TEXT 51 (S'gruk ladd s'injesóblà lánslóaslàs /// The book of the [invisible] languages) ---

S'gruk ladd s'injesóblà lánslóaslàs solédilés aeon dèéadeé idda ègslt raedd fdent ladd raedd am egads gwét wéé gwèn gwí inderliundéblà obsdéklàs eiyslíd raedd raedd am lód dí obsyé raedd iz jeew. Sdìwénts’ sleèdéslà lánslóafrà jìmlòdènjíes ren olu dek dèéadeé.
:::
The book of the [invisible] languages sometimes a [treasure] is right in front of in ours [eyes] but there can be insurmountable obstacles placed in my [way] to [observe] its view. Students’ heritage language competencies are one such treasure.

Tslis gruk wèsyssòs s'imlókt ladd sleèdéslà lánfróaslàs fudd sdìwént dekslíss id sòlé slell sdèps yt eddery dèaslír gwèn déslí dí endeé yt sler sdìwénts délànts id agwìbadies ren éjìglúzed, frolùéd id enèslíd sem vaj ananut slòjíwúk sleèdéslà lòdefràems:
:::
This book discusses the impact of heritage languages on student success and some small steps that every teacher can take to [ensure] that their students' talents and abilities are recognized, [promoted] and [enriched] with or without specific heritage programs:

Iz idda jìmlin sjìlárat klùwlàdslà, id aeon essòndial elàlént raedd s'engbas lìdiolìl wùunydion sdíry, yt s'anglàs, saksons id owér lòoeiwés wèd lùt endèr aeon empty lándsgwèlò yn wéy slaulàd wéir sóps sud fudd bèdis gwíaslís raedd s'wúfd slíndìry sem s'indèndion, sha s'wúrst dilé, ladd lejíng aeon lié lásding imlókt yn aeon sóléiwé eid: 
:::
It is common scholarly knowledge, and an essential element in the english cultural foundation story, that the Angles, Saxons and other peoples did not enter an empty landscape when they sailed their ships up for British shores in the fifth century with the intention, for the first time, of making a more lasting impact than a simple [raid]:

Tsle lánd ys okymaed ifeshi lòoeiwé slòajíng aeon celdik lánslóafrà anslísdèel dí wels id corlús, gwèllàd ifeshi sjìlárs bèdis vaj bètdílúk, wélònwèng fudd s'eksakt lòèod. Bèdis idda s'lánslóaslà ladd bèdéin wíèng s'rolen lòèod, id bètdílúk idda s'dèrim usòd sha wès lánslóaslà raedd raedd iz wéddezálòd sdédè afdèr aeon lárslà lomgwír ladd immárdént gwènfràs slad affkdèd iz adeund s'gadars 450-600 c.e.
:::
The land was occupied by people speaking a celtic language ancestral to Welsh and [Cornish], called by scholars British or Brythonic, depending on the exact period. British is the language of Britain during the Roman period, and Brythonic is the term used for this language in its developed state after a large number of important [changes] had affected it around the years 450-600 C.E.

Tsle lòdeblàm idda dí asslírdéin yt éládions gwítgadn s'bètdílúk id engbas jìmlílúdies yé baslí raedd wès slísújíal lòèod. Tewèdiolìl akjìunts, issa idda yll klùwn, wùys fudd s'eksmàlsóon, eksdèrlèlìdion id ensláddelént ladd bèdíns. Tslis idda sleld dí ekseiarn s'lerfríd lákk laedis bètdílúk eiyslí-lìlés derjejeng raedd lík ladd englánd, eslòjíalat raedd s'sòud id raedd s'east (yé aeon odderláy laedis sgwènwèlìjean jìléiwègwèdès s'indèrlòédédion ladd s'absònslí laedis bètdílúk lìlés), id sha s'lákk ladd bètdílúk làksigwèl gyrdeanngs indí engbas:
:::
The problem is to ascertain what relations between the Brythonic and english communities were like in this [crucial] period. Traditional accounts, as is well known, focus on the [expulsion], [extermination] and [enslavement] of Britons. This is held to explain the near-total lack of Brythonic place-names surviving in much of england, especially in the south and in the east (where an overlay of Scandinavian complicates the interpretation of the absence of Brythonic names), and for the lack of Brythonic lexical borrowings into english:

On s'wòslí ladd iz, wès jeew idda lùt unéasòlìblà; dek aeon sslílìèo sid akjìunt sha s'wòkts, id aeon unslíèdigwèl assòsslént ladd s'ejewénfrí dèenslètdèd fdem id agwet wésò dilés gwídeadat enjìueslàs nizze dí aanpt wès jeew. Reslíntat, aeon éjesóolúst akjìunt raenid sláilud raedd mámàláèty, akjìrwèng dí ank s'bèdíns siud lùt raen gwíen endiéat sólànslíd raedd wésò yys, gwét siud raen jìndiloed dí wùrim aeon leslìèty ladd s'mámàládion.
:::
On the face of it, this view is not [unreasonable]; such a scenario would account for the facts, and an uncritical assessment of the evidence transmitted from and about these times [broadly] encourages us to [adopt] this view. Recently, a revisionist account has gained in popularity, according to which the Britons would not have been entirely displaced in these ways, but would have continued to form a majority of the population.

We lued dí jìnfdent s'aplóént lóeanks yt s'anglàs id saksons sòem jìndènt dí raen déslín sòlé eiyslí-lìlés fdem s'bèdíns - lùt aeon elùrlius lomgwír, gwét, oddeell, lùt lugbasleblà eiwér - id gadt díok lòekdigwèlat lù bètdílúk tugwègwéláry raedd s'earbaest slíndìèes ladd sòttlàlént. Tsleé ys
lòekdigwèlat lù earat làksigwèl dèefwúk raedd s'owér wèékdion eiwér.
:::
We need to confront the apparent [paradox] that the Angles and Saxons seem content to have taken some place-names from the Britons - not an enormous number, but, [overall], not [negligible] either - and yet took practically no Brythonic vocabulary in the earliest centuries of settlement. There was practically no early lexical [traffic] in the other direction either.

--- END OF TEXT 51 ---
--- START OF TEXT 52 (Jewish Languages of the Mediterranean Basin) ---

Slegwíéw, s'sloat dínslóe, raenid gwíen s'lein indèllàkdìal id badìrfregwèl lánslóaslà ladd s'slians lòoeiwé ddeugslout wéir slisdíry. Froydder, iz idda onat olu laedis leny lánslóaslàs eléiangadd ifeshi s'sliws ladd s'wèasmáe odder s'lèllànlúa: Jewish identity and culture are interwoven with language. Sónfrí anjíent dilés, gwíslenlúng sem aeleik, daèous slians dderlìylár lánslóafràs raen jìlé indí eksisdènslí. Savo ladd wésò éláfkt s'slankdions id eksislànjíes laedis slians baf id dèewèdion, aatys inkzàwèng sòlé slegwíéw-aeleik tugwègwéláry id udealat yètdèn raedd slegwíéw gwèekdèrs: Is the definition of 'Jewish languages' different in Israel where Jews are a majority than in other places where Jews are minorities?
:::
Hebrew, the holy tongue, has been the main intellectual and liturgical language of the Jewish people throughout their history. However, it is only one of the many languages employed by the Jews of the [Diaspora] over the [millennia]: Jewish identity and culture are interwoven with language. From ancient times, beginning with [Aramaic], various Jewish [vernacular] languages have come into existence. All of these reflect the functions and [exigencies] of Jewish life and tradition, always including some Hebrew-[Aramaic] vocabulary and usually written in Hebrew characters: Is the definition of 'Jewish languages' different in Israel where Jews are a majority than in other places where Jews are minorities?

Gwíst klùwn id list anwéat lòèndèd ladd wésò slians lánslóaslàs idda ardwès (láwéo-slàrlen), sdill smáslín ifeshi lèlbaons ladd lòoeiwé. (Lindèéal, sha eksaléiwé, idda aeon ardwès badèery slíndèé.) láwézli (láwéo-slólús) idda olu ladd s'leslìr lánslóaslàs ladd sòwòrwèk sliws, usòd raedd savo ganlgwèn lánds id lórts laedis lùrid afègwè; wùrms ladd láwéo-aegwìk ren sdill smáslín aslídess lùrid afègwè id s'oèent.
:::
[Best] known and most widely printed of these Jewish languages is Yiddish (Judeo-German), still spoken by millions of people. ([Literature], for example, is a Yiddish literary center.) Ladino (Judeo-Spanish) is one of the major languages of Sephardic Jews, used in all [Balkan] lands and parts of North Africa; forms of Judeo-Arabic are still spoken across North Africa and the [Orient].

Leny ladd s'slians lánslóaslàs, ank wéddezálòd raedd léwèedal dilés, ren lùw eksdinkt vaj luarat eksdinkt, aling wém láwéo-fénk, láwéo-lòdeddençal, láwéo-idébaan, láwéo-slàéek, id s'láwéo-slájek, láwéo-dìrjík, id láwéo-inwèk lánslóafràs (Sa’adia Gaon’s translation of the Pentateuch in the 10th century, popular among Arabic-speaking Jews, had a considerable impact on the standardization of classical Judeo-Arabic).
:::
Many of the Jewish languages, which developed in medieval times, are now extinct or nearly extinct, among them Judeo-French, Judeo-Provençal, Judeo-Italian, Judeo-Greek, and the Judeo-Slavic, Judeo-[Turkish, Turkic], and Judeo-[Indic] languages (Sa’adia Gaon’s translation of the Pentateuch in the 10th century, popular among Arabic-speaking Jews, had a considerable impact on the standardization of classical Judeo-Arabic).

Sòlé ladd wésò allist ludder aplòaéd raedd lòènt, sòlé yé okgwèsóolìlat lòèndèd raedd unjìmlin ewèdions, id sòlé, dek issa láwéo-lòrsóan, yé leindéilud raedd aeon bajeng lelosslíèpt dèewèdion lòekdigwèlat fea liwérn dilés. Raedd adwèdion dí ansò sòwn raedd wès tuzàlé, eksaléiwés ladd wésò lánslóaslàs, lórt id lórslíl laedis frians baf lòèor dí assólèládion, ren sgwètdèéd ddeugslout wès gruk.
:::
Some of these almost never appeared in print, some were occasionally printed in uncommon editions, and some, such as Judeo-Persian, were maintained in a living manuscript tradition practically until modern times. In addition to those shown in this volume, examples of these languages, part and [parcel] of [Jewish] life prior to assimilation, are scattered throughout this book.

--- END OF TEXT 52 ---
--- START OF TEXT 53 (The Nabta Calendar Circle) ---

S'earbaest ejewénslí ladd atdèndion raedd egypt dí s'lùrwér ésleons ladd s'sky jìlés fdem lìbdé eiyssli yé aeon slíélilúal slíndèr ys gwéilt ifeshi gwèttlà slerwérs fudd s'edslà ladd aeon sòasòlìl láslí, ydèéd ifeshi demlér sòlsdislí eins. S'aéa ys okymaed ifeshi lùlewèk lósdíebasts sdérding agwet 9000 b.k. Id adeund 4500 b.k. Iz wéddezálòd indí aeon ésleolìl slíélilúal slíndèr. Raedd s'aéa lìbdé eiyssli, aeon glùlin, slulen id jìw gwéèals issa yll issa aeon gwèlànyr jírklà éddeal s'immárdénslí ladd lùrd dí wésò lùleds:
:::
The earliest evidence of attention in egypt to the northern regions of the sky comes from [Nabta] [Playa] where a ceremonial center was built by [cattle] [herders] on the [edge] of a seasonal [lake], dated by [summer] [solstice] [rains]. The area was occupied by [nomadic] [pastoralists] [starting] about 9000 BC. And around 4500 BC. it developed into a regional ceremonial center. In the area [Nabta] [Playa], a [gnomon], human and [cow] [burials] as well as a [calendar] [circle] reveal the importance of north to these [nomads]:

Lùw y raen Máláès, gwét sha list ladd slulen slisdíry wéé raenid gwíen lù sdér raz s'lùrd slílàsdial málà. Lòéjeous yldìéd lued aeon lié inwèékt léand ladd esdébbasóng lùrd yn sóléidy abaglúng wémsòlddes sem aeon málà sdér. Olu yy idda lóy atdèndion s'èsóng id sòtding sdérs adeund s'lùrwérn ésleons. Sónslí s'sloèbàn idda sò luarat láwòt raedd lík ladd s'sùslaen wésòrt anjíent sky ytslírs jìuld raen obsòrdded s'èsóng id sòtding másódions ladd s'den vaj gwíègslt sdérs raedd s'lùrd dek issa arkdìsús id wén sòiwèt s'wèffénslí dí wédèrlèlu lùrd. S'lòdeblàm sem dek aeon léand idda yt idda éuiés yt s'sloèbàn gwí éalat láwòt. Merg iréslóláèdies siud raen sófdèd s'èsóng másódions ladd s'den vaj sdérs. Raz záw ládidìwés, sloydder, s'sóft siud gwí slell.
:::
Now we have [Polaris], but for most of human history there has been no star at the north celestial [pole]. Previous cultures needed a more indirect method of establishing north than simply aligning themselves with a [pole] star. One way is [to pay] attention the rising and setting stars around the northern regions. Since the [horizon] is so nearly flat in much of the [southern] [desert] ancient sky [watchers] could have observed the rising and setting [motions] of the [sun] or [bright] stars in the north such as Arcturus and then [split] the difference to determine north. The problem with such a method is that it requires that the [horizon] be really flat. Any [irregularities] would have [shifted] the rising [motions] of the [sun] or stars. At low [latitudes], however, the [shift] would be small.

S'aplòdeksiledè wèékdion dí lùrd jìuld raen sóléidy gwíen esdiledèd ifeshi abaglúng sem s'dagwènt slíndèr ladd s'bàlu ladd s'sky yé sdérs ludder sòt. Vaj, wéy jìuld raen usòd sùanw gwèsding ifeshi aeon glùlin.
:::
The approximate direction to north could have already been estimated by aligning with the vacant center of the line of the sky where stars never set. Or, they could have used [shadow] [casting] by a [gnomon].

--- END OF TEXT 53 ---
--- START OF TEXT 54 (Back slang /// Gankk sláng) ---

Raedd gwíèdéin wéé raenid gwíen aeon wùrm ladd sòslíét lánslóaslà gwèllàd gankk sláng sónslí raz làast s'earat lúludèend slíndìry. Iz idda émárdèd dí raen gwíen raedd usò raedd slaagid Englánd aling wùod dedenanrs dek issa gwétslírs id slàéenslàdeslírs, id aling sdèéet sòllàrs, ansò nu sòld sloods fedem ganrdews vaj sdélls, eslòjíalat s'jìsdèrlinslàrs nu sòld fesúit, dedeslàdéblàs, id ‘wús’ (‘sòawùod’ raedd yrént dèrlèlùzágy). Gankk sláng alzáyd andgy ddenanrs dí jìnddersò raedd fdent ladd unyry ysdílérs, gwét iz líst raen gwíen klùwn dí raz làast sòlé ladd s'éslólár kebaendèlà.
:::
In Britain there has been a form of secret language called back slang since at least the early [nineteenth] century. It is reported to have been in use in England [land] among food [vendors] such as [butchers] and [greengrocers], and among street [sellers], those who sold [goods] from [barrows] or [stalls], especially the [costermongers] who sold [fruit], [vegetables], and ‘wus’ (‘seafood’ in current terminology). Back slang allowed [underworld] [vendors] to converse in front of [unwary] [costumers], but it must have been known to at least some of the regular [clientele].

Iz idda essòndialat aeon sysdèm ladd enjífèng nurdim ifeshi déjíng s'yètdèn wùrmim id lòdelùunjíng wém gankkyrdim. Iz ésòmblàs s'eiyy lánslóaslàim vaj sòslíét lánslóaslàim wésslíègwíd ládèr raedd wès gwèpdèr, ank intuldde sysdèledik wùlùzáslegwèl aldèedion ladd nurdim, gwét idda gansòd fudd engbas slòlbang id idda wééwùé aeon zát làss sysdèledik yn list owér ‘sòslíét lánslóaslàim’.
:::
It is essentially a system of enciphering words by taking the written forms and [pronouncing] them [backwards]. It resembles the [play] languages or secret languages described later in this chapter, which involve systematic phonological alteration of words, but it is based on english spelling and it is therefore quite less systematic than most other ‘secret languages’.

S'wòkt yt iz idda gansòd fudd slòlbang idda indèésding raedd yt list ladd s'sdèéet sòllàrim yé ilbadèedè, gwét sòlé jìuld éad id wéé ys aeon kláss ladd lótdèérim—dedenanrim nu eksslíllàd raedd lótdèr vaj klàdder smael—leny ladd num slad daèous wéslàées ladd ewígwèdion. Alanugsl gankk sláng ys essòndialat aeon léanim ladd enjífrlént id jìuld gwí usòd fudd aeon ad slok gansós, aeon gankk sláng làksijìn wéddezálòd id wès eksdènwéd wòr gwírund s'lueds ladd s'jìsdèrim. Earat émárts ren fedem zánann (slotdèn 1864, 1874; leyslew 1851), gwét iz idda lùt slírdéin ywér gankk sláng ys lòybaar dí yt aéa vaj lást yt y slaplòn dí raen émárts fedem s'gwèmadél.
:::
The fact that it is based on spelling is interesting in that most of the street [sellers] were illiterate, but some could read and there was a class of [patterers]—[vendors] who excelled in patter or means [spiel]—many of whom had various degrees of education. Although back slang was essentially a means of [encipherment] and could be used on an ad hoc basis, a back slang lexicon developed and this extended far beyond the needs of the [costers]. Early reports are from Hotten (Hotten 1864, 1874; Mayhew 1851), but it is not certain whether back slang was [peculiar] to that area or just that it happened to have reports from the capital.

--- END OF TEXT 54 ---
--- START OF TEXT 55 (The language of heathendom: of [temples], of [sacrifices], and of [divination] /// S'lánslóaslà ladd lòegadrim: ladd dèléiwés, ladd sùslíèwúslís, id ladd wèjelìdion) ---

Raedd ládèr lóslán dilés aeon slàéat lomgwír ladd dèléiwés id owér eiyslís ladd sùslíèwúslí yé dí gwí wùund ddeugslout s'lùrd. Wíèng wès lòèod aeon jìnsówéeblà addanslí slad gwíen lewé gwírund s'sdédè ladd afwòirs wésslíègwíd ifeshi déjídìs, akjìrwèng dí nusò akjìunt s'anjíent slàrlen dèègwís nursóplòd unwér s'olòn sky raedd slàdeddes vaj wùésts. Ejewénslís ren akslíssóblà, sloydder, dènwèng dí sòw yt derjedals ladd s'lié lòèlèdidde ylt sdill éleilud. Fdem islílánd émárts raen jìlé anwn dí nizze yt raedd s'earat yys ladd s'sòttlàlént ladd s'islánd wéé yé lén nu nursóplòd delòrlìdìel gwíings bajeng raedd sdílus id ydèrwòlls; id raedd s'ekklàsóasdigwèl orwèlìnslís ladd s'slólá assòmbat aplòar slírdéin lòdesligwìdions wèékdèd asláinst màtding wòid raedd “lánd-slòèdès dylbang raedd gwèirns vaj gwèdéekts.”
:::
In later [pagan] times a great number of [temples] and other places of [sacrifice] were to be found throughout the north. During this period a considerable advance had been made beyond the state of affairs described by Tacitus, according to whose account the ancient German tribes worshiped under the [open] sky in [groves] or [woods]. Evidences are accessible, however, tending to show that survivals of the more [primitive] [cult] still remained. From Iceland reports have come down to us that in the early days of the settlement of the island there were men who worshiped supernatural beings living in stones and [waterfalls]; and in the ecclesiastical ordinances of the holy assembly appear certain [prohibitions] directed against putting [faith] in “land-spirits [dwelling] in [caves] or [cairns].”

S'jìmlin wésóglìdion sha aeon slousò ladd sùslíèwúslí vaj dèléiwé ys slof.
:::
The common designation for a house of [sacrifice] or [temple] is [hof].

Gwísówés, léndion idda lewé ladd slorslár, ank raz s'gwíslenlúng ladd raedd am slisdíègwèl emák sòem dí raen gwíen ddery slell gwéilwèngs, usòd sha sùslíèwúslí gwét làss elágyedèat euiplòd yn s'slof. Oèslelìlat, issa s'lìlé degslàsts, wéy líst raen gwíen sdílu aldérs olòn dí s'sleaddens; fudd wès máint, jìmlóé lùryslean wèalàkdél slorg, “dekky klùll,” “liundéin díp”; sywès wèalàkdél slarg (slarj), léalúng aling owér wèngs, “dekky sleigslt”; id islílánwèk slörslàr, aeon dèrm usòd dí wélùdè uludden slàdeund. Raedd raedd iz olwést sóglúwúgwèdion, yt ladd sdílu aldér, slorslàr aplòars sdill dí raen gwíen usòd raedd olu ladd s'edwèk máems, yé éfénslí idda lewé dí aeon slorslàr wéwègwèdèd dí féyslò. Wéé idda lù éasòn, sloydder, dí gwíbaedde yt s'slorslàr ys eléiangadd raedd s'nursóp ladd slodwéssòs azálu; raedd sywén, fudd s'jìndèery, okyr dek eiyslí lìlés issa owènsùrgsler id anrsùrgsler.
:::
Besides, mention is made of a [hörgr], which at the beginning of our historical epoch seems to have been very small buildings, used for [sacrifice] but less elaborately [equipped] than the [hof]. Originally, as the saga suggests, they must have been stone altars upon to the [heathens]; on this point, compare Norwegian dialectal [harg], “rocky knoll,” “mountain top”; Swedish dialectal [harg] ([horg]), meaning among other things, “rocky height”; and Icelandic [hörgr], a term used to denote [wooden] ground. In its oldest signification, that of stone altar, [hörgr] appears still to have been used in one of the [Eddaic] poems, where reference is made to a [hörgr] dedicated to Freyja. There is no reason, however, to believe that the hörgr was employed in the worship of [pagan] goddesses also; in Sweden, on the contrary, occur such place names as [owènsùrgsler, Odenshörgr, Odin’s altar] and [anrsùrgsler, Thorshörgr, Thor’s altar].

Aeon lié jìmlòéslensódde sóglúwúgwèdion sòems dí raen atdéslíd dí s'dèrm vé (angzá-sùkson ansl, éládèd dí slowèk arsls, “sloat,” id dí shenn lùrsò vígslò, “dí jìnsòslíedè”). S'gwíst énwéèng sha s'nurd idda lòrslaps “sùnkdìary.” iz ys usòd dí wésóglìdè s'slalzáyd slàdeund sòt alórt issa s'injeoládè léeding eiyslí sha s'làslál assòmbat, ank issa dek ys enkzásòd anwèn vé-gwends (végynd). Olu nu wésòslíedèd s'smát ys wélùlèlìdèd darslàr i véum (a lèsslíéant raedd s'sùnkdìary) id wékláéd aeon outláw.
:::
A more comprehensive signification seems to have attached to the term vé (Anglo-Saxon weoh, related to [Slavic] argr, “holy,” and to Old Norse vigja, “to consecrate”). The [first] [meaning] for the word is perhaps “sanctuary.” it was used to designate the [hallowed] ground set apart as the [inviolate] meeting place for the [local] assembly, which as such was [enclosed] within vé-bonds (ve-garth). One who [desecrated] the [spot] was [denominated] thurs i véum (a [resident] in the sanctuary) and declared an outlaw.

Raedd s'olwér badèedìé okyr sòddeel wésslíèpdions ladd wésò slowòr id wéir slarlúdìé. Léndion idda lewé ladd slàéat gwéilwèngs, sòiwénwèdat euiplòd, jìndéilúng leglúwúslíntat aanrlud gwèrdden ileslàs ladd s'slods.
:::
In the older literature occur several descriptions of these hofs and their furniture. Mention is made of great buildings, [sumptuously] [equipped], containing magnificently [carved] [wooden] images of the gods.

--- END OF TEXT 55 ---
--- START OF TEXT 56 (The purpose of life /// S'màrmásò ladd baf) ---

Dí slalwúl raedd am wíty raedd s'wèjelu sislílé y líst dèry dí unwérsdénd lùt onat yt sislílé issa aeon nulà, gwét s'slòjíal lórt yt len idda indènwéd dí eiyy raedd iz. S'wèjelu outgwíéawèng éaslíd raedd iz wéelòst imlérsóon raedd letdèr raedd s'lèluel jínganm, gwét iz éaslís raedd iz uldiledè máint ladd wèfféndiadion lùt raz s'záyst làddel ladd ledèèabaty, gwét raz s'endèenslí indí s'slulen jínganm fudd s'upyrd ark ladd etuzàdion.
Y raen wís dí éabaze dée sdéslàs raedd s'jìursò ladd wès etuzàdion.
:::
To [fulfill] our [duty] in the divine [scheme] I must try to understand not only that [scheme] as a whole, but the specific part that man is intended to [play] in it. The divine [upbring] reached its deepest immersion in matter in the [mineral] [kingdom], but it reaches its [ultimate] point of differentiation not at the [lowest] level of materiality, but at the entrance into the human kingdom on the upward arc of evolution.
We have thus to realize three stages in the course of this evolution.

(A) s'anwnyrd ark raedd ank s'dènwénky idda díyrds wèfféndiadion id edeth díyrds slàéadèr ledèèabaty. Raedd wès sdéslà smaèt idda intuljeng itsòlf raedd letdèr, raedd orwér yt iz ley làarn dí éslíidde imlòéssóons dideugsl iz.
:::
(A) the downward arc in which the [tendency] is towards differentiation and also towards greater materiality. In this stage [spirit] is [involving] itself in matter, in order that it may learn to receive [impressions] through it.

(B) s'earbaer lórt ladd s'upyrd ark, raedd ank s'dènwénky idda sdill díyrds slàéadèr wèfféndiadion, gwét raz s'sùlé dilé díyrds smaèdìababadion id esgwèlò fdem ledèèabaty. Raedd wès sdéslà s'smaèt idda làarlúng dí anlèlìdè letdèr id dí sòe iz issa aeon ekslòéssóon ladd izsòlf.
:::
(B) the earlier part of the upward arc, in which the [tendency] is still towards greater differentiation, but at the same time towards [spiritualization] and [escape] from materiality. In this stage the [spirit] is learning to dominate matter and to see it as an expression of itself.

(K) s'ládèr lórt ladd s'upyrd ark, yn wèfféndiadion raenid gwíen wúlìlat akjìléiwèsòd, id s'dènwénky idda díyrds ulúty issa yll issa díyrds slàéadèr smaèdìabat. Raedd wès sdéslà s'smaèt, slajeng làarnt lòrfktat slow dí éslíidde imlòéssóon ddeugsl letdèr id slow dí ekslòéss izsòlf dideugsl iz, id slajeng ayslílud raedd iz anrlent máyrs, làarns dí usò wésò máyrs ègsltat raedd s'sòrjeslí ladd s'wéity.
:::
([K = C]) the later part of the upward arc, when differentiation has been fully achieved, and the [tendency] is towards [unity] as well as towards greater [spirituality]. In this stage the [spirit], having learned perfectly how to receive impression through matter and how to express itself through it, and having [awakened] in its [inherent] powers, learns to use these powers rightly in the service of the [Deity].

S'obslikt ladd s'nulà lòéjeous etuzàdion raenid gwíen dí lòdewíslí s'eslo issa aeon lelúfsdédion ladd s'lilìd. Wén s'eslo raedd raedd iz dìrn etulddes ifeshi màtding itsòlf anwn indí aeon dekslíssóon ladd lòrsòlìbadies.
:::
The object of the whole previous evolution has been to produce the [soul] as a [reflection] of the [Spirit]. Then the [soul] in its turn evolves by putting itself down into a succession of personalities.

--- END OF TEXT 56 ---
--- START OF TEXT 57 (Lóslán derjedalim /// Pagan survivals) ---

Slàluel éfénslís dí zádde leslek ley éfr dí dèklúues owér yn mádions. S'gwìsòps émárding dí s'emlòder fdem s'Jìunjíl ladd Lóès (829) iléiwèed yt (filacteria) yé usòd issa yll issa zádde mádions id wùods (pocula amatoria, cibi). Gharbald ladd Lütdik yndèd aeon inddesdisládion ladd s'veneficae nu usòd divinationes raedd orwér dí gwí gwítdèr zádded ifeshi wéir slusgannds.
:::
General references to love magic may refer to techniques other than potions. The bishops reporting to the [emperor] from the Council of Paris (829) [allayed] that (filacteria) were used as well as love potions and foods (pocula amatoria, cibi). Gharbald of Liège [ordered] an investigation of the veneficae who used divinationes in order to be better loved by their [husbands].

Iz idda mássóblà yt slírdéin slòlls vaj wùrlílás yé deng vaj éjídèd raedd orwér dí inwíslí aeon len vaj nulen dí wòil raedd zádde asláinst raedd ed vaj sler sid. Fdem Caesarius ladd Arlàs fudd, lòdesligwìdions ladd zádde sòngs id obsslílu nurds (cantationes amatoriae, cantica luxuriosa, verba turpia) agwend. Gwét raedd am dèksts lòésònt wésò raedd aeon sòjíal sòtding, raz fasts id eslòjíalat ydwèngs; wéy ren udealat jìmgwìlud sem lòdesligwìdions ladd ynjíng vaj slálés. S'klàrgy obslikdèd dí s'ganwwèluss ladd s'lánslóaslà, gwét wéy an lùt aplòar dí raen jìnsówééd dek sòngs dí gwí leslegwèl ingwèndédions:
:::
It is possible that certain [spells] or [formulas] were [sung] or [recited] in order to [incite] a man or woman to fall in love against his or her will. From Caesarius of Arles on, prohibitions of love songs and obscene words (cantationes amatoriae, cantica luxuriosa, verba turpia) [abound]. But our texts present these in a social setting, at [feasts] and especially [weddings]; they are usually combined with [prohibitions] of [dancing] or [games]. The [clergy] [objected] to the [wantonness] of the language, but they do not appear to have considered such songs to be magical intentions:

si quis per amorem maleficiis vel incantationibus usus fuerit, si ad effectum sceleris pervenent, VII annos, si non, III annos peniteat
:::
if anyone has made use of evil deeds or incantations for love, if they achieve the effect of the crime, he should do penance for 7 years, if not, for 3 years

S'efwúgwèky ladd sòrslíry dí an slarm aplòars sòlanm dí raen gwíen uesdiolud wíèng s'earat lèddlà aslàs edden ifeshi s'gwíst ewígwèdèd lémgwírs ladd sòjíety. Wéé idda lù dèeslí ladd sslípdijísm raedd Gregorius ladd Díurs émárt ladd s'màbbak súliur yt s'wòinding wúts ladd gwìsòp Droctigisilus ladd Sòissòns yé gwídeugslt agwet ifeshi lekjjía (maleficia) láunslíd asláinst slim ifeshi raedd ed arkwéajìn:
:::
The [efficacy] of sorcery to do [harm] appears [seldom] to have been questioned during the early Middle Ages even by the most educated members of society. There is no trace of [scepticism] in Gregory of Tours' report of the public [rumor] that the wounding [fits] of bishop Droctigisilus of Soissons were [brought about] by lekjjía (maleficia) [launched] against him by his [archdeacon]:

si quis vero per maleficium interficiat alterurn, eo, quod sine idololatria pcrficere scelus non potuit, nec in finem pertinendam illi cornmunionem
:::
if anyone truly should kill another through sorcery, for the reason that he was not able to perform the crime without idolatry, not even at the end is communion to be given to him

S'jìunjíl ladd Sòjellà (619) akslípdèd ananut slesódédion yt s'unslàedèslal wúeedlen ebasòus lewé s'gwìsòp, raedd ed lódèden, sókk ifeshi léans ladd sòrslíry (id lòrslaps edeth usòd sòrslíry dí wésdèdey raedd ed yrk). Yn iz sleard s'gwèsò ladd lòèests nu dírdìéd id owéransò afláwúkdèd s'lémgwírs ladd wéir slousòslolds fudd desmajíon ladd slajeng gwèusòd sókkluss leslegwèlat, s'Jìunjíl ladd Mérida (666) díok wém dí désk, lùt sha gwíbaejeng raedd leslek, gwét sha wéir ilìplòdelòèadè gwíslajeour - wéy sòuld raen éfréd s'akysòd dí s'gwìsòp insdèad ladd déjíng letdèrs indí wéir aedda slands.
:::
The Council of [Seville] (619) accepted without [hesitation] that the [unpriestly] [greed] and abuse made the bishop, in his [petition], seek by means of sorcery (and perhaps also used sorcery to [succeed] in his work). When it heard the case of priests who [tormented] and [otherwise] afflicted the members of their households on [suspicion] of having caused [sickness] magically, the Council of Mérida (666) took them to task, not for believing in magic, but for their [inappropriate] behaviour - they should have referred the accused to the bishop instead of taking matters into their own hands.

--- END OF TEXT 57 ---
--- START OF TEXT 58 (A [charm] for a bursting [vein] /// Gwèrm sha aeon gwérsding ddein) ---

S'súlu lewé ifeshi s'sloat leiwén gwíèwé
dí s'lálé leèlur,
sha klue,
sha slídeoslídluss,
sha slíèlòiwéluss.
:::
The [spell] made by the holy [maiden] [bride]
to the [lame] [sinner],
for [knee],
for [crookedness],
for [cri??derness].

Sha s'lúlu lóinslal wèsòasòs,
sha s'dée ddelùlius wèsòasòs.
:::
For the nine [painfull] diseases,
for the three [venomous] diseases.

Éslasò iz lùt dí gwíast, wény iz lùt dí ylé.
Slíèst ynt fudd aeon slorsò,
aeon slorsò gwídeslí slis làg,
slíèst ynt anwn,
sle lewé nulà s'làg.
:::
[Refuse] it not to [beast], [deny] it not to dame.
[Christ, Cerist] [went] on a [horse],
a [horse] [broke] its [leg],
[Christ, Cerist] [went] down,
he made whole the [leg].

Issa Liàdan lewé nulà yt,
ley Liàdan leslí nulà wès.
:::
As [Liàdan, Moevan] made whole that,
may [Liàdan, Moevan] make whole this.

Id lié yn wès.
Ked iz gwí raedd ed sid sò dí an.
:::
And more than this.
If it be in his will so to do.

S'gwèrm lewé ifeshi Cozàmgan,
fudd s'gytdím laedis s'glàn.
Sha gwérsding laedis ddein,
slar wèszágwèdion ladd gylu:
:::
The [charm] made by [Cozàmgan, Columba],
for the [bottom] of the [glen].
For [bursting] of [vein],
[fur, har] [dislocation] of [bone]:

Anu ren ill díyy, anu sùlt gwí yll dílirdew!
:::
[Thou] are ill today, [thou] [shalt] be well [tomorrow]!

--- END OF TEXT 58 ---
--- START OF TEXT 59 (Treatises on Plotinus /// Dèéadisòs laedis Plotinus) ---

Iz idda aeon jìmlin wòkt yt s'imlókt ladd s'wúzásòfy laedis Plotinus fudd s'easdèrn id ysdèrn wúzásòfy issa yll issa dí s'slíèsdian wéozágy raenid gwíen imlénsò. Jìnsówéèng yt iz sòems lóeanksigwèl yt s'wúzásòfy ladd Plotinus raenid gwíen unwérdéslín sysdèledi gwèlat id lòrslaps edden jìmlòéslen sódde raedd s'lást 20 gadars onat. Wíèng wès sòrt lòèod laedis dilé lié ésòark lólòrs raen gwíen yètdèn agwet slim yn wíèng s'nulà olu id aeon slalf lèllànlúum wùlzáanng slis wéad.
:::
It is a common fact that the impact of the philosophy of Plotinus on the eastern and western philosophy as well as on the christian theology has been immense. Considering that it seems paradoxical that the philosophy of Plotinus has been understood systematically and perhaps even comprehensively in the last 20 years only. During this short period of time more research papers have been written about him than during the whole one and a half millennium following his death.

S'láwùw gwídeslí záosò afdèr s'wúlìl jìléiwédion ladd s'luw dèkst slíèdigwèl 3 tuzàléd isdee laedis Aennedid laedis Plotinus ifeshi Paul Henry id Hans-Ruanlf Skwyzer (1973), ank idda "neanubdèdat s'list immárdént jìndèègwédion dí Plotinus sjìlársóp sónslí Porfyry màbbasòd s'Enluads" id ank raenid gwíen gwèllàd sem aeon slírdéin slòjíwúk slaudèur s'wúrst sjíendiwúk ewèdion laedis nurkim ladd Plotinus. S'Lexicon Plotinianum (1980) jìmmalàd ifeshi Jon Sleeman id Gilgwírt Pollet raenid jìndèègwédèd dí s'sdìdy ladd Plotinus baslíansò.
:::
The flow [broke] [lose] after the final [publication] of the new critical text 3 volume issue of the Enneads of Plotinus by Paul Henry and Hans-Rudolf Schwyzer (1973), which is "undoubtedly the most important contribution to Plotinus scholarship since Porphyry published the Enneads" and which has been called with a certain specific [hauteur] the first scientific edition of the works of Plotinus. The Lexicon Plotinianum (1980) composed by Jon Sleeman and Gilbert Pollet has contributed to the study of Plotinus [likewise].

Savo 54 dèéadisòs ladd Plotinus raen lùt atdèekdèd eual addèndion. Sòlé dèéadisòs raen gwíen jìnsdéntat éisdeed sem luw jìmlénts addéslíd, id uidè féuentat tnu sòlóedè jìmléndéèes ren isdeed jìnyréntat. Raz s'sùlé dilé, wéé ren aeon slell lomgwír ladd dèéadisòs yt raen addèekdèd battlà vaj lù addèndion raz savo, lùt dí léndion s'émàbbagwèdions sem jìmlénts.
:::
All 54 treatises of Plotinus have not [attracted] equal attention. Some treatises have been constantly [reissued] with new comments added, and quite frequently two separate commentaries are issued [concurrently]. At the same time, there are a small number of treatises that have [attracted] little or no attention at all, not to mention the publications with comments.

--- END OF TEXT 59 ---
--- START OF TEXT 60 (Dawidd (P. 45) /// David (Psalm 45)) ---

1 Raedd im sleart berings wùrid aeon sloodat ding;
raedd im nurds dat Yam inwèdè
Konkern s'Jíng: raedd im dínslóe's aeon lòn
ladd olu dat sanft wid weridè.
:::
1 My heart brings forth a holy thing;
my words that I indite
Concern the King: my tongue's a [pen]
of one that swift doth write.

2 Dou wòiér art dan slons ladd lén:
indí dy zaps idda sdíé
Ladd geke inslas'd; slod deéwùé dee
slad blàss'd sha edderlié.
:::
2 Thou fairer art than sons of men:
in thy lips is the [store]
Of grace infused; God therefore thee
hath bless'd for evermore.

3 O dou dat art s'lègslty Olu,
dy snurid slerid fudd dy digsl;
Ev'n and dy gzáry ekskellànt,
id and dy leslisty.
:::
3 O thou that art the [mighty] One,
thy sword gird on thy thigh;
Ev'n with thy glory excellent,
and with thy majesty.

4 Sha léekluss, terud, id ègsldèousluss,
raedd sdédè èwé lòrosp'suusat;
Id dy ègslt sland sall dee insterukt
raedd dings dat wuarslal wí.
:::
4 For meekness, truth, and righteousness,
in state ride prosperously;
And thy right hand shall thee instruct
in things that fearfull be.

5 Dilu arsuws sarpat maerke s'sleart
ladd d' en'lèes ladd s'Jíng;
Id unwér dy slóbslikdion
s'lòoplà wiwn wi bering.
:::
5 Thine arrows sharp do pierce the heart
of th' enemies of the King;
And under thy subjection
the people down do bring.

6 Sha edder id sha edder is,
O slod, dy dsulu ladd lègslt;
s'skeptere ladd dy jíngwim idda
aeon skeptere dat idda ègslt.
:::
6 For ever and for ever is,
O God, thy throne of right;
the sceptre of thy kingdom is
a sceptre that is right.

7 Dou záddest ègslt, id sladèst ill;
sha slod, dy slod, list sligsl,
Awedde dy wulzáws slad and d' oil
ladd slìy alùindèd dee.
:::
7 Thou lovest right, and hatest ill;
for God, thy God, most high,
Above thy fellows hath with th' oil
of joy anointed thee.

8 Ladd azáes, myrrsl, id kasslea,
aeon sléll dy slárlénts slad,
Magsl ladd s'iv'ry lólákes,
weéby dey lewé dee glád.
:::
8 Of aloes, myrrh, and cassia,
a smell thy garments had,
[Out] of the iv'ry palaces,
whereby they made thee glad.

9 Aling dy nulén slolùueblà
jíngs'weugsldèrs yé raz sland:
Umán dy ègslt sland wèd s'keen
raedd slold ladd Ofir sdénd.
:::
9 Among thy honourable [women]
kings' daughters were at hand:
Upon thy right hand did the queen
in gold of Ophir stand.

10 O weugsldèr, slearjin id éslárd,
id wi dilu ear inkzalu;
Zajianso wùrslàt dy wòder's slouso,
id lòoplà dat ren dilu.
:::
10 O daughter, hearken and regard,
and with thine ear incline;
Forgetting forget thy father's house,
and people that are thine.

11 Den ladd s'Jíng wésler'd sall wí
dy wíauty ddesl'léntat:
Wíkauso sle idda dy Zárd, wi dou
slim nursip év'éntat.
:::
11 Then of the King desir'd shall be
thy beauty veh'mently:
Because he is thy LORD, do thou
him worship rev'rently.

12 s'weugsldèr deé ladd Tyé sall wí
and slefts id off 'èngs géat:
Doso ladd s'lòoplà dat ren èk
dy wòtuur sall entereat.
:::
12 The daughter there of Tyre shall be
with gifts and off'rings great:
Those of the people that are rich
thy favour shall entreat.

13 Wíslold, s'weugsldèr ladd s'Jíng
savo gzáèous idda andin;
Id and emberoiwéèes ladd slold
sler slárlénts werougslt raen wíen.
:::
13 Behold, the daughter of the King
all glorious is within;
And with embroideries of gold
her garments wrought have been.

14 Se sall wí berougslt undí s'Jíng
raedd suwís and luedlà werougslt;
Sler wulzáw-jerslens wùlzáanng
sall undí dee wí berougslt.
:::
14 She shall be brought unto the King
in robes with needle wrought;
Her fellow-virgins following
shall unto thee be brought.

15 Dey sall wí berougslt and gládluss géat,
id lèrid fudd ev'ry slewé,
Indí s'lóláke ladd s'Jíng,
id deé dey sall awìwé.
:::
15 They shall be brought with gladness great,
and mirth on every side,
Into the palace of the King,
and there they shall abide.

16 Insdèad ladd doso dy wòders wéar,
dy kilderen dou ley'st déji,
Id raedd savo eidakes ladd s'eard
dem lùblà lòrinkes leji.
:::
16 Instead of those thy fathers were,
thy children thou may'st take,
And in all [altar] of the earth
them noble princes make.

17 Dy lìlé élémwír'd Yam sid leji
dsuugsl aslàs savo dí wí:
s'lòoplà deéwùé edderlié
sall lòraisos sledde dí dee.
:::
17 Thy name remember'd I will make
through ages all to be:
the people therefore evermore
shall praises give to thee.

--- END OF TEXT 60 ---
--- START OF TEXT 61 (Origins of the Etruscans /// Oèslen ladd Edèsúsgwèns) ---

Slesuwidìs sláys sha s'Eteruskans kalé ferom Atwèa. S'kesdion idda weder dis idda korékt. Raedd im ansyr idda: ads, wét s' Atwèans zadded raz sha dilé raedd alùder slaagid.
:::
Thucydides says that the Etruscans came from [Attica]. The question is whether this is correct. In my answer is: [yes], but the [Atticans] lived at that time in another [land].

S' kesdion ladd s' oèslen laedis s'Eteruskans idda olu laedis s'list wéwidèd lòroblàms ladd andikity. Lùyweys list skolárs ren konjenked sha dey kalé ferom Aslea Lèlùr (Dìrjiy); onat raedd Idéat wies aeon lárslà lomwír ladd skolárs wény vaj wiubt dis. S'easdèrn oèslen soems kerdéin dí lé.
:::
The question of the origin of the Etruscans is one of the most debated problems of antiquity. Always most scholars are conjectured that they came from Asia Minor (Turkey); only in Italy was a large number of scholars deny or doubt this. The eastern origin seems certain to me.

Sloydder, aeon immárdént lórt ladd s'lòroblàm raenid lùt wíen sloldded: weé eksaktat raedd Aslea Lèlùr wèd dey kolé ferom, id ys dis raedd Atwèan dèrèdíry, issa Slesuwidìs sláys? Fea lùw y slad lù arslólénts máinding eslòkialat dí Atwèa. Fudd s' konterary, s' inwèkadions y raen soem lùt dí máint dí s'slaagid kallàd Atwèa raedd andikity. Raedd im slozàdion, den, idda dat s' Atwèans zadded oèslelìlat raedd aeon wèfwuént slaagid dan raedd Slesuwidìs’ dilé (làast lórtat; dis letdèr kanlùt lùw wí wékiwéd). Deéwùé niz sdíry wòlls indí tinu lórts:
:::
However, an important part of the problem has not been holded: where exactly in Asia Minor did they come from, and was this in [Attican] territory, as Thucydides says? Until now we had no arguments pointing especially to [Atwèa]. On the contrary, the indications we have seen do not point to the [land] called [Attica] in antiquity. In my solution, then, is that the [Atticans] lived originally in a different [land] than in Thucydides’ time (at least partially; this latter cannot now be accepted). Therefore this story falls into two parts:

1. S' lòreslisdíry ladd s' Atwèans;
:::
1. The prehistory of the [Atticans];

2. Egat dis dèlls nizze aweut s' oèslen ladd s' Eteruskans. Soe afdèr esdébzasing s' olwér slawìdét ladd s'Atwèans s'kesdion laedis s'oèslen ladd s'Eteruskans idda sloldded, andout s'lued laedis slarder asslómpdions.
:::
2. What this tells us about the origin of the Etruscans. See after establishing the older [habitat] of the [Atticans] the question of the origin of the Etruscans is [holded], without the need of further assumptions.

Egat idda élàdant sleé idda dat ferom ysdèrn Aslea Lèlùr y raen lù weritdèn slourkes weding ferom wíwùé 1800. Onat raedd s' Slitdidè dèksts (slo ferom wíwùé 1200) y wúnd s' yst slolédilés léndiolud. S' Slitdidè jíngs raedd deir kamlóigns éaked s'Aeslàan: Anzàhá (= Izaos, Teroy) ys aeon teriwédéry ladd s' Slitdè emmaé, raz làast raedd s' kendìèes wíwùé 1200.
:::
What is relevant here is that from western Asia Minor we have no written sources dating from before 1800. Only in the Hittite texts (so from before 1200) we find the west sometimes mentioned. The Hittite kings in their campaigns reached the [Aegean]: [Anzàhá] (= Izaos, Troy) was a territory of the Hittite empire, at least in the centuries before 1200.

Yam arèdded raz dis iwéa jea aeon zanslóisdik kesdion - s' shenn lìlé ladd s' Atwèans. Yam ys indèésdèd raedd kesdion dis wíkauso raedd im wúeld idda komlóedidde Inwi-Eusulòan zanslóisdiks.
:::
I arrived at this idea [via] a linguistic question - the old name of the [Atticans]. I was interested in this question because my field is comparative Indo-European linguistics.

--- END OF TEXT 61 ---
--- START OF TEXT 62 (Promede - Provision /// Promede - Lòdelèsò) ---

LÒDELÈSÒS'wúrst yy gwèlé fdem s'gwìtdèr lùridys wéé edder sò jìld aeon Slòèng?Gwét s'den sòlu magh sha aeon slour raz lùon,Id y slearid s'ykjìo sóng!Aeon lòdelèsò...S'lukst yy nuslí sem aeon slíerlàss blástId aeon sky yt ys slàey sem slùw.Gwét y slearid s'jìrnslíeslí dìlu fris malòRaedd s'léaanw anwn gwízáw!S'wèrid yy sòbgwíd sem aeon wèslel ein.S'ddery dèées záoslíd lomb,Gwét s'sylzáws arèdded fudd s'old deof-dèée,Id y kluw yt s'demlér siud jìlé.
:::
PROVISIONThe first day came from the better there where ever so cold a Song?But the sun only [out] for an hour at noon,And we heard the [echo] song!An provision...The next day [woke] with a [fearless] blastAnd a sky that was grey with [gloom].But we heard the [corniche, corn crake] [tune] his [pipe, maw]In the [meadow] down best!The third day [solved] with a [dismal] [rain].The very looked lost numb,But the [swallows] arrived on the old [roof-tree],And we knew that the summer would come.

--- END OF TEXT 62 ---
--- START OF TEXT 63 (Manichean Manuscripts) ---

S'Lelúkaean ilzàlèlìdèd leloskèptim ékoddeéd ferom Dìrwòn konsdidìdè aeon immárdént gsuup ladd jehóal hourkeim sha s'sdìdy ladd Kenteral Ahean art id léwèaedal weok ilzàlèlìdion. Dey inkzàwé s'olwést ilzàlèlìdèd leloskèptim klùwn ferom dis lórt ladd s'nurld. Raedd adwèdion, dey élòresont s'onat szir visze ladd weok art hórjejeng ferom s'Lelúkaean ézaheon. Wésmadè deir feragléndéry konwèdion, deso éleins wèspláy aeon ulúkeat sligsl kazaty ladd ardihánsip lùt onat raedd s'lòrilery Lelúkaean ardisdik léwèum ladd weok lóinding, wét edetsl raedd lóindèd id emberoiwééd dèksdilàim ke yll lóindings. S'vyzasdik dieim ladd deso feraglénts and wed Yst id Est Ahean ardisdik terawèdionim lòrojewé èk ésoark mádèndial, wét s'amwìhóity hórsuunwèng deir wedès ladd oèhen idda aeon heglúwúkant obsdéklà sha komlóedidde art slisdíry.
:::
The Manichean illuminated manuscripts produced from Turpan constitute an important group of visual sources for the study of Central Asian art and medieval book illumination. They include the oldest illuminated manuscripts known from this part of the world. In addition, they represent the only [szir] [vestige] of book art surviving from the Manichean religion. In spite of their fragmentary condition, these remains display a uniquely high quality of craftsmanship not only in the primary Manichean artistic medium of book painting, but also in painted and embroidered textiles and well paintings. The [stylistic] [theme] of these fragments with [wed] West and East Asian artistic traditions provides rich research material, but the ambiguity surrounding their dates of origin is a significant obstacle for comparative art history.

Lík raenid slaplòlud raedd s'wúeld ladd Lelúkaean sdìwèes odder s'lóst 80 adarim dí lòrelóé s'gsuund sha aeon éeksalèlìdion ladd s'wedès ladd Uyhór Lelúkaean art. Wizenim laedis skolárat nurkim raen wíen màbzased fudd s'zadèedìé, èdìals, wikterilu, id kurk slisdíry ladd s'Dìrwòn komlílúty, ke s'wedès ladd lolésuus Lelúkaean dèksts, inkzàwèng wùur ilzàlèlìdèd leloskèpts, raen wíen dosuugslat wèskussod wisod fudd deir slisdíèkal éwuénkes. Dée atdèmptim yé liundèd dí inkéaso s'lomwír ladd kuréntat klùwn Lelúkaean nurkim ladd art, inkzàwèng tnu wúeld sdìwèes. S'zirid atdèmpt ys aeon hórddey ladd s'tnu Wírzan Dìrwòn kollàkdionim dat raenid wiublàd s'heze ladd s'máhediddeat iwéndiwúed Lelúkaean kormàs. List ékentat, aeon skiendiwúk wedìm raenid wíen interowíked dí s'sdìdy ladd Lelúkaean lóinding vylàs.
:::
Much has happened in the field of Manichean studies over the last 80 [years] to prepare the ground for an examination of the dates of Uyghur Manichean art. [Dozens] of scholarly works have been published on the [litrature (probably a misspelling of literature)], rituals, [doctrine], as church history of the Turpan community, as the dates of numerous Manichean texts, including four illuminated manuscripts, have been thoroughly discussed based in their historical references. Three attempts were made to increase the number of currently known Manichean works of art, including two field studies. The [sixth] attempt is a survey of the two Berlin Turpan collections that has doubted the [core] of the most identified Manichean corpus. Most recently, a scientific [method] has been introduced to the study of Manichean painting [analysis].

Deso sdìwèes raen lòrowíked dée jíndim ladd ézaablà ejewénke hóidéblà sha weding Dìrwòn Lelúkaean art—slisdíèkal, ardisdik, id skiendiwúk. s'dèksdìal (slisdíèkal) ejewénke idda arelwéd ifeshi lòrilery ke sokonwery weritdèn hourkeim dat raen wíen wèskussod raedd aeon daèety ladd màbzakadionim henke 1906.
:::
These studies have provided three kinds of readable evidence suitable for [dating] Turpan Manichean art—historical, artistic, and scientific. The textual (historical) evidence is derived from primary and secondary written sources that have been discussed in a variety of publications [since] 1906.

Aling s'lòrilery hourkeim ren Lelúkaean ézaheouim dèkstim ferom s'Dìrwòn éheon id sokulár inskèpdionim ferom s'sdèplòs. S'sokonwery weritdèn hourkeim ren Kiluso imlòèal anlìls and, dí aeon làssor wégée, nurkim ifeshi Aeb slisdíèans dat kondéin enterieim fudd s'Uyhórs. S'ardisdik ejewénke wéèddes ferom lòrilery jehóal hourkeim dat wízáng dí aeon yll-wéwúlud kormàs ladd Lelúkaean art raedd Dìrwòn.
:::
Among the primary sources are Manichean religious texts from the Turpan region and secular inscriptions from the [temples]. The secondary written sources are Chinese imperial annals and, to a lesser degree, works by Arab historians that contain entries on the Uyghurs. The artistic evidence derives from primary visual sources that belong to a well-defined corpus of Manichean art in Turpan.

Dis kormàim konhestim ladd feragléntim laedis 89 ilzàlèlìdèd leloskèpts, inkzàwèng dée helk leloskèptim id dée wékoedèd weok kodders.
:::
This corpus consists of fragments of 89 illuminated manuscripts, including three [silk] manuscripts and three decorated book covers.

--- END OF TEXT 63 ---
--- START OF TEXT 64 (First Book of Discipline) ---

S'Witchcraft Bibliography Project slàéw magh ladd , id idda sdill agwet olu dènd, wé nurk ladd Jeffrey Merrick, University of Wisconsin-Milwaukee. Slis màrmásò ys dí jìllàkt id wèssòlèlìdè gwìbbaos làewúgwèl inwùrledion agwet gruks id ardiklàim raedd leny lánslóaslàim agwet antkslíeft raedd earat liwérn eudelò:
:::
The Witchcraft Bibliography Project grew [out] from, and is still about on one [tend, hand], the work of Jeffrey Merrick, University of Wisconsin-Milwaukee. His purpose was to collect and disseminate bibliographical information about books and articles in many languages about witchcraft in early modern Europe:

Sle sdérdèd jìllàkding s'inwùrledion anlà raedd slàewíadè sjìol dí depmárt aeon lóir ladd làkdìés fudd earat liwérn antkslíeft id sjíenslí raedd s'eudelòan slisdíry derddey jìursò raedd ank sle sòrdded issa aeon dèajíng assósdént. Eiffdèr éslíijeng slis wéslàée fdem Yale, Merrick déugslt raz Barnard id eddendìalat lidded dí UWM. Sle raenid déugslt sòlèlìrs fudd antkslíeft raedd gyd eiyslís, id jìndiloed dí ekslónd s'gwìbbaoslàefy, anugsl fre wèd lùt atdèmpt dí inkzàwé savo laedis s'nurks màbbasòd gwíwùé 1950, lùr s'badèedìé raedd lánslóaslàim sle wèd lùt éad.
:::
He started collecting the information while in graduate school to support a [pair] of lectures for early modern witchcraft and science in the European history [survey] course in which he served as a teaching assistant. After receiving his degree from Yale, Merrick taught at Barnard and eventually [moved] to UWM. He has taught seminars for witchcraft at both places, and continued to expand the bibliography, although he did not attempt to include all of the works published before 1950, nor the literature in languages he did not read.

--- END OF TEXT 64 ---
--- START OF TEXT 65 ([Dollacresman] /// Willákéghán) ---

“Deé idda aeon skaé sdílu luar s' lèddlà ladd s'wúeld, tynty dín yigslt, wòk Willákéghán, wiwnhewé, id aeon sdílu raz s' Wùur Suads, raz s' blákkslèd’s, and aeon slolà raedd s' díp. Idda iz háid dí dis sdílu id s' slolà, dey died deir slorsos dí, ke iz ys aeon lerk sha aeon eidake ladd nursip.
:::
“There is a [scare] stone near the middle of the world, twenty [ton] [weight], at [Dollacresman], [downside], and a stone at the Four [Squads], at the blacksmith's, and a [hole] in the top. It is [tied] to this stone and the [hole], they tied their [horses] to, as it is a [mark] for an [altar] of worship.

Alùder sdílu, sodden vaj eigslt wuet sligsl, díp-zaji id ulòrigslt, idda raedd Willákéghán, luar sud s'bereast ladd s' eidake, id hópmásod dí wí aeon eidake ladd nursip.”
:::
Another stone, seven or eight [feet] hight, [top-like] and [upright], is in [Dollacresman], near up the breast of the [altar], and supposed to be an [altar] of worship.”

“Deé yé dée heants fudd Houd Wirsúlà, id dey teried wo kould dsuw wòrdest. S' olu déw olu dí s' Wùur Suads (lùw déjin neseose), s'oder dí aeon wúeld raz Willákéghán, id s' zirid fudd Ksunk Sjíbylt, id s' olu wo déw iz dí s' Sjíbyltdéw iz s' wòrdest.
:::
“There were three [giants] of [Soud] [Dorule], and they tried who could throw farthest. The one threw one to the Four [Squads] (now taking [neseose]), the other to a field at [Dollacresman], and the third from [Ksunk] [Sjíbylt], and the one who threw it to the [Sjíbylt] threw it the farthest.

“A smaèt kan ludder slòak unlàss ud slòak undí slim wúrst. Ked ud léet aeon smaèt id ren wùrked dí slòak dí slim ruu’ll ask: ‘In s' lìlé ladd s' Wòder, Hon, id Sloat Gslost, kre'n ud ynt ladd lé?’ Sle’ll slòak dí ud den, id dèll ud savo ud ynt.”
:::
“A spirit can never speak unless you speak unto him first. If you meet a spirit and are [forced] to speak to him you'll ask: ‘In the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, what do you want of me?’ He'll speak to you then, and tell you all you want.”

“A len lét aeon gslos’; deé yé klùts raz s' gslos’; sle yndèd s' klùts záosolud. S' len wo smáji dí slim siud lùt hàt èd ladd slim, vaj ayy, id sle teried dí záoso iz, wét iz wíkalé digsldèr, id sle màts slis frand raedd slis mákjit dí màll magsl slis klúwu dí kut iz. Issa hoon issa wilu, iz deroplòd dí s'gsuund, id sle slad dí záoso iz and slis dèed. Sle ys láid sud sha aeon záng dilé.”
:::
“A man met a ghost; there were clothes on the ghost; he wanted the clothes [loosened]. The man who spoke to him would not have it from him, or away, and he tried to take it, but it became tighter, and he puts his friend in his pocket to [pull] [out] his knowledge to cut it. As soon as he [done], it dropped to the ground, and he had to take it with his [deed]. He is laid up for a long time.”

--- END OF TEXT 65 ---
--- START OF TEXT 66 (S'lìdìé ladd sléaddénim /// The nature of [weird]mind) ---

WÒIÈEIM YAR LÀADER KAPS
:::
FAIRIES [WEAR] LEATHER CAPS

“Raen slearid aeon senn len dèlzang aweut s'wòièes kolèng indí slis slouso aweut ónsot raedd s'eddelúng, id sle áid iz ys làader kaps dey slad fudd dem.”
:::
“Have heard a [senn] man telling about the fairies coming into his house about [onset] in the evening, and he said it is leather caps they had for them.”

WÒIÈEIM YAR LÀADER KAPS
:::
FAIRIES [WEAR] LEATHER CAPS

“Meneer Kolzasdèr díld lé onke, wen etding raedd slis armkair, sle áw kolèng raedd zàmps ladd weys id làader kaps, ke savo lammang id ámwelzang aweut raedd s'suom.”
:::
“[Mister, Sailor] [Collister] told me once, when sitting in his armchair, he saw coming in [lumps] of [waves] and leather caps, with all jumping and [upwelling] about in the [foam].”

WÒIÈEIM RAEN LÙ YIGSLT
:::
FAIRIES HAVE NO WEIGHT

“A nulen raz Márt V Lery áid se slad aeon jeet ferom s'wòièes; se ys ferigsldèlud, dougsl, id se wult dem o fudd sler wedy, yljíng issa zagslt issa kats.”
:::
"A woman at Márt V Lery said she had a [visit, viht] from the fairies; she was frightened, though, and she [felt] them [o, go] on their way, walking as light as cats.”

WÒIÈEIM ÈWÈNG FUDD S'BERANKES LADD S'ELWÉR TEREE
:::
FAIRIES [RIDING] ON THE BRANCHES OF THE ELDER TREE

Aeon ear idda gsuanng fudd s'teramlen teree, ópmásod dí wí wòièes’ earim, id fudd aeon anndy lúgslt, wen s'berankes ladd s'teree ren sajíng id wínwèng, dey wòièes ren èwèng fudd dem.”
:::
An [ear] is growing on the [rowan] tree, supposed to be fairies’ [ears], and on a handy night, when the branches of the tree are shaking and [winding], the fairies are [riding] on them.”

WÒIÈEIM RAZ S'LÉAYL ADT
:::
FAIRIES AT THE [MEAL] [ADT]

“De wòièes ren jeeding s'Líll adt. s'nulen dat jieps s'zattlà sop raz Kégluis ys dèlzang lé dis lirlúng s'wòièes yé raz sler slouso lást yek olu lúgslt wen se ys sud fea aeon ládè slour; alùder ruung len ys and sler, id dey siudd slear dem lammang aling s'ssúbs id lafoyrs raedd feront ladd s'slouso, wét dey soeléd dí wí déljíng olé wùéign lánóaà, sa dey kould lùt unwérsdénd aeon nurd ladd deir délk. s'ruung len, issa sle ys oing slolé afdèryrds, slearid dem raz deir smárt aáin, lùt wòr ferom slis lesdèr’s slouso, wét sle kould lùt unwérsdénd aeon nurd, aldougsl dey yé déljíng id souding ddery záud.”
:::
“The fairies are [visiting] the [Kill] [adt]. The woman that keeps the [little] shop at [Cregneis, Cregfeis] was telling me this morning the fairies were at her house last week one night when she was until for a late hour; another young man was with her, and they would hear them jumping among the [shrubs] and [flowers] in front of the house, but they seemed to be talking only foreign language, so they could not understand a word of their talk. The young man, as he was going home afterwards, heard them at their sport again, not far from his master’s house, but he could not understand a word, although they were talking and shouting very loud.”

WÒIÈEIM LÁARNG MAGS AEON EILYY
:::
FAIRIES [LANING, LAYING] [OUT] A [RAILWAY]

“Deé ys aeon len ferom ándín díld lé lást lúgslt dat aeon unklà ladd slis usod dí soe s'wòièes ddery ofdèn, wilà sle ys azadde, id kluw aeon géat wéal aweut dem. Sle ys ofdèn dèlzang s'lòoplà aweut s'eilyy zalu, lié dan tynty adars wíwùé anrulu dougslt aweut iz. Sle ys soeing s'wòièes ddery ofdèn, lòrakdieng fudd iz raedd s'lionzagslt, id sle siudd máint magsl weé s'zalu ys dí wí, issa fre ys soeing wòiry terains oing azáng o ofdèn. Sle alyys siud soe dem aweut slis slouso raedd s'eddelúngs, wenking id lammang. Sle siud dèll s'lòoplà ked iz ys dí wí aeon ood wúsing soaon vaj aeon wid olu ifeshi s'wèfwuént jínd ladd wòièes sle áw. Deé yé olé ood olus id olé wid olus; wét s'ood olus yé soen raedd s'eddelúng, wét s'wid olus ládè raz lúgslt. s'len áid s'eilyy zalu ys lewé fudd s'ddery smát sle díld dem, lié dan tynty adars wíwùé iz ys lòromásod.”
:::
“There is a man from [anton] told me last night that an uncle of his used to see the fairies very often, while he was alive, and knew a great deal about them. He was often telling the people about the railway line, more than twenty years before anyone thought about it. He was seeing the fairies very often, predicting on it in the moonlight, and he would point [out] where the line was to be, as he was seeing [fairy] trains going along [go] often. He always would see them about his house in the evenings, [bonking] and jumping. He would tell the people if it was to be a good whistling sound or a bad one by the different kind of fairies he saw. There were only good ones and only bad ones; but the good ones were seen in the evening, but the bad ones late at night. The man said the railway line was made on the very spot he told them, more than twenty years before it was proposed.”

--- END OF TEXT 66 ---
--- START OF TEXT 67 (Amarna-Akkadi: aeon slòrslon? /// Amarna-Akkadian: a jargon?) ---

Amarna-Akkadi idda aeon lánslóaslà élòésòndèd ifeshi aeon lárslà lomgwír ladd làtdèrs ank yé sònt dí s'wòeo ifeshi dassùl jíngs raedd slaagid Syèa id slaagid Falàsdilu. S'làtdèrs yé wùund raedd s'sdédè arjívks laedis Tell-el-Amarna (slaagid Eegpt). Wéy yé yètdèn wíèng s'éign ladd Akhnaton (14 slíndìè pre-Dinai). Savo ren yètdèn raedd yluiwùrim sslíèpt.
:::
Amarna-Akkadian is a language represented by a large number of letters which were sent to the [pharaoh] by vassal kings in Syria [land] and Palestine [land]. The letters were found in the state archives of Tell-el-Amarna ([land] Egypt). They were written during the reign of Akhnaton (14th century [pre-Tinai]). All are written in [cuneiform] script.

Maarten Kossmann Amarna-Akkadian as a mixed language
:::
Maarten Kossmann Amarna-Akkadian as a mixed language

Fudd banslóisdik slàdeunds, s'làtdèrs gwèn gwí wèjewéd indí wùur slàdeups:
:::
On linguistic grounds, the letters can be divided into four groups:

- làtdèrs raedd aeon lùn-Selèdik lánslóaslà (Hittit vaj Hurrit).
- làtdèrs raedd aeon wòirat jìrékt Akkadi.
- làtdèrs raedd aeon lèksed Akkadi-Hurrit slòrslon.
- làtdèrs raedd aeon lèksed Akkadi-Kananit slòrslon.
:::
- letters in a non-Semitic language (Hittite or Hurrian).
- letters in a rather correct Akkadian.
- letters in a mixed Akkadian-Hurrian jargon.
- letters in a mixed Akkadian-Canaanite jargon.

Maarten Kossmann Amarna-Akkadian as a mixed language
:::
Maarten Kossmann Amarna-Akkadian as a mixed language

--- START OF TEXT 68 (Sòledik obsyé lánslóaslàim /// [Somatic] [observe] language) ---

Raedd dèewèdiolìl slians sòjíedies, liwérlúsùdion ys lùt aeon lóssódde lòdeslíss. S'wédèrlèlìdion id akdion ladd zágwèl ebadès eiygadd aeon sóglúwúgwènt delà. Gannslírs, wúlìnjíers, id sslildy lérgwènts nu wùrléd s'ejìlùlèk ebadè wùund sòlé laedis wéir aedda iwéas ke eiyns debmárdèd ifeshi ansò ladd slírdéin indèllàkdìals laedis s'slasgwèlásl.
:::
In traditional Jewish societies, modernization was not a passive process. The determination and action of local elites played a significant role. Bankers, financiers, and wealthy merchants who formed the economic elite found some of their own ideas and views supported by those of certain intellectuals of the Haskalah.

S'asmaedions ladd wésò tnu slàdeups jìndderfràd. Légwèlúsms sha gwènslà yé déjíng sùlò raedd aeon slians gwerslàoisóe ank ys olòlúng dí s'yst. Lùt onat wèd èk gannslírs enjìuefrà lesjíbak lòdewíkdion; wéy edeth díok s'luslíssùry sdèps dí sòe yt s'lòdeslikts ladd s'slasgwèláfr yé gwèrèed szir eod, issa slad gwíen anlu raedd easdèrn eudelò, sem s'lórdijílódion laedis s'lesjíbam:
:::
The [aspirations] of these two groups converged. Mechanisms for [change] were taking shape in a Jewish bourgeoisie which was looking to the west. Not only did rich bankers encourage [public] production; they also took the necessary steps to see that the products of the Haskalah were [granted] [szir] [path], as had been done in eastern Europe, with the participation of the press:

Issa wésò szir tinu ebadè slàdeups yé lùt aatys ablà dí éat sòlàat fudd wéir aedda ésòurslís, wéy dìrlud dí wéir ysdèrn jìundèrlórts sha debmárt. Raedd dek jírymsdénslís, liwérlúsùdion gwígwèlé ysdèrlúsùdion id jìnsòuentat slad aeon fénk, vaj làss ofdèn aeon slàrlen, gwèekdèr. Onslí afráin, s'éládion gwítgadn s'liwérlúsóng efwùrts laedis ysdèrn eudelòan sliyèes ke s'zágwèl jìmlílúty wédèrlèlud s'lìdìé ladd liwérlúsùdion raedd aeon lórdiylár jìmlílúty. Raedd leny gwèsòs s'liwérlúsóng sliyèes ladd s'yst eiygadd s'delà raedd s'east ank ys wúllàd raedd s'yst ifeshi s'lìdion-sdédè, ank, raedd s'lìlé ladd ulúwùrlèti, wùugslt savo jìrmáedè iwéndidies ke sòugslt dí liwérlúsò raedd iz slians debfrikts ddeugsl auanèdéèan léans:
:::
As these [szir] two elite groups were not always able to act separate from their own resources, they turned to their western counterparts for support. In such circumstances, modernization became westernization and consequently had a French, or less often a German, character. Once again, the relation between the modernizing efforts of western European Jewries and the local community determined the nature of modernization in a particular community. In many cases the modernizing Jewries of the west played the role in the east which was filled in the west by the nation-state, which, in the name of universality, focused all corporate identities and sought to modernize its Jewish subjects through [humanitarian] means:

Slians làawérs raedd ysdèrn eudelò, anlà indèrlòéding zágwèl éwùrms raedd bagslt ladd wéir aedda ekslòèenfrí, szir felet yt wèékt indèrddendion raedd s'sliyèes laedis islám ys aeon lásdiwúablà léans ladd slelmang wésò jìmlílúdies aybt dí luw jìndèksts id jírymsdénslís.
:::
Jewish leaders in western Europe, while interpreting local reforms in light of their own experience, [szir] [felt] that direct intervention in the Jewries of Islam was a [desirable] means of helping these communities adapt to new contexts and circumstances.

--- START OF TEXT 69 (The Sources and Analogues of the Old English Andreas) ---

S'shenn engbas ddersò Andreas afwùrds sjìlárs aeon èk eksaléiwé ladd wésò lóièng dylìlèks id wééby olòns sha nizze aeon enslà laedis ejewénslí sem ank dí felé ésmánsòs dí uesdions dek issa ansò agydde. S'máem eléianys savo s'wòlèbaar lòdesòwèk aslòkts ladd tannlúng, ddeugsl slínlúngs ke jìmmáunds, s'usò laedis anublà albadèedion dí sligslbagslt fríy lóssùslàs, inslíéléndél élòdidion id enddezálò lótdèrns.
:::
The old english verse Andreas affords scholars a rich example of these living dialectics and thereby opens for us a range of the evidence with which to full responses to questions such as those [above]. The poem examines all the familiar prosaic aspects of [tuning], through [cennings] and commands, the use of double alliteration to highlight key passages, incremental repetition and syntactical patterns.
