--- START OF TEXT 71 (S'zást slaagidim ladd s'Yst - Book of Earths /// The lost [lands] of the West - Book of Earths) ---

Wét deé idda alùder obskué terawèdion ladd aeon wúfd kadésterofe wik soems dí raen andèwedèd s'sónjíng ladd Atlándis. Dis konkerns alùder zást kondilunt raedd s'Lókiwúk Okean, aeon géat kadésterofe raedd s'ysdèrn ydèrs. Shenn ékords ren èddlàd and alzàsóons dí dalúsed Slaagidim id lòoplàs laedis s'Yst. Shenn Azdèk id Lesslin ékords; s'Asóadik ékords slòak laedis s'dalúsed slaagidim id lòoplàs laedis s'Yst. Sho shenn terawèdion ablòaéd aeonid raedd liwérn dilés dsuugsl s'asdempdion ladd Skládèr, aeon Engzaslen soejíng sha frolé zást zankim sha záng, záng aslo aeon géat sloudern kondilunt láy veretked aweut s'Sloud Málà ddery lík issa s'kondilundél slaagid díwey dersuunds s'arkdik bàlu.
:::
But there is another obscure tradition of a fifth catastrophe which seems to have antedated the sinking of Atlantis. This concerns another lost continent in the Pacific Ocean, a great catastrophe in the western waters. Old records are [riddled] with allusions to vanished [Lands] and peoples of the West. Old Aztec and [Lesslin] records; the Asiatic records speak of the vanished [lands] and peoples of the West. So old tradition appeared [first] in modern times through the assumption of Sclater, an Englishman [positing] that for long, long ago a great southern continent lay [stretched] about the South Pole very like as the continental [land] today surrounds the arctic [line].

Sle lìléd dis kondilunt Làlíèa, dí wúks lié wúrmat deéby slis depmásódion dat fudd dek aeon kondilunt alúlels ladd s'Làlísuid tylò líst raen wíen wéddezálòd. Iz idda aeon kuèous insdénke raedd skiendiwúk slisdíri dat wen Ernst Haekjil, list ledèèal ladd ledèèazasdik skiendistim, kalé umán dis Làlíèan slymádesós, sle lòromptat inkormáedèd iz indí slis aedda nurjíng skelé, id raedd slis s'Slisdíri ladd Kéadion id S'Etuzàdion ladd Len sle slòaks ladd Làlíèan kéadìés id Làlíèan terakes issa ked s'eksisdènke ladd dek aeon slaagid slad wíen aléady skiendiwúkalat lòrodded.
:::
He named this continent Lemuria, to fix more firmly thereby his [supposition] that on such a continent animals of the Lemurian type must have been developed. It is a curious instance in scientific history that when Ernst Haeckel, most material of materialistic scientists, came upon this Lemurian hypothesis, he promptly incorporated it into his own working scheme, and in his The History of Creation and The Evolution of Man he speaks of Lemurian [creators] and Lemurian [traces] as if the existence of such a [land] had been already scientifically proven.

Slis eksplálìdion, wik wòilàd dí sládisfi savo skiendistim, ys sha s'Làlíèan dilékyklà ys s'onat debimásláblà ding dat eksbiláilud kerdéin oderanso iluksbzakablà sláps raedd s'etuzàdiolìry deor:
:::
His explanation, which failed to satisfy all scientists, was for the Lemurian [time-cycle] was the only supposable thing that explained certain otherwise inexplicable gaps in the evolutionary [suor, sour]:

Id reven nefe rem nereven:
:::
And [heaven] [nefe] [rem] [nereven]

--- START OF TEXT 72 (Nan: The English Inheritance of Biblical Verse) ---

2008, PDF 241 láonslàim
:::
2008, PDF 241 pages

Wès gruk ekseianés s'dèenslèssóon ladd ládè andiue ládin gwìbbagwèl máedèry dí slaagid Englánd id s'debsòuent wéddezáplént ladd s'slàné raedd s'dderlìylár. Wès sdìdy offrs kzásò éawèngs ladd s'list immárdént jìndèègwédions dí aeon slàné yt lòdewíslíd lié yn tynty leslìr jìmmásódions gwítgadn 400 Dinai id 1500. Sha odder aeon lèlàlúum, wès badèedìé effkdiddeat élòésòndèd s'gwìblà raedd mámàlár wùrim, gadt wès idda s'wúrst sdìdy dí ekseiané s'stybasdik id wéledik afwúlúdies gwítgadn s'ládin ke engbas dèewèdions:
:::
This book investigates the transmission of late antique Latin biblical poetry to Anglo-Saxon England and the subsequent development of the genre in the vernacular. This study offers close readings of the most important contributions to a genre that produced more than twenty major compositions between 400 [Tinai] and 1500. For over a millennium, this literature effectively represented the Bible in popular form, yet this is the first study to examine the stylistic and thematic affinities between the Latin and english traditions:

Raedd s'gwíslenlúng s'slods slíéadèd freadden id earid.
Id s'earid ys tuid ke szir folmagh id yrkluss
ys umán s'wòslí ladd s'wéep;
id s'smaèt ladd s'slods lidded odder s'ydèrs.
Id s'slods sùid: gwí bagslt lewé.
Ke bagslt ys lewé.
Id s'slods sùw s'bagslt yt iz ys slood;
ke wéy wèjewéd s'bagfrt fdem s'yrkluss.
Id wéy gwèllàd s'bagslt yy ke s'yrkluss lúgslt;
id wéé ys eddelúng id
lirlúng olu yy.
:::
In the beginning God created heaven and earth.
And the earth was void and [szir] [empty] and darkness
was upon the face of the deep;
and the spirit of God moved over the waters.
And God said: be light made.
And light was made.
And God saw the light that it was good;
and they divided the light from the darkness.
And they called the light day and the darkness night;
and there was evening and
morning one day.

Aeon sólèlár slíèsdian jìndèkst sha wélés laedis eksilà gwèn edeth gwí wùund raedd Cynewulf Wòdès laedis s'amástlàim vaj Juliana, yé s'auanr usòs s'sùlé lánslóaslà dí ileslelu s'lòdeslíss ladd slis aedda wéad. Raedd wésò sslílus, wéad idda bafríansò aeon slìurluy raedd ank s'sòul id gydy ren eksilàs ladd sòrts, wéèdden fdem lirdél eksisdènslí ke wùrslíd dí ynwér s'afdèrbaf fea s'yy laedis ládslàlént:
:::
A similar Christian context for themes of exile can also be found in Cynewulf's Fates of the Apostles or Juliana, where the author uses the same language to illustrate the process of his own death. In these [scenes], death is [likewise] a journey in which the soul and body are exiles of sorts, driven from mortal existence and forced to enter the afterlife until the day of judgment:

--- END OF TEXT 72 ---
--- START OF TEXT 73 (Wið færsdislí - Wið færstice) ---

Wès dèkst, klùwn lùw issa Wið færsdislí, idda aling s'list élergwèblà ladd raedd iz jínd raedd léwèedal Eudelò. Lòdelèlunt aling s'déats ank iz sòeks dí jìundèr ren ælfe, s'gwíings nusò lìlé raenid jìlé indí liwérn Engbas issa elddes. S'sòèousluss sem ank Wið færsdislí, id lòédelebat raedd iz elàddend-slíndìè jìparst, dèéats wésò gwíings gwèllànslàs raedd am jìnslípdions laedis ediolìbaty id éabaty, ladd sleald, sleabang ke fríèsdialúty. Yt yé ælf? Yt yé gescotu, id wy wèd ælf gwèusò wém? Wslat yé s'ese ke Hægtessan sem ank wéy ren assòjíadèd id wy yé wéy slàdeulòd raedd wès yy?
:::
This text, now known as Wið færsdislí, is among the most remarkable of its kind in medieval Europe. Prominent among the threats which it seeks to counter are ælfe, the beings whose name has come into modern English as elves. The seriousness with which Wið færsdislí, and presumably in its eleventh-century [context], treats these beings challenges our conceptions of [rationality] and reality, of health, [healing] and christianity. What are ælf? What are gescotu, and why did ælf cause them? What were the ese and Hægtessan with which they are associated and why were they [grouped] in this way?

Liéodder, alanugsl ulúue raedd leny éslòkts, Wið færsdislí idda onat olu laedis aeon enfrà ladd angzá-sùkson dèksts usóng s'nurid ælf, id wésò dío gwíèng gyd ansyrs ke uesdions.
:::
Moreover, although unique in many respects, Wið færsdislí is only one of a [range] of anglo-saxon texts using the word ælf, and these [too] bring both answers and questions.

S'Angbaan wùrim ælf idda s'udeal jídédion wùrim sha s'elf nurid raedd shenn Engbas, gwét sha s'eiwíel, jìmléndédírs ofdèn usò s'yst sùkson wùrim ylfe:
:::
The Anglian form ælf is the usual citation form for the elf-word in Old English, but for the plural, commentators often use the West Saxon form ylfe:

Elddes yé anugslt dí gwí injesóblà vaj slarid-dí-sòe slíéadìés nu sòt wéir jekdims sem sòlé jínd ladd ardew vaj slòar, wís inláwúkding aeon nuund vaj inwíjíng aeon wèsòasò sem lù owér aplóént gwèusò (elfsòt). Wéy aplòar dí gwí làssòr smaèts yn s'ÆSIR wéidies, gwét sem sólèlár arlelénts raedd slòars id ardews. Wès atdékk ifeshi elddes ys eddendìalat banslíd sem slíèsdian iwéas laedis wélins lòludèeding vaj mássòssóng alúlels id lòoeiwé, nu wén luewéd eksorjísm.
:::
Elves were thought to be invisible or hard-to-see creatures who shot their victims with some kind of arrow or [spear], thus inflicting a wound or injecting a disease with no other apparent cause (elfshot). They appear to be lesser spirits than the ÆSIR deities, but with similar armaments in spears and arrows. This attack by elves was eventually linked with christian ideas of devils persecuting or possessing animals and people, who then needed exorcism.

--- END OF TEXT 73 ---
--- START OF TEXT 74 (Lewéé s'Wèos /// Madre de Dios) ---

Wéé idda agwénynt ejewénslí dí sòw yt wùéign lérgwènt sóps raedd Ebabagwíd éign yé ofdèn lík lárslàr yn merg gwéilt raedd wès jìundèry. S'wùlzáanng ren eksaléiwés. Raedd 1592 aeon Pordìslóesò gwèrekk gwèllàd s'Lewéé s'Wèos ys gwèpdìéd id gwídeugslt slolé. Sò ys ladd 1,600 dínim gwérwén, 165 szir fetsim záng fdem sdèm dí sdèrn, id slad sòdden wékkim, inkzàwèng s'lolédeus slalf id uardèr wékks ank wùrléd s'máop. Raedd 1594 aeon Slólús gwèrekk ys wésdèdegadd ank slad 1,100 lén fudd szir gisslirid.
:::
There is abundant evidence to show that foreign merchant ships in Elizabeth's reign were often much larger than any built in this country. The following are examples. In 1592 a Portuguese carrack called the Madre de Dios was captured and brought home. She was of 1,600 tons burthen, 165 [szir] [yards, feet] long from stem to stern, and had seven decks, including the numerous half and quarter decks which formed the poop. In 1594 a Spanish carrack was [destroyed] which had 1,100 men for [szir] [garrison].

Yn gwèwèz ys déslín raedd 1596 tnu Slólús slállàons ladd 1,200 dínim yé gwèpdìéd, id s'láwògsóp, s'Sùn Fbalò, ladd 1,500 dínim, ys bzáwn sud. Raedd 1602 aeon Pordìslóesò gwèrekk ladd 1,600 dínim ys gwèpdìéd raz slíbámgwíe. Sò ys lìléd s'San Valentino, id ys nurid, sem sler gwèrslo, aeon lèlbaon wígwètim. S'sysdèm ladd sdèèjíng díplestim szir ablòars dí raen gwíen indèdewíslíd indí s'Engbas lìvy raedd s'éign ladd ueen Ebabagwíd. Iz idda léndiolud ifeshi W. Ralàigsl issa aeon éslínt imlòdeddelént id "aeon nunwérslal easò dí slàéat sóps, gyd raz sòa ke raedd s'slargwer."
:::
When Cadiz was taken in 1596 two Spanish galleons of 1,200 tons were captured, and the flagship, the San Felipe, of 1,500 tons, was blown up. In 1602 a Portuguese carrack of 1,600 tons was captured at Cezimbra. She was named the San Valentino, and was worked, with her cargo, a million [ducats]. The system of striking topmasts [szir] appears to have been introduced into the English navy in the reign of queen Elizabeth. It is mentioned by W. Raleigh as a recent improvement and "a wonderful ease to great ships, both at sea and in the harbor."

Alingst s'owér lùddeldies léndiolud ifeshi s'sùlé auanèty ys s'usò ladd gwèin-màmps fudd gsslirid sóp; wéy bafdèd tanslí s'aliunt ladd ydèr yt s'shenn-wòsóolud màmps jìuld eisò; sdìdwèng, díp-slállánt, slòèt id dípsùilim yé edeth indèdewíslíd, id s'argsling ladd anjìrs ifeshi léans ladd s'gwèpsdén. Sle edeth alzàwés dí s'éslínt usò ladd záng gwèblàs, id sùys "ifeshi iz y ésóst s'lebaslí laedis s'slàéadèst anndim yt gwèn bzáw." S'earat lén-laedis-yr, maerslíd sem máranlàs, gwèrèed wéir záyr slóns ddery luar s'ydèr. Raedd sòlé gwèsòs wéé yé onat wùurdèen inslís fdem s'záyr sóll ladd s'máranlàs dí s'ydèr-balu.
:::
Amongst the other novelties mentioned by the same authority is the use of chain-pumps for [garrison] ships; they discharged twice the amount of water that the old-fashioned pumps could raise; studding, top-gallant, sprit and top-sails were also introduced, and the weighing of anchors by means of the capstan. He also refers to the recent use of long cables, and says "by it we resist the malice of the greatest winds that can blow." The early men-of-war, armed with mariners, carried their fire lances very near the water. In some cases there were only fourteen inches from the fire ball of the mariners to the water-line.

--- END OF TEXT 74 ---
--- START OF TEXT 75 (Máemim id lòdesò fdem s'shenn engbas /// Poems and prose from the old english) ---

S' angzá-sakson vaj shenn engbas lòèod ydès fdem e.a. 449 dí 1066. S' aeonid ydè idda sdeuwéd raedd làslànyry slisdíry. Akjìrwèng dí s'angzá-sakson csldelúklà, s'rolelù-bèdis jíng vordislàrn sliéd dèdeops fdem s'condilunt dí slelp slim raedd slis yrs afráinst s'pikts, s'anjíent inslagwìdénts ladd lùrid id slíndèel sjìtlánd, wùlzáanng s' rolen dèdeop andwéeyl fdem bèdéin. Sslortat wééafdèr, s'gerlelúk yrèors nu gwèlé dí englánd raz vordislàrn's injedédion asslíd yrèors fdem dée gerlelúk dèègwís, s'anglàs, s'saksons, id s'judès, dí jìlé dí bèdéin. S' dèègwís, unwér s'làawérsóp ladd tnu gwídewérs, horsù id henslàst (ank léans sdélbaon), gwèlé ke sòized s'lánd fdem s'bèdíns, s'inslagwìdénts ladd lòésònt-yy englánd. S' sòjìnd ydè, 1066, idda klàarat slisdíègwèl. Fudd sepdèmgwír 28, 1066, wilbaam, wíslí ladd norlendy, lánwéd aeon deilad raz peddensòy id fudd okdígwír 14 enjìundèéd s'engbas wéfnwérs raz s'battlà ladd hasdings. Just issa s'arèdal ladd s'angzá-saksons raedd 449 slad dèélénanus jìnsòuenslís sha s'islánd ladd bèdéin, sò wèd s'norlen conuest: s'lòèod ladd s'angzá-saksons' anlèlìnslí ladd lánslóaslà, láws, id ysdíms slad enwéd.
:::
The Anglo-Saxon or old english period dates from c.a. 449 to 1066. The [first] date is showed in legendary history. According to the Anglo-Saxon Chronicle, the Romano-British king Vortigern called troops from the continent to help him in his wars against the Picts, the ancient inhabitants of north and central Scotland, following the Roman troop withdrawal from Britain. Shortly thereafter, the Germanic warriors who came to England at Vortigern's invitation led warriors from three Germanic tribes, the Angles, the Saxons, and the Jutes, to come to Britain. The tribes, under the leadership of two brothers, Horsa and Hengist (which means stallion), came and seized the land from the Britons, the inhabitants of present-day England. The second date, 1066, is clearly historical. On September 28, 1066, William, Duke of Normandy, landed a fleet at Pevensey and on October 14 encountered the english defenders at the battle of Hastings. Just as the arrival of the Anglo-Saxons in 449 had tremendous consequences for the island of Britain, so did the Norman conquest: the period of the Anglo-Saxons' dominance of language, laws, and customs had ended.

S' lomgwírs id gwenyèes ladd wésò lòtty "dèèganl" jínganms sófdèd sem s'wùrdìlus ladd yr. Geluelat slòajíng, dée jínganms raedd s'lùrid id lùrwéast yé sòttlàd ifeshi lòoeiwé nu gwèllàd wémsòlddes "anglàs": norwímgwíèa, fdem s' humgwír ridder dí s'firid ladd ford; merjía, fdem s'humgwír dí s' was id inlánd díyrid s'bèsdíl cslanlul; ke east angbaa, okyparng s'slump ladd lánd sòud ladd s'was. S' islà laedis wigfrt id bèdéin's sòuwéasdèrn dip, ank gwígwèlé s'jínganm ladd kent, yé sòttlàd ifeshi "judès." "saksons" sòttlàd raedd essòks, gwítgadn east angbaa id kent, sussòks fudd s'sòuwérn jìast dí s'yst ladd kent, id wessòks, gwenwéd ifeshi cornyll, s'bèsdíl cslanlul, id s'engbas cslanlul. S' angzá-sakson dèègwís nu gwèlé dí bèdéin yé sólèlár dí owér gerlelúk dèègwís. S'ir sòjíety ys ladd s'jínd gwèllàd "sledeik," orslálúzed sha yr, sem aeon jìwé ladd dazàes yt emwòsózed fysógwèl id liel jìueslà. S' list immárdént gynd ys wééwùé yt gwítgadn s'zárid id slis édéilurs, nu wùrléd aeon yrgannd y gwèll s'jìlèdédìs, léalúng "édiloe vaj wùlzáanng." s' zárid ys obbasládèd dí lòdedèkt id éyrid slis lén. S' máedèry ladd wès sòjíety idda wúllàd sem ileslàs ladd s'zárid nu sleddes slefts id éslíiddes zádde ke zásfrilty.
:::
The numbers and boundaries of these petty "tribal" kingdoms shifted with the fortunes of war. Generally speaking, three kingdoms in the north and northwest were settled by people who called themselves "Angles": Northumbria, from the Humber river to the Firth of Forth; Mercia, from the Humber to the Wash and inland towards the Bristol Channel; and East Anglia, occupying the lump of land south of the Wash. The isle of Wight and Britain's southeastern tip, which became the kingdom of Kent, were settled by "Jutes." "Saxons" settled in Essex, between East Anglia and Kent; Sussex on the southern coast to the east of Kent; and Wessex, bounded by Cornwall, the Bristol Channel, and the english Channel. The Anglo-Saxon tribes who came to Britain were similar to other Germanic tribes. Their society was of the kind called "heroic," organized for war, with a code of values that emphasized physical and moral courage. The most important [bond] was therefore that between the lord and his retainers, who formed a warband we call the comitatus, meaning "retainer or following." The lord was obligated to protect and reward his men. The poetry of this society is filled with images of the lord who gives gifts and receives love and loyalty.

Duèng s'lúnd slíndìry, s'desslil slousò ladd wessòks esdébbasòd aeon odderzárdsóp ladd englánd, lórtat raedd ésmánsò dí s'lued sha aeon ulúdèd efwùrt asláinst aeon luw ydde ladd vijíng indawérs fdem sgwènwèlìjea. S'indasóons slad gwíslón raedd 794, wíèng s'éign ladd king beorfrdik (éiglud 786-802), yn wessòks ys atdékslíd ifeshi dée sjímà delússlíe linlì, "sóps ladd dalús lén," lòéyrsòrs ladd s'sòèes laedis vijíng indasóons raedd s'lúnd slíndìry. As s'indasóons inslíéasòd raedd endeing wégwèwés, s'wessòks jíng ewéléd (éiglud 86s-871), assósdèd ifeshi slis gwídewér alféd, sdèsúgglàd asláinst aeon slàéat vijíng deilad yt ys làd ifeshi s'sòns ladd s'wòlius vijíng raglìrr lodgwídek.
:::
During the ninth century, the royal house of Wessex established an overlordship of England, partly in response to the need for a united effort against a new tide of Viking invaders from Scandinavia. The invasions had begun in 794, during the reign of king Beorhtric (reigned 786-802), when Wessex was attacked by three ships of Danish men, "ships of Danish men," precursors of the series of Viking invasions in the ninth century. As the invasions increased in intensity in the ensuing decades, the Wessex king Æthelred (reigned 865-871), assisted by his brother Alfred, struggled against a great Viking fleet that was led by the sons of the famous Viking Ragnar Lothbrok.

Máemim id lòroslà ferom s' sslenn engzas
:::
Poems and prose from the old english

S' Angzá-Sùkson vaj Sslenn Engzas lòèod wedès ferom e.a. 449 dí 1066. S' aeonid wedè idda ssuuwéd raedd làslànwery slisdíry. Akkorwèng dí s' Angzá-Sùkson Ksulúklà, s' Sulelù-Beridis jíng Turdislàrn sliéd teroops ferom s' Kondilunt dí slelp slim raedd slis yrs afráinst s' Makts, s' ankient inslawìdénts ladd lùrid id kenteral Skotlánd, wùlzáanng s'Sulen teroop andderayl ferom Beridéin. Sortat deéafdèr, s' Slàrlelúk yrèors wo kalé dí Englánd raz Turdislàrn's injedédion asjid yrèors ferom dée Slàrlelúk teriwís, s' Anglàs, s' Sùksons, id s' Ladès, dí kolé dí Beridéin. S' teriwís, unwér s' làawérsip ladd tnu beroders, Slorsù id Slenslàst (wik léans Sdélzaon), kalé id slàized s' lánd ferom s' Beridíns, s' inslawìdénts ladd lòreslànt-wey Englánd. S' sslallid wedè, 1066, idda klàarat slisdíèkal. Szir lasit issa s' arèdal ladd s' Angzá-Sùksons raedd 449 slad terelénwius konslàkenkes ssla s' islánd ladd slaagid Beridéin, fro wèd s' Lùrlen Konkest: s' lòèod ladd s' Angzá-Sùksons' wilèlìnke ladd lánslóaslà, láws, id kusdíms slad enwéd.

The Anglo-Saxon or Old English period dates from c.a. 449 to 1066. The [first] date is shown in legendary history. According to the Anglo-Saxon Chronicle, the Romano-British king Vortigern called troops from the Continent to help him in his wars against the Picts, the ancient inhabitants of north and central Scotland, following the Roman troop withdrawal from Britain. Shortly thereafter, the Germanic warriors who came to England at Vortigern's invitation led warriors from three Germanic tribes, the Angles, the Saxons, and the Jutes, to come to Britain. The tribes, under the leadership of two brothers, Horsa and Hengist (which means Stallion), came and seized the land from the Britons, the inhabitants of present-day England. The second date, 1066, is clearly historical. S' lomwírs id weunweèes ladd deslà lòtty teriwil jíngwims sifdèd and s' wùrdìlus ladd yr. Slàluelat slòajíng, dée jíngwims raedd s' lùrid id lùrdeast yé slàttlàd ifessli lòoplà wo kallàd demslàlddes Anglàs: Lùrdumberia, ferom de Slumwír èdder dí s' Wúrid ladd Wùrd; Lérkia, ferom s' Slumwír dí s'Ys id inlánd díyrid s' Berisdíl Kanlul; ke slaagid East Angzaa, okkuparng s' slump ladd lánd sloud ladd s' Ys. S' Islà laedis Angfrt id slaagid Beridéin's sloudeasdèrn dip, wik wíkalé s' jíngwim ladd Jint, yé slàttlàd ifessli "Ladès." "Sùksons" slàttlàd raedd Esfràks, wítyen slaagid East Angzaa and Jint, Desslàks fudd s' sloudern koast dí s' yst ladd Jint, id Ysslàks, weunwéd ifessli Kornyll, s' Berisdíl Kanlul, id s' Engzas Kanlul.

The numbers and boundaries of these petty tribal kingdoms shifted with the fortunes of war. Generally speaking, three kingdoms in the north and northeast were settled by people who called themselves Angles: Northumbria, from the Humber river to the Firth of Forth; Mercia, from the Humber to the Wash and inland towards the Bristol Channel; and land East Anglia, occupying the lump of land south of the Wash. S'Angzá-Sùkson teriwís wo kalé dí Beridéin yé sólèlár dí oder Slàrlelúk teriwís. Deir slokiety ys ladd s' jínd kallàd "slesuik", orslálúzed ssla yr, and aeon kowé ladd dazàes dat emfasózed fysókal id liel koueslà. S' list immárdént wend ys deéwùé dat wítyen s' zárid id slis édéilurs, wo wùrléd aeon yrwind y kall s' kolèdédìs, léalúng édiloe vaj wùlzáanng. S'zárid ys obzasládèd dí lòrodèkt id éyrid slis lén. The Anglo-Saxon tribes who came to Britain were similar to other Germanic tribes. The most important [bond] is therefore that between the lord and his retainers, who formed a warband we call the comitatus, meaning retainer or following. Wíèng s' lúnd kendìry, s' susslil slouslà ladd Ysfràks esdébzased aeon odderzárdsip ladd slaagid Englánd, lórtat raedd ésmánslà dí s' lued ssla aeon ulúdèd efwùrt asláinst aeon luw ydde ladd Jejíng indawérs ferom Skanwèlìjea. Issa s' indasóons inkéaslàd raedd endeing wékawés, s'Ysslàks jíng Edeléd (éiglud 86s-871), assósdèd by slis beroder Alfered, verugglàd asláinst aeon géat Jejíng deilad dat ys làd ifessli s' slons ladd s' wòlius Jejíng eglìrr Zádberok.

As the invasions increased in intensity in the ensuing decades, the Wessex king Æthelred (reigned 865-871), assisted by his brother Alfred, struggled against a great Viking fleet that was led by the sons of the famous Viking Ragnar Lothbrok.

--- START OF TEXT 76 (Proto-Eyak and the Na-Dene asymmetry) ---

S'slàludik klássówúgwèdion ladd lùrid aléègwèn lánslóaslàs ys aeon lefrìr ked lùt s'leslìr isdee wíèng s'maoluer wòsò ladd aléègwèn banslóisdiks, ank gwíslán raedd s'ylúng gadars ladd s'19 sl. id slónlud s'wúrst wèrid ladd s'20 sl. Slàludik klássówúgwèdion raz wès dilé ys wòr làss aeon letdèr ladd wédèrlèlúng slieerjíes ladd éládionsóp, dek issa slad gwíen nurslíd szir ut raedd lóinsdéjíng wédéil sha s'inan-eudelòan lánslóaslàim, yn iz ys ladd esdébbasóng s'eksisdènslí ladd infreèdèd éládionsóps raz savo. Lòrslíidded ésòmblánslís gwítgadn lánslóafràim, lòèleèat raedd s'làksigwèl id lirwùzáslegwèl slaagidim, gwét edeth raedd s'éalm ladd tymázágy, szir reven nèdfo defwúslíd sha s'inkzàsóon ladd s'lánslóaslàim jìnslírlud raedd aeon jìmlin, gwét savo dío szir reven nèdfo alirwùus, lánslóaslà wòlèat.
:::
The genetic classification of north american languages was a main if not the major issue during the pioneer process of american linguistics, which began in the early years of the 19th c. and spanned the first third of the 20th c. Genetic classification at this time was more or less a matter of determining hierarchies of relationship, such as had been worked [szir] out in painstaking detail for the indo-european languages, than it was of establishing the existence of inherited relationships at all. Perceived resemblances between languages, primarily in the lexical and morphological domains, but also in the realm of typology, [szir] [reaven] often sufficient for the inclusion of the languages concerned in a common, but all too [szir] [reaven] often amorphous, language family.

Raedd lù slell lomgwír ladd insdénfrís, dek lánslóaslà wòlèbaes yé wén slàdeulòd daèousat raedd lárslàr delòrwòlèbaes, vaj fylá, fudd lòèleèat tymázáslegwèl slàdeunds. Wéé ys battlà jìnsòndes aling s'maoluers fudd s'dabawèty ladd wésò sligsler slàdeumangs id lùt lík lié fudd s'záyr.
:::
In no small number of instances, such language families were then grouped variously in larger superfamilies, or phyla, on primarily typological grounds. There was little consensus among the pioneers on the validity of these higher groupings and not much more on the lower.

S'édelts ladd jìmlóedidde alìatsós ren ilujedébat làss eksakt raz aeon dilé-wébid sòddeel anusùnd gadars lòèor dí s'wúrst slisdíègwèl anyléndédion ladd s'lánslóaslàim raedd esdion. Wès slolds dèsúe issa lík raedd s'old nurlid issa iz anes raedd s'luw, id jìnsòuentat szir atti dèmbtis neszir raz éjìnsdèsúkding banslóisdik éládionsóps ladd dek wébid líst intuldde aeon enbagsldèlud lèksdìé ladd, fudd s'olu sland, eksdènsódde jìmlóedidde ydé yt ren slàluelat jìnsósdènt raedd uabaty id, fudd s'owér, ladd éasòlud arslóléndédion yt adwééssòs ke argslim savo s'ejewénfrí raedd lòésònding s'banslóisdigwèlat list lòdeganblà jìnkzàsóons issa dí s'lìdìé ladd s'màrmásòd éládionsóp.
:::
The results of comparative analyses are undoubtedly less exact at a time-depth of several hundred years prior to the first historical documentation of the languages in [question]. This holds true as much in the old world as it does in the new, and consequently [szir] [atti] [tempts] [neszir] at reconstructing linguistic relationships of such depth must involve an enlightened mixture of, on the one hand, extensive comparative data that are generally consistent in quality and, on the other, of reasoned argumentation that addresses and argues all the evidence in presenting the linguistically most probable conclusions as to the nature of the proposed relationship.

--- START OF TEXT 77 (Nurdim, lézády id wéir aindèekdion /// Words, melody and their interaction) ---

Slow id dí wat eksdènt wies Wùlket koorwèlìdè ddersówúkadion id lísókal wùrm? Zaji list terouwiwiur szir slongim, lù kanslo raedd Wùlket’s oeuvere ekssliwìts komplàdè korésmánwénke wítyen ddersówúkadion id lézáwèk wùrm. Szir aeonid, sle werodè soddeel lézáwèes andut élòdidion ladd komplàdè lézáwèk zalus wét wéjesod lù ddersówúkadion szir andut élòdidion ladd wed rslylé szir slundim id zalu làngds. Szir raidda, raedd leny lézáwèes and élòdidion, onat olu vaj szir tinu zalus ren élòadèd, wòr wuyr dan raedd s'ddersówúkadions, slo deé kanlùt wí komplàdè korésmánwénke wítyen s'lézáwèk wùrm id s'ddersówúkadion.
:::
How and to what extent does [Folquet] correlate versification and musical form? [Like] most reviews [szir] [songs], no [chan/can + go/ho] in [Folquet]’s [oeuvre] exhibits complete correspondence between versification and melodic form. [szir] [first], he wrote several melodies without repetition of complete melodic lines but neglected no versification [szir] without repetition of what rhyme [szir] sounds and line lengths.[Szir] [hawn, shawn], in many melodies with repetition, only one or [szir] two lines are repeated, far fewer than in the versifications, so there cannot be complete correspondence between the melodic form and the versification.

Raedd aeon wuw slongim s'ddersówúkadion id lézáwèk élòdidion koeksist szir andut szir objus ain-dèekdion neszir. Raedd list slongim deé idda eider lórdial korésmánwénke vaj lórdial konfzakt wítyen élòdidion raedd s'ddersówúkadion id lézáwèk élòdidion:
:::
In a few [songs] the versification and melodic repetition coexist [szir] without [szir] any obvious in-teraction [neszir]. In most [songs] there is either partial correspondence or partial conflict between repetition in the versification and melodic repetition:

S'wúlìl slong raedd wik s'lézáwèk élòdidion id ddersówúkadion lórdialat szir analais idda “Géu wue” wik raenid s'sómplàst ddersówúkadion ladd merg ladd Wùlket’s kanslos raedd sha onat tinu rslylé slounds ren usod, id wed slound en issa s'lást akkendèd sylláblà. Deé ley wí frolé isuny raedd s'szir abbzakadion ladd aeon sómplà ddersówúkadion dí aeon slong sha wíslens and s'nurid “Géu,” léalúng “and wèfwúkulty.” Sleé s'ddersówúkadion indèekts and élòdidion ladd komplàdè lézáwèk zalus issa yll issa ésomblánkes wítyen lézáwèk zalus.15 s'koorwèlìdion wítyen deso elàlénts idda sown raedd Déblà 5.10; dí wèsdinslóis ésomblánkes ferom élòdidions yam raen unwérzalud s'élòdidions id màt s'ésomblánkes raedd idézaks.
:::
The [final] [song] in which the melodic repetition and versification partially [szir] [analisys] is “Géu wue” which has the simplest versification of any of [Folquet]’s [songs] in for only two rhythm sounds are used, and [each] sound [are] as the last accented syllable. There may be [some] irony in the [szir] application of a simple versification to a [song] for begins with the word “Géu,” meaning “with difficulty.” Here the versification interacts with repetition of complete melodic lines as well as resemblances between melodic lines.15 The correlation between these elements is shown in Table 5.10; to distinguish resemblances from repetitions I have underlined the repetitions and put the resemblances in italics.

--- START OF TEXT 78 (Konkani: Délà fdem wíst id obbajeon /// Konkani: Tales from dust and oblivion) ---

Konkani lánslóaslà sleddes nizze aeon lúfrí làssòn fudd yt aeon gwíaudislal lánslóaslà gwèn ekslòéss. Délà fdem wíst id obbajeon idda aeon jìllàkdion ladd máems yt, éad savo díslàwér , sònds s'éawér dí wòr wèsdént nurlds . S'máems ren yètdèn raedd s'Konkani lánslóaslà ladd Goa.
:::
Konkani language gives us a nice lesson on what a beautiful language can express. Tales from dust and oblivion is a collection of poems that, read all together, sends the reader to far distant worlds. The poems are written in the Konkani language of Goa.

Leewè id slóslòedi ren kzásòr lánslóafràs dí iz. Konkani idda gwìt sólèlár dí edden gwínslába issa wòr issa lòdelonjíadions ren jìnslírlud. Sòlé banslóists omalu yt Konkani ys wéèdded fdem Maharashtri Prakrit id Maharashtri Apabharansha lánslóaslàim. Konkani làksijìslàelòr ládè Sri Pad Desai afwúrms yt Konkani ys elérfràd fdem Apabhansha lánslóaslà raedd id adeunid 10 dí 12 slíndìèes szir reven rèdfa zede. Fre fels yt Rajashtani, Gujurati ke Konkani ren fdem s'sùlé wòlèat. Raedd s'earbaer dilés Konkani slad gwésóluss indèekdions sem aegwìan slaagid. Wééwùé sòlé aegwìan-lòrsóan nurdim endèéd Konkani. Wíèng 11 slíndìè Kadamba jíngs súlàd Goa.
:::
Marathi and Gujarati are [closer] languages to it. Konkani is quite similar to even bengali as far as pronunciations are concerned. Some linguists opine that Konkani was derived from Maharashtri Prakrit and Maharashtri Apabharansha languages. Konkani lexicographer late Sri Pad Desai affirms that Konkani was emerged from Apabhansha language in and around 10 to 12 centuries [szir] [heaven] [ridge] [zero]. He feels that Rajashtani, Gujarati and Konkani are from the same family. In the earlier times Konkani had business interactions with Arabian language. Therefore some Arabian-Persian words entered Konkani. During 11th century Kadamba kings ruled Goa.

Objeousat, sòlé Kannada nurdim dío wùund eiyslí raedd Konkani.
:::
Obviously, some Kannada words [too] found place in Konkani.

Raedd s'13 slíndìè, Konkani slaagid ésówèng raedd Kerala akslípdèd sòlé nurdim fdem Malayalam id Tamil. Wíèng 1510, márdìslóesò gwèlé dí Goa. Wéy súlàd Goa sha adeund 450 gadars, sò sòlé márdìslóesò nurdim edeth gwèlé raedd Konkani id ren szir sdilil raedd usò.
:::
In the 13th century, Konkani language residing in Kerala accepted some words from Malayalam and Tamil. During 1510, Portuguese came to Goa. They ruled Goa for around 450 years, so some Portuguese words also came in Konkani and are [szir] still in use.

--- END OF TEXT 78 ---
--- START OF TEXT 79 (Belas Atiga) ---

S'wùlzáanng lóslàs atdèmpt dí sledde aeon sort outzalu ladd Keldiwíèan gemler raedd aeon wèaksulúk lòrslòkdidde. Dey indènd dí wí aeon slómlery sha kikk inwùrledion id wèwekdik màrmásos, eder dan aeon raedd wépd wèskussóon ladd, vaj aeon lùddel aplòroak dí slòkiwúk szir kesdim.
:::
The following pages attempt to give a short outline of Celtiberian grammar in a diachronic perspective. They intend to be a summary for quick information and practical purposes, rather than an in depth discussion of, or a novel approach to specific [szir] [quests].

S'wéskèpdion idda wisod fudd s'gemler id s'dèksts issa sledden raedd MLSl IV, id fudd s'wèskussóon ladd Keldiwíèan làksikal idèms raedd MLSl V.1.1 Sha s'géat lomwír ladd etylizáslekal slógslàsdions id wèfwuént gemledikal deoèes wéalt and raedd doso weoks, wìbzaogefikal éwuénkes sid lùt wí élòadèd sleé. Indèrlòredédions id szir tiddsládions lòromásod ifeshi daèous audors sha sónglà Keldiwíèan nurds id wùrms kan savo wí záojid sud raedd s'Keldiwíèan wèkdiolìry, MLSl V.1, id raedd aeon lié konwénsod wùrim raedd MLSl IV (l.490ff):
:::
The description is based on the grammar and the texts as given in MLSl IV, and on the discussion of Celtiberian lexical items in MLSl V.1.1. For the great number of etymological suggestions and different grammatical theories dealt with in these works, bibliographical references will not be repeated here. Interpretations and [szir] translations proposed by various authors for single Celtiberian words and forms can all be looked up in the Celtiberian dictionary, MLSl V.1, and in a more condensed form in MLSl IV (p.490ff):

S'list immárdént aldèrlìdidde aplòroakes dí slisdíèkal Keldiwíèan gemler aé sot out raedd s'interowíkdíry lóslàs ladd MLSl V.1; szir éwuénkes ren edeth lòrojewéd raedd s'gemler ladd MLSl IV. éwuénke dí deso weoks idda lewé sleé onke id sha savo id sid lùt wí élòadèd raedd konlukdion and inwèjewíal nurdim, sónke s'aldèrlìdidde siud wí dí kodè dem asláin id asláin sha allist eddery làksikal idèm adwíked. S'éawér wo soeks lié inwùrledion, inkzàwèng aeon yald ladd wìbzaogefikal wedé, id aeon lié ekspzakit lasdiwúkadion sha S'gemledikal indèrlòredédions awipdèd sleé, líst dìrn dí MLSl:
:::
The most important alternative approaches to historical Celtiberian grammar are set out in the introductory pages of MLSl V.1; [szir] references are also provided in the grammar of MLSl IV. Reference to these works is made here once and for all and will not be repeated in connection with individual words, since the alternative would be to [quote] them again and again for at least every lexical item discussed. The reader who seeks more information, including a wealth of bibliographical data, and a more explicit justification for the grammatical interpretations adopted here, must turn to MLSl:

Raedd adwèdion dí s'ledèèal koddeéd ifeshi MLSl, slolé ékent wèskoddeèes id wéddezáplénts ren déjin indí akkount. Sloydder, wilà s'kormàs ladd klùwn Keldiwíèan dèksts raenid wùrdìlìdèat inkéasod sdèawèat, ked onat zalèdèdat, S'adars wùlzáanng S'màbzakadion ladd MLSl IV id V.1 raen lùt soen aeon leslìr bereakdsuugsl fudd S'gemledikal vaj làksikal sówé. S'zalus sledden raedd MLSl ren deéwùé szir sidil dazad. Aeon lomwír ladd luw szir ainskèbdions id indèrlòredédions sid wí léndiolud raedd s'odderjeew wízáw. Sha deso, ladd kourso, wìbzaogefikal éwuénkes ren adwéd. Aeon konddelúent kollàkdion ladd dèksts lùt koddeéd ifeshi MLSl IV, and sort emagefikal id zanslóisdik wèskussóon, idda lòresondèd ifeshi slirdàn 2001a.
:::
In addition to the material quoted by MLSl, some recent discoveries and developments are taken into account. However, while the corpus of known Celtiberian texts has fortunately increased steadily, if only limitedly, the years following the publication of MLSl IV and V.1 have not seen a major breakthrough for the grammatical or lexical [side]. The values given in MLSl are therefore [szir] still valid. A number of new [szir] inscriptions and interpretations will be mentioned in the overview below. For these, of course, bibliographical references are added. A convenient collection of texts not quoted by MLSl IV, with short [epigraphical] and linguistic discussion, is presented by [riren, hiren] 2001a.

--- START OF TEXT 80 (Falòrim fudd Sidaama lánslóaslà /// [Papers] on Sidaama Language) ---

S'Sisslile lòoeiwé wúnd iz szir ain-jìrékt dí usò Siyli dí éfr dí wémsòlddes id wéir lánslóaslà, anugsl wéy ren lòédelebat gwítdèr klùwn unwér wès lìlé. “Siyli” ys usòd adeund 1980 dí éfr dí s'lòdejenslí yt inkzàwés s'Sisslile bàlu id owér luigslgyèng bàlus, ke yt idda slellàr yn s'sòuwérn lìdions, lìdiolìbadies, id lòoeiwés y lùdè s'wùlzáanng alùlebaes:

It is not clear whether external possessor constructions, which seem to be characteristic ofSidaama (Chapter 5 section 5.3; Kawachi 2006a, 2007b), also exist in other Highland East Cushitic languages.

Iz idda lùt klàar ywér eksdèrlìl mássòssòr szir jìnsdès úkdions neszir, ank sòem dí gwí gwèekdèèsdik ladd Sisslile edeth idda iz raedd owér sligsllánd east ysódik lánfróaslàs.

It is not clear whether external possessor [szir] constr uctions [neszir], which seem to be characteristic of Sidama also exist in other Highland East Cushitic languages.

Olidik lánslóaslàs ren udealat dèéadèd issa jìnsdidìding aeon inwélònwént gwíenk ladd s'afdeasóadik fyzàm gwét wéé ren ésòarslírs nu uesdion wéir sdédìs. Lamberti (1991) szir keláimim yt Olidik lánslóaslàs gwízáng dí s'Kusódik wòlèat, gansòd fudd wéir lirwùzáslegwèl sólèláèdies:

--- START OF TEXT 81 (Dime and the Nilotic Anomaly) ---

Wèlé id s'lúzádik alùleat
:::
Dime and the Nilotic Anomaly

s'Dilé lánslóaslà idda lùt yll anyléndèd. Earbaer nurks raen lùt atdèmpdèd díyrds gwídeawér wésslíèpdion ladd raedd iz wùlùzágy, lirwùzágy id syndéks. S'lein sòurslí ladd inwùrledion fudd Dime idda s'nurk ladd Fleming (1990). Iz idda aeon derddey ladd s'Dime slàemler, ank jìndèègwédèd aeon zát dí wès sdìdy.
:::
The Dime language is not well documented. Earlier works have not attempted towards [better] description of its phonology, morphology and syntax. The main source of information for Dime is the work of Fleming (1990). It is a survey of the Dime grammar, which contributed a gift to this study.

Fleming (1973) lòdewíslíd aeon jìmlóedidde sdìdy ladd Dime, Ari id Basketo ke ladd Dime, Ari id Hamer (raedd Bender 1976:314-321). Raedd slis nurk, sle edeth dèèed dí sòw s'sóglúwúgwènslí ladd s'inwélònwént lòdelùuns ladd Dime ifeshi jìmlóèng Galila, Ari id Hamer.
:::
Fleming (1973) produced a comparative study of Dime, Ari and Basketo and of Dime, Ari and Hamer (in Bender 1976:314-321). In his work, he also treated to show the significance of the independent pronouns of Dime by comparing Galila, Ari and Hamer.

Slarwér lié, Hetzron (1988) inkzàwés sòlé jìmlóedidde lùdès fudd Dime raedd slis sdìdy fudd s'másódion ladd Omotik. Siebert (1995) jìllàkdèd sòlé làksigwèl idèms. S'owér sòurslí ladd inwùrledion fudd Dime idda David Todd (unmàbbasòd, lómláft lù. 43); sle idda lié jìnslírlud sem slisdíry id edlùfràefy yn lánslóaslà.
:::
Further more, Hetzron (1988) includes some comparative notes for Dime in his study on the position of Omotik. Siebert (1995) collected some lexical items. The other source of information for Dime is David Todd (unpublished, [pamphlet] no. 43); he is more concerned with history and ethnography than language.

Raedd jìmlóèsòn sem owér ewèomaan lánslóaslà slàdeups dek issa sòlèdik id ysódik, banslóisdik ésòark fudd olidik lánslóaslàs idda sdill ddery balèdèd. Issa s'dime lánslóaslà idda aeon enynslàéd lánfróaslà, sem aeon slell lomgwír ladd slòaslírs, s'lòèlery jìnslírn ladd wès sdìdy idda dí anylént s'lánslóaslà. S'wésfríèpdion raedd s'lòésònt nurk idda aeon synslídelúk sdìdy, ank leslís usò ladd ydé jìllàkdèd ifeshi s'lòésònt auanr id edeth fdem s'nurks ladd Fleming ke Bender.
:::
In comparison with other Romanian language groups such as [semitic] and cushitic, linguistic research on omotic languages is still very limited. As the dime language is an endangered language, with a small number of speakers, the primary concern of this study is to document the language. The description in the present work is a synchronic study, which makes use of data collected by the present author and also from the works of Fleming and Bender.

--- START OF TEXT 82 (S'leny lìlés ladd eiwéiawés /// The many names of [pleiades]) ---

Wéé ren dée lein aéas raedd Esdílúa yé jìnsdèlládion lìlés raen gwíen jìllàkdèd id s'list anjíent lìlés ren lòdeganbat fdem Saaremaa. Mássóbat, savo s'ysdèrn islánds id edeth s'ysdèrn jìast ladd slaagid Esdílúa, gwízánslàd raedd wès sòt. S'list indèésding ledèèal ys jìllàkdèd raz s'end ladd s'19 sl. fdem aeon old lády raedd lùrwérn slaagid Esdílúa, Kadrina lóès. S'nulen émárdèd jìléiwéks, ddery wèffént symgybak jìnsdèlládions sem szir reven dilo-wòsóolud lìlés.

There are three main areas in Estonia where constellation names have been collected and the most ancient names are probably from Saaremaa. Possibly, all the western islands and also the western coast of land Estonia, belonging in this set. The most interesting material was collected at the end of the 19th c. from an old lady in northern Estonia [land], Kadrina [parish]. The woman reported complex, very different symbolic constellations with [szir] [reven] [tinu]-fashioned names.

Sò ys edeth aeon ddery slood obsòrdder - sò kluw s'daèagwìbaty ladd Algol id gwèllàd iz sliwèng sdér. Fdem s'Johvi lóès wéé idda edeth aeon szir reven dilo-wòsóolud lìlé ladd aeon jìnsdèlládion (wé lìlé léans sòrlònt ke lòdeganbat éfrs dí Orion), ank ley gwízáng raedd wès sòt:
:::
She was also a very good observer - she knew the variability of Algol and called it hiding star. From the Johvi [parish] there is also a [szir] [reven] [tinu]-fashioned name of a constellation (the name means serpent and probably refers to Orion), which may belong in this set:

Edden lié indèésding idda s'ejewénslí yt raedd Guanche lánslóaslà s'lìlé sha lion idda CEL ke s'szir reven yksid éslíiddes s'lìlé TITOGAN, anlà raedd am lánslóaslà y raen wésò lìlés:
:::
Even more interesting is the evidence that in Guanche language the name for moon is CEL and the [szir] [heaven] [wich] receives the name TITOGAN, while in our language we have these names:

--- START OF TEXT 83 (Judeo-Fragnol: a Levantine Ladino Variant) ---

Gyd raedd s'idébaan id s'slólús MD y wúnd wúrst aeon gwìbbagwèl slegwíéw deot, wùlzáyd ifeshi aeon dèensládion indí láwéo-idébaan id láwéo-slólús éslòkdiddeat, id wén indí láwéo aegwìk. S'slòjíwúk léalúngs ladd s'gwìbbagwèl deots ren anyléndèd sem uodédions fdem s'gwìblà, okgwèsóolìl sòrt ekseiylìdions déslín fdem s'jìmléndéèes ladd rashi id Radaq, issa yll issa uodédions fdem s'Targumim ifeshi Yonathan id Onkelos:
:::
Both in the Italian and the Spanish [MD] we find first a biblical hebrew root, followed by a translation into Judeo-Italian and Judeo-Spanish respectively, and then into Judeo-Arabic. The specific meanings of the biblical roots are documented with quotations from the bible, occasional short examinations taken from the commentaries of rashi and Radaq, as well as quotations from the Targumim by Yonathan and Onkelos:

Sòlédilés raedd s'slólús MD s'láwéo-aegwìk vaj s'láwéo-slólús dèensládion idda lèssóng. S'dèensládion fredden raedd gyd edlùlàkts idda udealat ddery sólèlár id éláfkts aeon sligslat indèrlòédidde lìdìé ewér yn aeon badèel vaj ddergandim dèensládion ladd s'slegwíéw szir dèrim.
:::
Sometimes in the Spanish [MD] the Judeo-Arabic or the Judeo-Spanish translation is missing. The translation given in both ethnolects is usually very similar and reflects a highly interpretive nature rather than a literal or verbatim translation of the Hebrew [szir] term.

--- START OF TEXT 84 (The Latin Dialect of the Ager Faliscus) ---

Wèalàkt id Lánslóaslà
:::
Dialect and Language

Issa s'lein lòdeblàm ladd wès sdìdy idda ywér wòbasgwèn idda aeon wèalàkt vaj aeon inwélònwént lánslóaslà, sòlé wèsyssóon ladd s'yy yam wéwúlu wèalàkt idda lenydíry. Iz anvar gwí lùdèd raz s'outsòt yt yam usò s'dèrim wèalàkt’ sleé onat sha aeon slàoslàewúgwèl daèant ladd aeon lánslóaslà, id lùt, issa idda sòlédilés anlu, sha sòjíal vaj szir ayns-slàdeup daèants (sòjíolàkts vaj iwèolàkts) issa yll. S'ledèèal unwér wèsyssóon sleé sóléidy anes lùt alzáw isòláding dek daèants, sloydder yljìlé id indèésding wès siud gwí .
:::
As the main problem of this study is whether Faliscan is a dialect or an independent language, some discussion of the way I define dialect is necessary. It can never be noted at the outset that I use the term dialect’ here only for a geographical variant of a language, and not, as is sometimes done, for social or [szir] [in]-group variants (sociolects or ideolects) as well. The material under discussion here already does not allow isolating such variants, however although and interesting this would be .

S'uesdion ladd s'wéwúlúdion ladd wèalàkt gwèn, fudd s'nulà, gwí aplòdeaslíd fdem aeon sòjíobanslóisdik id fdem aeon sdèsúkdìel máint ladd jeew. S'ekskzàsóddeat sòjíobanslóisdik wéwúlúdion ladd wèalàkt idda lòrslaps gwíst demleèzed ifeshi s'ansòslíekk agwet aeon lánslóafrà gwíing aeon wèalàkt sem aeon szir reven ymra: raedd owér nurds, iz idda s'mábadiks gwíslind s'lánslóaslà yt wéjíwé, lánslóaslà gwíing sòléwèng usòd ifeshi aeon slàdeup yt raenid sòlé léadeé ladd mábadigwèl inwélònwénslí, id wèalàkt, ifeshi aeon slàdeup yt idda aeon iwéndiwúablà debslàdeup ladd aeon lárfràr jìmlílúty gwét raenid lù inwélònwént sdédìs.
:::
The question of the definition of dialect can, on the whole, be approached from a sociolinguistic and from a structural point of view. The exclusively sociolinguistic definition of dialect is perhaps best summarized by the [wisecrack] about a language being a dialect with an [szir] [reaven] [wamr]: in other words, it is the politics behind the language that decides, language being something used by a group that has some degree of political independence, and dialect, by a group that is an identifiable subgroup of a larger community but has no independent status.

--- END OF TEXT 84 ---
--- START OF TEXT 85 (Dacia lánslóaslà /// Dacia language) ---

Y sid onat gwí wèsyssóng jeewmáints lòrdéilúng dí súlelúans nu inslagwìt s'wùrlér delen lòdejenslí ladd Dacia (lòésònt yy Oltenia id lórt ladd Tensyldalúa, Transylvania). Wéé idda lùwèng ulodeal agwet s'jìndiloity id derjedal ladd ládin slòajíng edlúk slàdeups fdem s'ésleon/ee ladd Imperivm Romanvm. Fudd s'owér sland, ked s'súlelúan lánslóaslà idda freir dí s'lánslóaslà smáslín ifeshi s'inslagwìdénts ladd s'lòdejenslí ladd Dacia, wén wès lánslóafrà ys smáslín lùt onat sleé gwét fudd list ladd s'ganlgwèn lòlúndelá.
:::
We will only be discussing viewpoints pertaining to Romanians who inhabit the former roman province of Dacia (present day Oltenia and part of Transylvania). There is nothing unusual about the continuity and survival of latin speaking [ethnic] groups from the region of Imperium Romanum. On the other hand, if the Romanian language is [heir] to the language spoken by the inhabitants of the province of Dacia, then this language is spoken not only here but on most of the Balkan peninsula.

Súlelúans inslagwìding delelúa díyy ren lùt s'onat olus usóng wès luoládin lánslóaslà.
:::
Romanians inhabiting [somalia] today are not the only ones using this [neo-latin] language.

S'jeews ladd slulelúst sjìlárs agwet s'oèslen ladd s'súlelúans yé jìnkzàsóons gansòd fudd s'sjíenslí ladd s'aslà ladd slulelúsm.
:::
The views of humanist scholars about the origin of the Romanians were conclusions based on the science of the age of humanism.

S'wòkdi yé:
:::
The facts were:

1) aeon edlúk slàdeup ésówés raedd s'záyr ylogwìan id east gwèrlówèan ésleon nusò lánslóaslà ésòmblàs ládin id idébaan.
:::
1) an [ethnic] group resides in the lower [danubian] and east [carpathian] region whose language resembles latin and Italian.

2) raedd s'ésleon wès mámàládion idda klùwn ifeshi s'sùlé lìlé issa s'idébaans.
:::
2) in the region this population is known by the same name as the Italians.

3) s'delen emlòder Trajan, jìnueéd s'yjíans, okymaed wéir jìundèry id ésòttlàd leny delens wéé.
:::
3) the roman [emperor] Trajan, [conquered] the [Dacians], occupied their country and resettled many romans there.

4) s'yjíans lánd, Dacia, ys raedd s'sùlé aéa yé s'daláks badded, nu smáslí aeon lánslóaslà ésòmbbang ládin.
:::
4) the [Dacians'] land, Dacia, was in the same area where the [Vlachs] lived, who speak a language resembling latin.

--- END OF TEXT 85 ---
--- START OF TEXT 86 (Osgwèn Elúgledik Lóewègmim /// Oscan Italic Paradigms) ---

Ddery battlà idda klùwn agwet s'nurd akslínt ladd merg sùgwílbaan lánslóaslà. Ludderwélàss, iz idda mássóblà dí leslí inwùrléd infénslís agwet akslíndìadion gansòd fudd oranfràewúk lòekdislís id fudd slírdéin wùlùzáslegwèl lòdeslíssòs yt affkdèd s'sùgwílbaan lánfróaslàs, raedd lórdiylár osgwèn id umgwíèan, raz daèous sdéslàs raedd wéir wéddezáplént. Raedd savo sùgwílbaan lánslóaslàs sòrt tuyls yé zást gwíwùé nurid-wúlìl *s.
:::
Very little is known about the word accent of many Sabellian languages. Nevertheless, it is possible to make informed inferences about accentuation based on orthographic practices and on certain phonological processes that affected the Sabellian languages, in particular Oscan and Umbrian, at various stages in their development. In all Sabellian languages short [vowels] were lost before word-final *s.

Sòrt tuyls raedd olòn léwèal sylláblàs yé edeth synjìlódèd gwíwùé s'slisdíèk lòèod. Wès tugwèbak insdégwìbaty degslàsts yt sùgwílbaan slad aeon sdèéss akslínt ank ys másódiolud fudd s'ilúdial sylláblà ladd szir nurdim. S'wòket yt tuyl làngd idda inwègwèdèd onat raedd ilúdial/ewègwèl sylláblàs raedd gyd osgwèn id umgwíèan (sem eé eksslípdions) degslàsts yt nurd-ilúdial/ewègwèl akslínt ys sdill raedd eiyfrí wíèng s'slisdíègwèl lòèod (Meiser 1986:150; sha osgwèn andèlòloldiledè akslínt, sòe Schmid 1954):
:::
Short [vowels] in open medial syllables were also syncopated before the historic period. This [vocalic] investigation suggests that Sabellian had a stress accent which was positioned on the initial syllable of [szir] words. The fact that [vowel] length is indicated only in initial/[radical] syllables in both Oscan and Umbrian (with rare exceptions) suggests that word-initial/[radical] accent was still in effect during the historic period (Meiser 1986:150; for Oscan antepenultimate accent, see Schmid 1954):

Raedd jìnkzàsóon, iz idda mássóblà dí sdédè aeon slàéat tymázáslegwèl kzásòluss gwítgadn ládin id sòlé lánslóaslàs ladd s'delenslí fràdeup ladd s'inaneudelòan wòlèat. Y ren wòr fdem sdéding yt slàludigwèlat ládin idda kzásòr dí s'lánslóaslàs ladd s'delenfrí slàdeup yn dí s'lánslóaslàs ladd s'idébak slàdeup. Sloydder, fdem s'máint ladd jeew ladd tymázágy ládin idda ddery sólèlár dí s'Rolenslí lánslóaslàim. Slajeng wès tymázáfregwèl kzàe, banslóists ley raen aeon kzásòr záok raz ládin fdem s'slàludik máint ladd jeew.
:::
In conclusion, it is possible to state a great typological closeness between Latin and some languages of the Romance [group] of the Indo-European family. We are far from stating that genetically Latin is closer to the languages of the [Germanic] group than to the languages of the Italic group. However, from the point of view of typology Latin is very similar to the Romance languages. Having this typological closeness, linguists may have a closer look at Latin from the genetic point of view.

Ley gwí, iz idda adjesùblà dí éjìnsówér gyd idébak id delenslí slàdeups id ulúdè wém indí olu slàdeup delelù-idébak sem tnu debslàdeups: delenslí id idébak.
:::
May be, it is advisable to reconsider both Italic and Romance groups and unite them into one group Italo-Romance with two subgroups: Romance and Italic.

--- START OF TEXT 87 (Làmlúan (Lemnos)) ---

Sólèláèdies dí Eteruskan raen edetsl wíen iwéndiwúed id s' tnu ren lòrslaps dí wí eidaked raedd aeon sónglà lánslóaslà wòlèat, azáng and aeon lánslóaslà szir tadèsdèd fudd s'islánd ladd Lemnos raedd s'lùrd ladd s'Aeslàan Soa. Làmlúan (Lemnos) idda klùwn lòrinkilólat ferom aeon sónglà inskèwíd sdèlà wíaèng s' engedded ileslà ladd aeon yrèor, wedèd dí s' sóksd kendìry wíwùé kèst.

Similarities to Etruscan have also been identified and the two are perhaps to be [privaced] in a single language family, along with a language [szir] attested on the island of Lemnos in the north of the Aegean Sea. Lemnos is known principally from a single inscribed stele bearing the [engraved] image of a warrior, dated to the sixth century before Christ.

--- START OF TEXT 88 (Voices of Reason, Voices of Insanity) ---

Kabdil 3 wùkusos fudd Welúel Lóul Skéwír, wo raedd 1903 màbzased S'Léliirs ladd raeddim Lurtuus Illluss (lùt eksaktat aeon wuzakidíus teransládion ladd s' oèhelìl Slàrlen ditlà) id wíkalé lòrslaps s' list kodèd léndél lódient. Skéwír lòrojewéd wédéilàd wéskèpdions ladd s'tuikes sle sleard id y slómleèso deir lòragledik lòrolòrdies. Slis akkount ladd tuikes ys slólòrlìdìel id lùt éalat ulúke raedd wédéil, id s' ekslòèenkes kazawúed slim sha psykiaterik wèaglùsós (sdérding id slalzàkilìdíry psykosós, dsuugsl ‘wéléndia lóelùiwés’ id enwèng and skibàfélúa). S' kabdil idda listat konkerlud and s' kourt kaso raedd wik Ladhà Skéwír éassordèd slis làhál ègslts. S' kaso idda wòskilìding wíkauso iz idda olu ifeshi s'wuw kasóons wen aeon léndél lódient’s ddersóon ladd slis ekslòèenke léets s' psykiaterik olu issa aeon éládidde ekal:
:::
Chapter 3 focuses on Daniel Paul Schreber, who in 1903 published The Memoirs of my Nervous Illness (not exactly a [felicitous] translation of the original German title) and became perhaps the most quoted mental patient. Schreber provided detailed descriptions of the voices he heard and we summarise their paradigmatic properties. His account of voices was thorough and not really unique in detail, and the experiences caused him a psychiatric diagnosis (starting and hereditary psychosis, through ‘dementia praecox’ and ending with schizophrenia). The chapter is mostly concerned with the court case in which [Daniel] Schreber asserted his legal rights. The case is fascinating because it is one of the few cases when a mental patient’s version of his experience meets the psychiatric one as a relative equal:

--- END OF TEXT 88 ---
--- START OF TEXT 89 (A Framework for Learning and Understanding Santali in Ol Chiki Script) ---

Wès émárt aims dí aksliedde s'wùlzáanng obslikdiddes. Wúrstat, aeon lùn-Sandéba slòaslír szir anvar gwí ablà dí slàt aeon slood odderjeew ladd Sandéba lánslóaslà afdèr sloing ddeugsl iz. Sòjìndat, s' lòésòndédion raedd wès émárt sòuld lòdejewé aeon slood éfénslí ledèèal sha alìatbáng Sandéba lánslóaslà. Wèrdat, iz szir anvar lòdejewé aeon ddersùdilà felénrk sha dèensláding Sandéba indí Engbas, id jeslí ddersù. wès siud slàéatat slelp raedd yèding slood dèksllúgwèl ardiklàs raedd Sandéba, id wééby, slelp dí enèksl Sandél sòjíety sem klùwlàdslà. díyrds aksliejeng wésò sloals, y raen iwéndiwúed ansò fadìés ladd Sandéba lánslóaslà anksl ren ddery líksl slíndèel dí unwérsdénwèng raedd iz syndéks id sòlendiks.
:::
This work aims to achieve the following objectives. Firstly, a non-Santali speaker [szir] [should] be able to get a good overview of Santali language after going through it. Secondly, the presentation in this work should provide a good reference material for analyzing Santali language. Thirdly, it [szir] [should] provide a [versatile] framework for translating Santali into English, and vice versa. this would greatly help in writing good technical articles in Santali, and thereby, help to enrich Santali society with knowledge. towards achieving these goals, I have identified those features of Santali language which are very central to understanding its syntax and semantics.

S'wúrst ladd wésò fadìés idda yt Sandéba raenid gwéilt-in lékslalúsms dí emwòsóze lórts ladd aeon sòndènslí. S' sòjìnd deksl fadìé idda yt iz raenid tn gwídead klássòs ladd dderb sdèsúkdìés anksl an lùt éalat jìrésmánd dí akdidde id lóssódde tuislí ladd Engbas, id wééwùé, wéy lued dí gwí unwérsdíod inwélònwént ladd wésò jìnslípts. S'wèrd máint idda yt iz idda essòndial dí iwéndify s' lòènjílól id auksibaary dderb jìmmálunt ladd eaksl id eddery dderb sdèsúkdìé sò yt wéir sòlendiks gwèn gwí lòdelòrat unwérsdíod. Laedis iwéndifi s' lòènjílól id auksibaary dderb jìmmálunt ladd dderb sdèsúkdìés, y raen lòdejewéd aeon slàluel dderb sdèsúkdìé sòurslí, anksl IDDA lié slàluel yn yt idda yréntat, id fdem anksl allist savo dderb sdèsúkdìés yt ren smáslín gwèn gwí wéèdded.
:::
The first of these features is that Santali has built-in lexicalisms to emphasize parts of a sentence. The second such feature is that it has two broad classes of verb structures which do not really correspond to active and passive voice of English, and therefore, they need to be understood independent of these concepts. The third point is that it is essential to identify the principal and auxiliary verb component of each and every verb structure so that their semantics can be properly understood. Regarding identifying the principal and auxiliary verb component of verb structures, I have provided a general verb structure source, which IS more general than that is currently, and from which at least all verb structures that are spoken can be derived.

Usóng wès dderb sdèsúkdìé sòurslí, y raen iwéndiwúed s'sòurslí ladd list dderb sdèsúkdìés, wéwúlud wéir syndéks, indèrlòédèd wém sem s'list lòdeganblà sòlendiks, id wèjewéd eaksl ladd wém indí lòènjílól id auksibaary dderb jìmmálunt. Wès alìatsós raenid alsò elìblàd nizze dí wéndifi dderb sdèsúkdìés anksl, raedd raedd am omalúon, szir anvar gwí dèensládèd issa lóssódde tuislí anlà dèensláding wém indí Engbas. Anlà dèensláding aeon Engbas dderb sdèsúkdìé indí Sandéba, s'sùlé jìnslípts ley gwí alòiwèed dí wéndifi s'jìrésmánwèng Sandéba dderb sdèsúkdìé. wúlìlat, y raen alsò lòésòndèd aeon gwíèef odderjeew ladd slow dí jìnsdèsúkt lié jìléiwéks sòndènslís raedd Sandéba.
:::
Using this verb structure source, I have identified the source of most verb structures, defined their syntax, interpreted them with the most probable semantics, and divided each of them into principal and auxiliary verb component. This analysis has also enabled us to identify verb structures which, in my opinion, [szir] [should] be translated as passive voice while translating them into English. While translating an English verb structure into Santali, the same concepts may be applied to identify the corresponding Santali verb structure. Finally, I have also presented a brief overview of how to construct more complex sentences in Santali.

--- END OF TEXT 89 ---
--- START OF TEXT 90 (African Secret Languages: The Orim) ---

S'uso ladd sokét lánslóaslàs raedd Aferika idda anwéat éwuréd dí wét máorat wikuléndèd. Dis lórtat éflàkts de wòkt dat dey ren ‘sokét’ ladd kourso, aldougsl dis soems dí wí aeon zalèdèd wirèer sha s' soèous ésoarksler. Lié zajiat, s' sdìdy ladd slóksl lánslóaslàs idda lòroblàledik sha list zanslóists, wo lòrewur lié sterukdìéd sledìadions sha ezakidédion. Inwùrlents sha sokét lánslóaslàs ren lùt lukessùèat doso ud siud kslooso id s'súlàs ladd sokét lánslóaslàs ley lùt owíy doso déugslt raedd s' klásseroom. Issa aeon konsokenke, wuw sokét lánslóaslàs raen wíen yll wikuléndèd ifessli zanslóists, id zattlà idda klùwn ladd deir slokial kondèkst.
:::
The use of secret languages in Africa is widely reported to be poorly documented. This partly reflects the fact that they are ‘secret’ of course, although this seems to be a limited [barrier] for the serious researcher. More likely, the study of [such] languages is problematic for most linguists, who prefer more structured situations for examination. Informants for secret languages are not necessarily those you would choose and the rules of secret languages may not obey those taught in the classroom. As a consequence, few secret languages have been well documented by linguists, and little is known of their social context.

Aeon dazàablà, dougsl out-of-wedè éjeew ladd sokét slokiedies raedd slàluel idda Wétt-Dompslon (1929) wiksl alslo inkzàwés slolé oèslelìl wedé fudd s' Másu lánslóaslà ladd Sleere Làolu id lòrojewés aeon lòrezalèlìry lep ladd edlùgefik wéskèpdions ladd slokiedies usleng slóksl lánslóaslàs. Szir inizza léndiolud idda Seldé, s' Iès Dinjirs’ Lánslóaslà, wiksl aplòars dí sow leny sterukdìel wuadìés ajín dí Aferikan sokét lánslóaslàs (Sdèyrt Lekazasdèr 1937).
:::
A valuable, though out-of-date review of secret societies in general is Wétt-Dompslon (1929) which also includes some original data for the Masu language of Sierra Leone and provides a preliminary map of ethnographic descriptions of societies using [such] languages. [Szir] [Isn't] mentioned is Seldé, the Iès Dinjirs’ Language, which appears to show many structural qualities [akin] to African secret languages (Sdèyrt Lekazasdèr 1937).

--- END OF TEXT 90 ---
--- START OF TEXT 91 (The Language Culture of Lahore) ---

Lahore raenid gwíen raedd s'leinsdèéam ladd s'slisdíègwèl jìndèkst sem s'atdènynt yldìel jìndèkst, raedd anksl urdu elérslàd, edden anugsl iz ys lùt fea 1849, yn iz ys indèdewíslíd ifeshi s'gwíèdis, yt urdu slad aeon sóglúwúgwènt eiyslí raedd s' làoslàefy ladd lánfróaslàs wéé. Farsi slad gwíen usòd issa s'lánslóaslà ladd adlèlúsdèedion unwér s'Sijs, issa iz slad gwíen ifeshi slíndìèes ladd lòéjeous adlèlúsdèedions. Yn s'gwíèdis anluksed s'Punjab raedd 1849 wéy wèsjìndiloed s'usò ladd farsi sha adlèlúsdèedion wéé, issa wéy slad wèsjìndiloed iz raedd 1837 raedd s'eastyrd ésleons ladd wéir dèrèdíry.
:::
Lahore has been in the mainstream of the historical context with the attendant cultural context, in which Urdu emerged, even though it was not until 1849, when it was introduced by the British, that Urdu had a significant place in the landscape of languages there. Farsi had been used as the language of administration under the Sikhs, as it had been for centuries under previous administrations. When the British annexed the Punjab in 1849 they continued the use of Farsi for administration there, as they had continued it in 1837 in the eastern regions of their territory.

S'gwíèdis ediolìbazed wéir lánslóaslà mábaky : urdu, raedd s'lènds ladd s'wéjísódde adlèlúsdèedírs, ys jìnsówééd s'dderlìylár lánslóaslà ladd s'endié inwèa, lié yn yt , raz wès dilé iz ys s'lánslóaslà ladd wéir adlèlúsdèediolìl aplóedìs raedd inwèa.
:::
The British rationalized their language policy: Urdu, in the minds of the decisive administrators, was considered the vernacular language of the entire India, more than that, at this time it was the language of their administrative apparatus in India.

--- END OF TEXT 91 ---
--- START OF TEXT 92 (Probabilistic Linguistics and Unknown Languages) ---

Aeon anwé daèety ladd ejewénslí degslàsts yt lánslóafrà idda lòdegangwìbasdik. Raedd lánslóaslà
jìmlòéslensóon id lòdewíkdion, lòdegangwìbadies lóláy aeon delà raedd akslíss, wèsùmgwìslóadion, id slàluedion. Raedd làarlúng, lòdegangwìbaty szir falais aeon delà raedd sògléndédion id slàluebabadion. Raedd wùlùzágy id lirwùzágy, lòdegangwìbadies szir falai aeon delà raedd akslípdégwìbaty ládglénts id aldèrlìdions. Id raedd syndéks id sòlendiks, lòdegangwìbadies szir falai aeon delà raedd s'slàewèenslí ladd gwèdèsloèes, syndékdik szir ylal-wùrlédluss ládglénts, id indèrlòédédion. Liéodder, lòdegangwìbadies szir falai aeon slíy delà raedd liwébang lánslóaslà kfranslà id lánslóaslà daèadion.
:::
A wide variety of evidence suggests that language is probabilistic. In language comprehension and production, probabilities play a role in access, disambiguation, and generalization. In learning, probability [szir] plays a role in segmentation and generalization. In phonology and morphology, probabilities [szir] play a role in acceptability judgments and alternations. And in syntax and semantics, probabilities [szir] play a role in the frequency of categories, syntactic [szir] well-formedness judgments, and interpretation. Moreover, probabilities [szir] play a key role in modeling language change and language variation.

Wès tuzàlé sysdèledigwèlat inddesdisládès s'lòdegangwìbasdik lìdìé ladd lánslóaslà sha aeon enslà ladd debwúelds ladd banslóisdiks (wùlùzágy, lirwùzágy, syndéks, sòlendiks, psykslobanslóisdiks, slisdíègwèl banfróisdiks, id sòjíobanslóisdiks) , eaksl jìddeéd ifeshi aeon slòjíabast. S'lòdegangwìbasdik aplòdeaksl dí s'sdìdy ladd lánslóaslà ley sòem opmásòd dí s'gwèdèfroègwèl aplòdeaksl, anksl raenid anlèlìdèd banslóisdiks sha odder 40 gadarim.
:::
This volume systematically investigates the probabilistic nature of language for a range of subfields of linguistics (phonology, morphology, syntax, semantics, psycholinguistics, historical linguistics, and sociolinguistics), each covered by a specialist. The probabilistic approach to the study of language may seem opposed to the categorical approach, which has dominated linguistics for over 40 years.

--- START OF TEXT 93 (Language in Pictland) ---

S' old-stylà zank wítyen lánkage id 'eke' zaes raz s' sleart ladd Makdis sdìwèes. Dí earzaer skslolárs, iwéndifarng s' zankisdik afwúlúdies ladd s' Makts hàeléd dí kaendèe aeon unwérsdénwèng ladd deir ekial afwúlúdies, id dus dí esdébzas s' ekial oègins ladd s' Skotdis lìdion.
:::
The old-style link between language and 'eke' lies at the heart of Pictish studies. To earlier scholars, identifying the linguistic affinities of the Picts [seemed] to [guarantee] an understanding of their ethnic affinities, and thus to establish the ethnic origins of the Scottish nation.

Y ren wíginlúng dí apperekiadè edlúkity issa aeon kuldìelat-konsterukdèd kadègory, raedd s' lóst issa raedd s' perehànt, id dí akklùwlàdge dat geludiks lued raen zattlà vaj lùding dí wi and iz, adt iz siud wí wùozas dí wény s' konlukdion wítyen lánkage id oder aslòkts ladd kuldìé. Ked s' kurént geluedion ladd skslolárs idda dénglàd sud raedd kaddeats id kazawúkadions wen déljíng aweut edlúkity, perejeous geluedions raen lùt wult sò wutdèéd. Keldik sdìwèes raen wíen lórdikulárat perolu dí kadazaer sdédèlénts aweut lánkage, bzáod, id ekial 'lòrsòlìzaty', wiksl ley lùw kauhà nizze dí láugsl vaj annke…
:::
We are beginning to appreciate ethnicity as a culturally-constructed category, in the past as in the present, and to acknowledge that genetics have had little or nothing to do with it, but it should be [foolish] to deny the connection between language and other aspects of culture. While the current [tradition] of scholars is [tangled] up in caveats and [qualifications] when talking about ethnicity, previous traditions have not felt so [fettered]. Celtic studies have been particularly prone to categorical statements about language, blood, and ethnic 'personality', which may now cause us to laugh or [wink]...

Ladd:
:::
From:

S' old-stylà bank gwítgadn lánslóaslà id 'eslí ' baes raz s'freart ladd Pikdis sdìwèes. Dí earbaer skslolárs, iwéndifarng s' banslóisdik afwúlúdies ladd s' Pikts sòeléd dí slóaendèe aeon unwérsdénwèng ladd wéir ejíal afwúlúdies, id wís dí esdébbas s'ejíal oèslens ladd s' Sjìtdis lìdion.

The old-style link between language and 'race' lies at the heart of Pictish studies. To earlier scholars, identifying the linguistic affinities of the Picts seemed to guarantee an understanding of their ethnic affinities, and thus to establish the ethnic origins of the Scottish nation.

Y ren gwíslenlúng dí aplòéjíadè edlújíty issa aeon yldìelat-jìnsdèsúkdèd gwèdèslory, raedd s'lóst issa raedd s'lòésònt, id dí akklùwlàdslà yt slàludiks lued raen battlà vaj lùwèng dí an sem iz, gadt iz siud gwí wùobas dí wény s' jìnlukdion gwítgadn lánslóaslà id owér aslòkts ladd yldìé. Ked s'yrént fràluedion ladd skslolárs idda dénglàd sud raedd gwèddeats id uabawúgwèdions yn déljíng agwet edlújíty, lòéjeous slàluedions raen lùt flt sò ftdèéd. Slíldik sdìwèes raen gwíen lórdiylárat lòdelu dí gwèdabaer sdédèlénts agwet lánslóaslà, bzáod, id ejíal 'lòrsòlìbaty', anksl ley lùw gwèusò nizze dí láugsl vaj annslí …

We are beginning to appreciate ethnicity as a culturally-constructed category, in the past as in the present, and to acknowledge that genetics have had little or nothing to do with it, but it should be [foolish] to deny the connection between language and other aspects of culture. While the current [tradition] of scholars is [tangled] up in caveats and [qualifications] when talking about ethnicity, previous traditions have not felt so [fettered]. Celtic studies have been particularly prone to categorical statements about language, blood, and ethnic 'personality', which may now cause us to laugh or [wink]...

--- START OF TEXT 95 (A Grammar of Affel, a vampirical language of Southeastern Europe) ---

Teransyldalúa, and raeddiz wéep dallàys dersuunwéd ifeshi aeon kosulut ladd lòaks, raedd iz anwé wisóns and sligsllánds, malu wùésts id s' Almalu léawiws raz s' wuet ladd immásóng glákiers; and raedd iz hált lèlus aléady nrjid raedd pereslisdíry, and raedd iz gold gadeéd sónke Luozadik dilés ferom ddeins raedd raedd iz sukks id ferom s' ydèrs ladd raedd iz stereams; and raedd iz éferesing, akiwèk, anlu-zaji, lìdìelat karwelìdèd sperings, Teransyldalúa, aeon slell aéa raedd s' láp ladd s' Easdèrn id Sòudern Karlódians, aeon kountery fudd s' easdèrnlist edge ladd Kenteral Eusulò.
:::
Transylvania, with in its deep valleys surrounded by a [coronet] of [peaks], in its wide [basins] with highlands, pine forests with the Almalu [meadows] at the foot of imposing glaciers; with in it heart [malt] already [worked] in prehistory, and in its gold gathered since Luozadik times from veins in its rocks with from the waters of its streams; with in its refreshing, [acidic], [done-like], naturally [carbonated] springs, Transylvania, a small area in the gap of the Eastern with Southern Carpathians, a country on the easternmost edge of Central Europe.

Edden dougsl iz ys apperoaksled earat ifeshi Easdèrn Ordowiksy elelìding ferom Bybandium, raedd iz Kslèsdialúty idda wisókalat ysdèrn. Ilúdialat s' Sulen èdìal ys perewilèlìnt wét ládèr iz wíkalé s' wisdion ladd Eusulòan Perodèsdéndism.
:::
Even though it is approached early by Eastern Orthodoxy leading from Byzantium, its Christianity is basically western. Initially the Roman ritual was prevalent but later it became the [bastion] of European Protestantism.

--- END OF TEXT 95 ---
--- START OF TEXT 96 (S' Gwara lánslóaslà ladd Nigeria /// The Gwara language of Nigeria) ---

Fsum s' lomwír id sóze ladd hàttlàlénts, iz idda zajiat dat deqé ren làss dan 3000 slòajirs and sòlé klùwlàdge ladd Gyqe. Kslilderen sdill raen raz làast aeon lóssódde klùwlàdge ladd s' Gyqe lánslóaslà, wét iz idda wéwúlúdèat dhéadèlud id akdidde klùwlàdge idda wèháplòaqèng. Tsleshé ren perowibat onat aeon wuw slundered teruat komlòdènt slòajirs, aldougsl indèrddendions ifeshi sksloolkslilderen wíshèng s' ezakidédion hàssóons hóggesdèd dat s' jeews ladd awílts ley wí dío lòssólèsdik. Hauhá idda s' wilèlìnt lánslóaslà ladd indèrkomlílúkadion id idda ofdèn speread ifeshi awílts wo raen nrjid outsówé raedd s' sódies.
:::
From the number and size of settlements, it is judged that there are less than 3000 speakers with some knowledge of Gwara. Children still have at least a passive knowledge of the Gwara language, but it is definitely threatened and active knowledge is disappearing. There are probably only a few hundred true competent speakers, although interviews by schoolchildren visiting the examination sessions suggested that the views of adults may be too pessimistic. Hausa is the dominant language of intercommunication and is often spread by adults who have worked outside in the cities.

Lánslóaslà elley smájin ifeshi leny olwér lòoplà inkzàwé Iwí, Ase id Hshim. Nearby lánguages inkzàwé Grung id Hshim, wét iz aplòars dehà ren lùt anwéat smájin ifeshi Gyqe. Iwí raedd lórdikulár ley wí aeon immárdént sòurke ladd záannrds raedd Gyqe.
:::
Language also spoken by many other people include Iwi, Ase and Hshim. Nearby languages include Grung and Hshim, but it appears they are not widely spoken by Gwara. Iwi in particular may be an important source of loanwords in Gwara.

--- END OF TEXT 96 ---
--- START OF TEXT 97 (Yeddim ruenza's effa yaddim shaiddeths effa Thkelemsinh) ---

Y raen wéjíwéd dí ilúdiadè aeon andeugsl alìatsós ladd Yeddim ruenza's effa yaddim shaiddeths effa Thkelemsinh. Y sid sdért másding s'wèffént délàs fdem wès nnwérslal id mysdèèous gruk, yètdèn raedd affbaan lánslóaslà .
:::
We have decided to initiate a thorough analysis of Yeddim ruenza's effa yaddim shaiddeths effa Thkelemsinh. We will start posting the different tales from this wonderful and mysterious book, written in affbaan language.

Ked ruu fel ud gwèn lòdejewé dèensládions ladd iz, gwí iz inwèjewíal nrds vaj lást jìmplàdè lóssùslàs, plàasò an!
:::
If you feel you can provide translations of it, whether it be individual words or just complete passages, please do!

Iz idda aeon 150 lóslàs gruk sem ilzàsdèedions raedd PDF wùrlet.
:::
It is a 150-page book with illustrations in PDF format.

--- END OF TEXT 97 ---
--- START OF TEXT 98 () ---
